  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts three employment arrangements: (1) Client employed as HGV driver through agency with intended retirement date 2027-10-14 (inferred from 'expect to retire within the next two years' as at 14/10/2025); (2) Volunteer role with Sea Cadets earning £20 per week; (3) Kim (spouse) intends to continue working until age 66, with intended retirement date 2026-10-14 (inferred from age 65 at 14/10/2025).

    File:     Fact Find [P5] and [P13] record: Employment Status 'Employed', Occupation 'HGV Driver', Employer 'CDH Recruitment' (not 'agency'). Suitability Report [P15] states 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver' and 'You receive £20 per week helping out the sea cadets on a weekend'. Planning cashflow [P3] shows Employment income of £16,640 for one party and £13,200 for Kim. Zurich policy illustration [P20] shows 'Retirement date 7 June 2031 (age 75)' - this is a provider illustration, not a client intention. No document evidences Kim's intended retirement age as 66 or retirement date as 2026-10-14. No document states client expects to retire 'within the next two years' from 14/10/2025 (i.e., by 14/10/2027).

    Discrepancies:

      - Employer stated in report as 'agency' but Fact Find [P5] records employer as 'CDH Recruitment' (a specific named agency/recruitment company)

      - Retirement date for client inferred as 2027-10-14 but no supporting document quoted states expectation to retire by that date; Suitability Report [P6] states objective 'You want to retire in 2 years time' (from 14/10/2025) which would be 14/10/2027, but this is an objective/goal, not an employment fact

      - Retirement date for Kim (spouse) inferred as 2026-10-14 based on age 66, but no document provides Kim's age at the report date or confirms 2026 as intended retirement date

      - Zurich policy illustration [P20] references 'Retirement date 7 June 2031 (age 75)' which differs materially from the 2027-10-14 inferred client retirement date, though this is a provider illustration not a client fact

    The employment status and primary occupation (HGV driver) are corroborated across multiple documents. The £20 per week volunteer income is explicitly confirmed in [P15]. However, discrepancies exist: (1) The employer is named as 'CDH Recruitment' in the Fact Find, not simply 'agency'. This is a minor clarification rather than a material contradiction — CDH Recruitment is an agency. (2) The client's intended retirement date of 2027-10-14 is inferred by extraction from the stated objective to 'retire in 2 years time' from the report date of 14/10/2025, documented in [P6]. This is a legitimate inference from a stated goal, though the extraction notes it as 'Inferred' rather than directly stated. (3) Kim's intended retirement age (66) and derived retirement date (2026-10-14) are not explicitly supported by any passage in the case file. No document provides Kim's current age, stated retirement intention, or target retirement date. (4) The Zurich retirement date of 7 June 2031 (age 75) in [P20] is a provider illustration assumption and not a statement of client intention. The guard notes that intended retirement age in a fact find is superseded by later file notes — the most recent document is the Suitability Report itself. The report's statements are internally consistent with its own sources (the objective to retire in 2 years is stated in [P6]), but the inference of Kim's retirement details lacks supporting evidence. Employment income figures reconcile across documents (£300/week HGV = £15,600 annual gross; £20/week volunteer = £1,040 annual). The core employment facts (status, primary occupation, volunteer role and amount) are supported, but Kim's retirement particulars are unsupported by the evidence pack.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Employment income: £300/week net (HGV driver) + £20/week net (Sea Cadets volunteer). Kim: £1,100/month net. State Pension: £230/week net (current). Kim State Pension: £230/week net (future from 2026). Household expenditure: £1,700/month. Monthly equivalents calculated: HGV £1,300/month, Sea Cadets £86.67/month, State Pension £1,000/month. Total monthly income net: £4,486.67. Monthly surplus: £2,786.67. Assets: £6,000. Liabilities: £0.

    File:     Fact Find [P1] [P10]: Total Monthly Income shown as £1,430.00 (not itemised in detail but shows State Pension £230.00/month and Basic Income £1,200.00/month in one table). Expenditure calculated total: £1,718.00/month. Monthly Disposable Income: -£288.00. Suitability Report [P17]: States HGV income "typically earning around £300 per week net". States Sea Cadets "£20 per week". States Kim "approximately £1,100 per month net". States State Pension "£230 per week". States household expenditure "approximately £1,700 per month". Planning scenarios [P11]: Total Assets £122,997 comprising Liquid Assets £6,000, Pensions £116,997. Income and Expenditure chart [P9]: Shows employment income, state pension, and total expenses with no shortfall projected.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report derives HGV monthly equivalent as £1,300/month (£300 × 52 ÷ 12), but Fact Find [P10] shows basic employment income of £1,200/month - a 7.7% variance. Guard permits up to 5% tolerance.

      - Report derives State Pension monthly equivalent as £1,000/month (£230 × 52 ÷ 12), but no matching figure in Fact Find; Fact Find [P1] shows State Pension £230.00/month, implying £11,960 annualised (not £11,960 weekly converted).

      - Report shows Sea Cadets derived monthly equivalent £86.67 (£20 × 52 ÷ 12), Fact Find [P1] does not itemise this separately; closest match is 87 in tables, 0.4% variance.

      - Report total monthly income £4,486.67 (1300+86.67+1000+1100) does not match Fact Find [P1] total of £1,430.00 - a 214% discrepancy. This suggests either the Fact Find has incomplete income data or the report is using different income sources.

      - Report states expenditure £1,700/month; Fact Find [P1] shows calculated household expenditure of £1,718.00/month - a 1% variance, within tolerance but not exact.

      - Fact Find [P1] shows Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00 (income £1,430 less expenditure £1,718), indicating a deficit, whereas report derives a surplus of £2,786.67. This is contradictory: either income or expenditure figures differ materially between documents.

    The core issue is a material conflict in total monthly income between the Fact Find and the Suitability Report. The Fact Find records total net monthly income as £1,430.00, yielding a monthly deficit of -£288.00 when set against expenditure of £1,718.00. The Suitability Report records total net monthly income as £4,486.67 (itemised as C1 HGV £1,300 + C1 Sea Cadets £86.67 + C1 State Pension £1,000 + C2 Employment £1,100), yielding a surplus of £2,786.67. These cannot both be correct. The individual components show further discrepancies: (1) HGV income: report uses £1,300/month derived from stated £300/week; Fact Find [P10] shows £1,200/month basic income, exceeding the 5% tolerance guard (7.7% variance). (2) State Pension: report derives £1,000/month from £230/week; Fact Find [P1] shows State Pension £230.00/month, suggesting a different basis (monthly not weekly). (3) Sea Cadets: report derives £86.67/month from £20/week; Fact Find does not separately itemise this. (4) Expenditure: report £1,700 matches evidence to within 1% (Fact Find shows £1,718), within tolerance. The discrepancies exceed rounding and presentation differences. The Fact Find shows income of £1,430/month net; the report shows £4,486.67. The guard on weekly/monthly conversion (52 not 4.33×12) has been correctly applied by the report but does not reconcile with the Fact Find's figure for basic employment income. The report asserts income figures that, when summed, produce a monthly total not supported by the Fact Find, and this drives the derivation of a substantial surplus (£2,786.67) where the Fact Find records a deficit (-£288.00). This is material to the suitability assessment: a surplus supports retirement planning differently from a deficit. The discrepancy is not explained by any guard and represents a fundamental mismatch in the financial position on which the recommendation rests.

    File:     [P5] Fact Find shows 'Savings' of £6,000.00 in Cash/GBP as at 22/09/2025. [P4] intelliflo Baseline scenario shows 'Savings' of £6,000 owned by JS with opening value in 2025. [P8] intelliflo Proposed scenario shows total assets of £122,997 comprising Liquid Assets (£6,000 Savings), Pensions (£116,997), with no property equity, businesses or possessions. [P4] Liabilities table shows 'Total' of £0. [P15] Baseline scenario Liabilities table shows no entries, implying zero. No liabilities are documented in any passage.

    Discrepancies:

      - Valuation date mismatch: report states 2025-10-14 but fact find [P5] shows 22/09/2025, a 22-day difference

      - Ownership mismatch: report asserts savings owned by both C1 and C2 (joint), but [P4] and [P8] show savings owned by JS only, not joint

    The savings value of £6,000 is consistently corroborated across multiple documents: fact find [P5], baseline scenario [P4], and proposed scenario [P8]. However, two material discrepancies exist. First, the valuation date differs between the report (2025-10-14) and the fact find (22/09/2025); this is a 22-day gap. The guard permits comparison of as-at dates when they differ, but a gap of this magnitude may reflect different valuation points rather than trivial rounding. Second, the report asserts the savings are owned jointly by C1 and C2, but the evidence consistently shows JS (John) as the sole owner in [P4] and [P8]. The fact find [P5] does not show ownership explicitly in the quoted cell but the planning documents unambiguously attribute the £6,000 to JS alone. Liabilities are confirmed absent and reconcile: [P4] shows a Liabilities table with Total of £0, and no other liabilities are documented anywhere. The ownership discrepancy is material because it affects the characterization of the asset position for both clients and bears on their combined capacity for loss and household financial resilience, which are asserted in the report to support the recommendation.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts that the client (JS, age 69) is in receipt of full State Pension of £230 per week. The report also asserts that Kim (KS, age 65) will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age. The report states Kim's State Pension age is 67 and payable from 2027-10-14. The canonical model records the client's state pension as already in payment at £230/week (full entitlement), and Kim's as not yet in payment, also £230/week (full entitlement), with state pension age 67 and payable from 2027-10-14.

    File:     Passage [P9] confirms: 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week, and Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age.' Passage [P5] states: 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' and 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.' Converting £230/week to annual: £230 × 52 = £11,960, which reconciles to £11,973 within rounding tolerance. Passage [P1] indicates State Pension Age for one party as 66, with retirement planned at age 66 in 2026. The planning documents [P1] show 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' with retirement ages of 66 (2026) and 71 (2027), but these appear to relate to different household members. Passage [P5] explicitly confirms Kim's state pension age as 67: 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.'

    Discrepancies:

      - Canonical model states Kim's state pension payable from date 2027-10-14 (October 2027), but Kim is currently age 65 and if state pension age is 67, the date should align with when she turns 67, which would be approximately 2029 based on a 1960 birth date shown in [P1]

      - Canonical model lists Kim's state pension age as 67, but passage [P1] shows 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' for one party in the planning baseline scenario, creating ambiguity about whether the correct age is 66 or 67

      - The suitability report states 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully' [P9], but the state pension age extracted into the canonical model is 67, not 66, creating a discrepancy between stated retirement age and state pension age

    The weekly state pension amounts of £230 for both clients are clearly evidenced and corroborated in [P9] and [P5]. The annual equivalent of £230 × 52 = £11,960 reconciles to the £11,973 annual figure stated in [P5], within acceptable rounding tolerance (the difference of £13 is 0.1%). The report correctly states the client is in receipt of full state pension. However, there is a material inconsistency regarding Kim's state pension age. The planning document [P1] shows 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' while passage [P5] and the suitability report [P9] state she will receive state pension 'from age 67.' Additionally, the suitability report explicitly states 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully' [P9], but the canonical extraction records state pension age as 67. The payable-from date of 2027-10-14 in the canonical model also appears misaligned: if Kim is currently 65 and reaches age 67, that would be approximately 2029, not October 2027. The report narrative and the canonical model extract contain internal inconsistency on whether Kim's retirement/state pension age is 66 or 67. The guard notes that 'State pension age and intended retirement age are routinely conflated in reports,' which appears to have occurred here. The evidence shows both ages mentioned: 66 as retirement/planned work cessation age, and 67 as formal state pension age.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P9)

        "You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week, and Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P5)

        "You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum. Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P9)

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts five income streams: (1) £300 per week net (HGV driver employment); (2) £20 per week net (Sea Cadets volunteer); (3) £1,100 per month net (Kim's employment); (4) £230 per week net (client's State Pension); (5) £230 per week net (Kim's future State Pension). These are converted to monthly equivalents: £1,300, £86.67, £1,100, £1,000, £1,000 respectively. Total monthly income net stated as £4,486.67. Single expenditure figure: £1,700 monthly net.

    File:     Fact Find [P2] shows: Basic Income (Employed, CDH Recruitment) Monthly £1,200.00 net; State Pension Monthly £230.00 net. Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00. Expenditure breakdown [P2] itemises monthly expenses totalling £1,718.00. Retirement goal [P5] and [P12] state need for £1,700 net per month income in retirement.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report income item 1 (HGV driver £300/week net = £1,300/month) but Fact Find [P2] shows £1,200.00 net monthly for employed income; 8.3% variance

      - Report income item 2 (Sea Cadets £20/week net) but Fact Find [P2] shows Building Insurance £25.00 monthly and no separate volunteer income line; £20/week would be £86.67/month but Fact Find does not isolate this

      - Report income item 3 (Kim £1,100/month net) but Fact Find [P2] does not list a separate Kim employment income; only aggregated household income shown

      - Report income item 4 (client State Pension £230/week = £1,000/month) but derived conversion uses 52 weeks annually (£230 × 52 / 12 = £1,000); Fact Find [P2] shows £230 monthly for State Pension, not weekly

      - Report income item 5 (Kim State Pension £230/week future = £1,000/month) but this is prospective income not yet in effect; Fact Find does not list this separately as it is not current income

      - Report total monthly income £4,486.67 but Fact Find [P2] states Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; difference of £3,056.67 or 214% variance—fundamental mismatch

      - Report expenditure £1,700/month but Fact Find [P2] shows Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure £1,718.00; £18 or 1.1% variance within tolerance

      - Report derives £86.67 from £20/week volunteer income but Fact Find [P2] does not separately identify Sea Cadets role; only shows aggregate income and itemised expenses

    The report and Fact Find are irreconcilable on core income figures. The Fact Find [P2] records total monthly net household income as £1,430.00, comprised of Basic Income (employed) £1,200.00 and State Pension £230.00. The report asserts five distinct income sources totalling £4,486.67 monthly net. This is not a rounding or frequency conversion issue: the report's stated sources and amounts do not exist in the source documents in the form presented. Specifically: (1) The HGV driver income is stated in Fact Find as £1,200 net monthly, not £300 per week (£1,300 monthly). The report's quote 'typically earning around £300 per week net' [P2] appears on page 3 of the suitability report but the Fact Find [P2] records the employment income as £1,200 monthly gross basic. This is a material discrepancy not explained by any guard. (2) The Sea Cadets volunteer income of £20 per week has no match in the Fact Find. The only adjacent figure is Building Insurance at £25/month in the expenditure section [P2], which is not income. (3) Kim's employment income of £1,100/month is not separately listed in the Fact Find [P2]; the document shows only one income line for 'Basic Income' at £1,200 net monthly attributed to 'Employed, CDH Recruitment', which appears to be the client's own income, not Kim's. (4) The derivation of £1,000/month from State Pension by converting £230/week is arithmetically correct (£230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,000), but the Fact Find [P2] already records State Pension as £230 per month, not per week. This suggests the report has either double-converted or misread the frequency. (5) Kim's future State Pension of £230/week is a prospective income not yet being received and appropriately absent from current income in the Fact Find. The fundamental issue is that the report's total of £4,486.67 monthly net income is unsupported by the Fact Find's £1,430.00 total. The expenditure reconciliation (£1,700 vs £1,718) is within tolerance and the guard on rounding explains the £18 difference. However, the income totals diverge by £3,056.67, which is 214% of the Fact Find total—a material error that cannot be explained by frequency conversion, rounding, or basis differences. The report appears to have assembled income items that do not match the source documents either in value, frequency interpretation, or attribution (e.g., Kim's income is not separately evidenced).

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts a retirement target date of 2027-10-14 (14 October 2027) with a termYears of 2 years, an intended retirement age of 66, and timeHorizon values of LongTerm, MediumTerm, and MediumTerm. The report does not explicitly state a targetAge for the retirement objective.

    File:     The evidence shows multiple retirement dates and horizons: (1) [P10] intelliflo planning proposed scenario states 'Retire (John)' at age 71 in 2027 and 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026. The timeline shows John retiring in 2027 when he will be 71 years old (born 07/06/1956). Kim retires in 2026 when she will be 66 years old (born 07/05/1960). (2) [P11] Risk profile report states 'Investment Period 2 years' and references an investment of £106,000. (3) [P15] Zurich Policy Info states 'Selected Retirement Age was chosen and was fixed at the start of the plan' at age 65 (Planned Retirement Age for that plan is 75). (4) [P1] Pension switch report uses retirement age 75 for some projections across all arrangements. (5) [P24] Suitability Report states life expectancy analysis for 'a 69-year-old male is age 86' and 'a 65-year-old female is age 88'.

      - Zurich plan in [P15] shows Selected Retirement Age 65 with Planned Retirement Age 75, creating ambiguity about which contractual retirement age applies to which arrangement

    The core discrepancy is between the report's stated intended retirement age of 66 for John and the evidence in [P10] which clearly shows John retiring at age 71 in 2027. The multiple timeHorizon values (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) are not clearly mapped to distinct objectives in the extracted data, making it unclear whether they represent a single retirement goal or multiple goals with different horizons. The evidence does support a 2-year medium-term investment horizon for the pension consolidation/switch, but the retirement age stated in the report contradicts the planning evidence. The pension switch analysis in [P1] projecting to age 75 is the switch provider's standard illustration age, not the client's intended retirement date. The comparison can be performed because the key figures (2027 date, 2-year term, retirement ages) appear across multiple documents; the discrepancy is not one of data absence but of conflicting values.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts four foreseeable changes: (1) Client retirement within two years (expected 2027-10-14), with loss of £1,386.67/month net income from employment and Sea Cadets roles; (2) Kim's retirement at age 66 (expected 2026-03-26), with loss of £1,100/month employment income; (3) Kim's State Pension commencement at age 66 (2026-03-26), adding £1,000/month net income; (4) Inheritance Tax regime change for pensions from 6 April 2027, making unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits part of the taxable estate.

    File:     Evidence supports the retirement dates and income impacts as follows: [P8] shows timeline events 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026 and 'Retire (John)' age 71 in 2027. [P6] states 'You expect to retire within the next two years' and 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully'. [P6] confirms 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age'. [P10] references 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67' (note: age 67, not 66). [P6] quotes the IHT change: 'From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits (such as drawdown funds and uncrystallised pots) will be included in your taxable estate for IHT'. Employment income is detailed in [P6]: client 'typically earning around £300 per week net' with 'additional income of £20 per week from your volunteer role with the Sea Cadets' and Kim 'earns approximately £1,100 per month net'. [P15] shows first-year employment income of £16,640 (client) plus £13,200 (Kim) totalling £29,840 annually.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Kim's State Pension commencement at age 66 in 2026 (expectedOn: 2026-03-26) with £1,000/month income; evidence [P10] states Kim receives state pension from 'age 67' not age 66, creating a one-year discrepancy in the State Pension age assumption.

      - Report asserts client retirement date as 2027-10-14 (age 71); evidence [P8] shows 'Retire (John)' at age 71 in 2027 which is consistent, but the specific October date is not evidenced in retrieved passages.

      - Report financial impact for client retirement shows loss of £1,386.67/month net (£1,300 employment + £86.67 Sea Cadets); evidence shows £300/week HGV income (£1,300/month) and £20/week Sea Cadets income (£86.67/month), mathematically consistent. However, [P6] qualifies HGV income as 'typically earning around £300 per week net', introducing variability not reflected in the deterministic financial impact calculation.

    The report's stated foreseeable changes are substantially supported by the evidence. Three of four changes are clearly corroborated: (1) client retirement within two years is explicitly stated in [P6]; (2) Kim's retirement at age 66 in 2026 is evidenced in [P6] and [P8]; (3) the IHT regime change from 6 April 2027 is directly quoted in [P6]. The financial impacts of employment income loss are mathematically consistent with income figures in [P6] and [P15]. However, a material discrepancy exists regarding Kim's State Pension age: the report calculates Kim's State Pension commencement at age 66 (2026-03-26) with an impact of +£1,000/month, but [P10] states Kim will receive state pension 'from age 67'. This creates ambiguity about whether the financial model uses the correct State Pension age for Kim. The report description for the client's retirement cites the financial impact basis from page 3, which correctly identifies the income sources and amounts, but [P6] describes the HGV income as 'typically earning around £300 per week net', introducing conditional language ("typically") not reflected in the deterministic model assumptions. The IHT change is a legislative change affecting all clients, not a client-specific foreseeable change, and per the guard 'Legislative changes affecting everyone are not client-specific foreseeable changes', so its inclusion does not constitute a material finding. The State Pension age discrepancy is material because it affects the cashflow projections and retirement income adequacy assessment.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states three objectives ranked by priority: (1) 'ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime'; (2) 'retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month'; (3) 'clearer understanding of pension arrangements and streamlined approach to managing pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans'. The report identifies a target retirement date of 2027-10-14 (approximately 2 years from the advice date). One objective has a targetDate; the other two have no stated target amounts. The report also references a retirement age of 75 in critical yield calculations and cashflow projections.

    File:     The evidence shows: (P4) Three objectives confirmed: 'ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime', 'retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month', and 'clearer understanding of your pension arrangements...more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans'. (P11) The planning timeline shows retirement events: 'Retire (Kim) 2026 — 70/66' and 'Retire (John) 2027 — 71/67'. The client (JS, aged 69) would retire in 2026 at age 70, not 2027. The spouse (KS, aged 65) would retire in 2027 at age 71. (P9, P5) Cashflow projections run to 2027 and beyond, showing 'Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions' maintained above £122,950 throughout, with 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' remaining at zero or positive (surplus spent per (P8) assumption: '0% of any in-year surplus will be saved'). The retirement household income target of £1,700 net per month (£20,400 annually) is evidenced in the baseline cashflow: (P16) shows first-year expenditure of £24,001 excluding tax/NI additions. (P8) states 'The retirement age used is 75' for the critical yield calculation, not the actual retirement date of 2026/2027. The foreseeable life changes are documented: (P11) shows 'Illness (John) age 100 in 2056', 'Illness (Kim) age 100 in 2060', 'Death (John) age 100 in 2056', 'Death (Kim) age 100 in 2060'.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report cites a retirement target of 2027-10-14 (approximately October 2027, when the client would be 71). The planning timeline (P11) specifies retirement for the primary client JS at 2026 (age 70), not 2027. This is a one-year misalignment in the stated retirement date.

      - The report uses age 75 as the 'retirement age' for critical yield and investment projections (P8: 'The retirement age used is 75'), but the clients' stated retirement dates are 2026 (age 70) and 2027 (age 71). Age 75 is not a retirement age in the case file; it appears to be used as a target age for pension fund maturity evaluation, not the actual retirement date.

      - The report asserts a household income target of £1,700 net per month, but does not explicitly state whether this is each client individually, combined, or in what sequence (e.g., before and after each spouse's retirement). The cashflow evidence (P9) shows total money in 2026 of £40,703 and in 2027 of £24,181, but does not isolate the £1,700/month (£20,400 annually) as a separate stated requirement.

      - The report does not state a target amount for objective 1 ('ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime') or objective 3 ('clearer understanding...streamlined approach'). These are present as objectives but unquantified.

    The objectives themselves are evidenced and properly prioritized. However, there is a material inconsistency in the retirement timeline. The report's extracted targetDate of 2027-10-14 does not align with the planning timeline evidence (P11), which shows two distinct retirement dates: JS retires in 2026 at age 70, and KS retires in 2027 at age 71. The report appears to have captured only the later spouse's retirement date. This is not merely a data entry variance; it affects the suitability assessment because the investment term, critical yield, and access date are all contingent on when capital becomes needed. The report's use of age 75 for the critical yield calculation is a separate issue: this appears to be a generic longevity assumption for fund maturity, not the retirement date itself. The £1,700 net monthly household income target is supported by the cashflow modelling (P5, P9) which projects continued affordability, though the evidence does not explicitly restate the £1,700 figure as a constraint or requirement. No vulnerability factors identified in the evidence materially affect the consistency of this check. The fact-find (P21) records client declaration that 'financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document,' confirming the objectives as stated in the report, though it does not resolve the retirement date misalignment.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report links six recommendations to objectives: Recommendations 1-2 to OBJ3 (pension consolidation); Recommendations 3-5 to OBJ2 and OBJ3 (fund switching and consolidation); Recommendation 6 to OBJ1 and OBJ2. The recommended plan is an Aviva Pension Portfolio with £110,185 transfer value, selected retirement age 71, with access via Flexi-Access Drawdown (FAD) from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028). Projections are provided at age 75 under low (0%), mid (2.95%), and high (5.88%) growth rates. The report states the client needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement and expects annual expenditure of approximately £1,700. The cashflow forecast runs to age 100 with surplus income spent rather than saved. The client receives £300 per week as an HGV driver and state pension of £11,973 per annum. The report asserts the recommended solution is 'most suitable given your circumstances and retirement objectives' and provides 'flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you decide to draw benefits.'

    File:     Evidence confirms a retirement income need of £1,700 net per month [P1]. The Aviva Pension Portfolio product literature confirms access at age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028) via Flexi-Access Drawdown, with up to 25% tax-free lump sum and remainder subject to income tax at marginal rate [P2, P3, P6, P7]. The fact find records total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 [P24] and notes 'No' to expected changes in expenditure in the foreseeable future [P24]. Income is stated as £300 per week net (£1,430 monthly) [P24] and £300 per week typical [P8]. State pension assumptions are £11,973 per annum [P10]. The cashflow model in the proposed scenario incorporates these figures and projects to age 100 with surplus income spent [P12]. Projections at age 75 under three growth rates are evidenced: low 0% yielding £97,600, mid 2.95% yielding £115,000, high 5.88% yielding £135,000 [P14, P15]. However, the objectives themselves (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) are not extracted or evidenced in the passages provided. The fact find does not record the stated objectives or their priority. No passage defines what OBJ3 (pension consolidation objective) entails beyond the recommendations themselves, nor OBJ1 or OBJ2. The recommendation to retire at age 71 is stated in the canonical model but no evidence explains why age 71 was selected or how it aligns with stated goals. The critical yield of 4.8% required by the new solution at mid growth is stated [P9, P14, P15], but no analysis links this requirement to the client's ability to achieve the stated retirement income need or whether 4.8% is achievable given the client's circumstances.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report names three objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) linked to recommendations but the actual objective statements, priorities, and rationale are not evidenced in the case file passages.

      - The report states retirement age 71 is selected but no evidence explains the basis for selecting age 71 versus other ages or how it serves the stated objectives.

      - The report projects value 'at age 75' but the selected retirement age is stated as 71 in the canonical model; the relevance of age-75 projections to a retirement age of 71 is not explained.

      - The report asserts the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' [P16] but this client preference is not evidenced in the fact find or interview notes provided.

      - The cashflow model assumes all surplus income is spent throughout lifetime [P10, P12] but the fact find shows current monthly disposable income of minus £288 (expenditure exceeds income) [P24], creating a logical inconsistency: if current expenditure exceeds income, how is surplus income generated to spend in the model?

      - The report states income 'typically' £300 per week [P3] but the cashflow deterministically assumes £300 per week without reflecting variability [P14].

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The actual objective statements (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) are not present in the evidence pack and are not defined in the suitability report text provided., No client interview notes or questionnaire documenting the client's stated goals, priorities, or preferred retirement age are in the evidence., No analysis explains why retirement age 71 was selected or how it serves each linked objective., No evidence documents the client's preferences regarding Flexi Access Drawdown or understanding of pension freedoms., No timeframe or target date for achieving objectives is evidenced beyond the retirement age., No analysis of foreseeable life changes or their impact on the plan is provided.

    The check requires evidence that each recommendation is linked to a specific stated objective and that the objective, term, access needs, and strategy are consistent. The report asserts six recommendations linked to three objectives, but the comparison cannot proceed because the objectives themselves are not extracted or evidenced. The extraction log notes 'expectedButAbsent' for multiple paths and 'ambiguities' in risk rating and capacity for loss, indicating material extraction or document completion issues. While access mechanics are evidenced (age 55/57 minimum, FAD available), the justification for retirement age 71 is absent. The fact find shows current household cash flow is negative (minus £288 monthly), yet the report and model assume positive surplus income spent in retirement—a fundamental inconsistency not reconciled in the evidence. The client's stated preference for FAD drawdown is attributed in the report but not sourced to any fact-find question or client statement in the case file. The timeframe linking is weakest for OBJ1 and the priority ranking among objectives is not established. The retirement income need (£1,700 monthly) is confirmed, but no evidence shows how the three growth scenarios (low, mid, high) relate to achieving this need at age 71 versus age 75. The critical yield analysis (4.8% required, 2.95% assumed) is stated but not tied to objective achievement. Under the guard 'an objective linked by boilerplate is not a link', the recommendations in the report state reasons like 'most suitable given your circumstances' and 'provides flexibility', which are generic and do not name specific objectives or explain why each recommendation serves its linked objective uniquely. The access mechanism guard is satisfied (age 55/57 is not restrictive for a 69-year-old), but the term inconsistency (retire at 71, project to 75) is unresolved. No vulnerability overlay is identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G3.2, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.9). Report labels CFL as 'Medium' without stating the scale or classification system on which this rating is based. Report does not explicitly quantify tolerance for loss or model a specific loss scenario. Capacity for loss questionnaire response section shows 'No additional notes were recorded' [P5,P20], yet report asserts capacity for loss assessment. 1 could not be assessed for want of a value the comparison needs (G3.10). 5 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts 'Medium' capacity for loss rating. Assessment basis includes: State Pension £230/week, employment income £300/week net plus £20/week from Sea Cadets, spouse income £1,100/month with future State Pension £230/week, combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of £1,700/month, savings of £6,000 with no liabilities, and confirmation that potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living.

    File:     Evidence corroborates all income figures cited: State Pension £230/week [P11], employment as HGV driver £300/week net [P11], Sea Cadets £20/week [P11], spouse contribution £1,100/month [P11], household expenditure £1,700/month [P11], savings £6,000 [P1]. Financial planning documents [P1,P2,P4,P9,P12] show baseline and proposed scenarios with total liquid assets and pensions of £122,997 and liquid assets of £6,000. No large-loss scenario modelled in the available evidence; stress tests show -30% immediate market crash plus 6% inflation would not require pension funds to be used [P15]. No formal capacity for loss questionnaire notes recorded [P5,P20].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report labels CFL as 'Medium' without stating the scale or classification system on which this rating is based

      - Report does not explicitly quantify tolerance for loss or model a specific loss scenario

      - Capacity for loss questionnaire response section shows 'No additional notes were recorded' [P5,P20], yet report asserts capacity for loss assessment

      - Report restates CFL conclusion ('medium') by reference to strong financial position and income surplus, but does not independently assess capacity for loss separate from attitude to risk description

      - No explicit documentation that CFL was assessed separately from ATR, although canonical model indicates 'isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr: true'

    The report asserts a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating supported by sound financial reasoning: combined household income of approximately £1,520/month net (£230+£300+£20 from client; £1,100 from spouse) against expenditure of £1,700/month, with £6,000 liquid savings and £116,997 in pension assets, no liabilities, and client confirmation that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living [P11]. All underlying financial facts are corroborated by evidence [P1,P4,P11]. However, the assessment presents three issues under the guards. First, the 'Medium' label lacks reference to any documented scale—no classification framework (e.g., Low/Medium/High or numeric) is cited, making the rating's basis unclear. Second, no capacity for loss questionnaire notes are recorded despite the questionnaire section existing in the risk profile [P5], yet the report asserts a capacity conclusion; the guard states 'a CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning is not evidenced merely because the words appear'—here, financial reasoning is provided, but the questionnaire itself is silent. Third, the assessment restates CFL as a consequence of the strong financial position and income surplus rather than through independent loss-tolerance questioning; the guard identifies this as a weak assessment. The report does not quantify tolerance for loss or model a large-loss scenario to substantiate the 'Medium' rating against a stated threshold. The stress test [P15] shows a -30% loss scenario, but this is not explicitly linked to capacity for loss assessment. The canonical model indicates separate assessment (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr: true), but the evidence does not show how CFL was distinguished from ATR in the actual assessment process; the risk profile shows ATR was assessed separately [P19,P20], yet no separate CFL assessment documentation exists apart from the questionnaire section with no notes recorded.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts an investment term of 10 years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears: [10]). The report also identifies three objectives with time horizons: LongTerm, MediumTerm, and MediumTerm.

    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Risk profile report [P2] states 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the justification table for the agreed risk level; (2) Planning scenario [P5] indicates client retirement ages as 2026 (age 70) and 2027 (age 71) for the two household members, which from 2025 represents 1 and 2 years respectively; (3) Planning scenario projections [P6] and [P7] run through 2060, spanning 35 years from 2025, reflecting a long-term planning horizon to advanced ages (104/100); (4) SIPP research report [P9-P10] confirms 'Agreed risk level 5' mapped to 'Balanced' investor profile but does not specify the investment term used for that agreement.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts investment term of 10 years; Risk profile report [P2] explicitly states 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the risk justification section

      - Report identifies three objectives with LongTerm and MediumTerm horizons, but Planning scenario [P5] shows retirement imminent (1-2 years) rather than supporting a 10-year investment term

      - The 10-year horizon is not reconciled with the 2-year investment period stated in the risk assessment documentation or with the retirement dates shown in the planning scenario

    A material discrepancy exists between the investment term asserted in the canonical model (10 years) and the Investment Period documented in the risk profile justification (2 years, [P2]). The Risk Profile Report is a category D document (risk profile report) and contains the risk level justification, which explicitly states the investment period used to support the agreed risk level as 2 years. This is the period over which projections were modelled to reach the agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced). The planning scenario [P5] further evidences that retirement is planned for 2026 and 2027, representing 1-2 years from the 2025 report date, not 10 years. The guard permits money left invested through retirement to have a longer horizon than the retirement date itself, but the report must state this. No such statement is evident in the risk profile documentation. The canonical objectives include LongTerm and MediumTerm categories, but these are not explicitly linked to the investment term of 10 years or reconciled with the 2-year Investment Period used to justify the risk level. The discrepancy is not explained by the risk justification narrative. The 10-year figure appears inconsistent with the documented 2-year investment period that was used to model the agreed risk level and the imminent retirement dates in the financial plan.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts a target risk rating of 5 and recommends two funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 50% allocation with risk rating 5 and 0.18% ongoing charge, and CT Universal MAP Balanced C at 50% allocation with risk rating 5 and 0.29% ongoing charge. The strategy is described as a Model Portfolio.

    File:     Evidence corroborates the agreed risk level as 5 ('Balanced') per [P10] and [P22]. The SIPP research report [P8] confirms both recommended funds at 50% each: 'CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (GBP)' at 50% and 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (GBP)' at 50%. The suitability report [P11] lists identical allocations and risk ratings of 5 for both funds. Fund factsheets [P12] confirm HSBC's fund objective states risk profile is rated as 3 on HSBC's own scale where 1 is lower and 5 is higher. The KIID [P2] identifies CT Universal MAP as category 4 with 'medium fluctuations historically' and asset allocation of 45-70% equities, 20-55% bonds, 0-25% other. Performance data [P16] and [P17] confirm combined portfolio performance and individual fund performances. Ongoing charges in [P8] show HSBC at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29%.

    Discrepancies:

      - Fund-level risk ratings: HSBC factsheet [P12] states the fund is 'rated as 3' on HSBC's own OpenFunds scale (1-5 lower to higher risk), not risk rating 5. The report asserts both funds carry risk rating 5, but HSBC's own scale positions this fund at 3.

      - Risk scale identification: The report does not explicitly state which risk scale is being used for the fund-level risk ratings (5) versus the client agreed rating (5). HSBC uses its own 1-5 scale where this fund is rated 3; the client agreed rating appears to be on a different scale (also 1-5 but with different mapping).

      - Asset allocation by asset class: The report asserts a Model Portfolio strategy but provides no asset class breakdown (equities %, bonds %, cash %). The canonical model shows this data absent from extraction. Evidence in [P2] and fund factsheets indicates underlying asset allocation (e.g., 45-70% equities, 20-55% bonds for CT Universal MAP) but no consolidated target allocation for the combined portfolio is stated in the report.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Asset class allocation breakdown for the recommended Model Portfolio (equities %, bonds %, cash % etc.) - the report asserts a Model Portfolio strategy but does not specify target asset allocation, Clarification of the risk scale used for fund-level risk ratings (the report states both funds are risk rating 5, but HSBC's own fund documentation indicates that fund is rated 3 on HSBC's internal 1-5 scale, creating ambiguity about whether ratings are comparable)

    The comparison cannot be fully performed because critical information is absent. The report asserts two specific fund holdings at 50% each with risk rating 5 for both funds, and allocations total 100% as required. However, a material discrepancy exists regarding fund-level risk ratings. The HSBC fund factsheet [P12] explicitly states 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk.' This contradicts the report's assertion that HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio carries a risk rating of 5. The guard permits fund-level risk ratings from providers on different scales, but the report does not identify which scale it is using for the fund ratings versus the client's agreed rating.   [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts an override from risk rating 4 (questionnaire result) to risk rating 5 (agreed), justified on the grounds that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite'. Client acknowledgement is documented: the client 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and confirmed not being reliant on pension funds, wishing for best growth potential over 10 years, and feeling a balanced approach would achieve this goal.

    File:     P4 from the Suitability Report states: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' It also records: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.' P2 and P20 (Risk Profile Report and SIPP Research Report) confirm an agreed risk level of 5 based on 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return'. P14 shows the ATR questionnaire responses, which when scored yield a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced). P1 and P3 note 'No additional notes were recorded' for 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections.

    Discrepancies:

      - P11 (File notes) contains contradictory wording: 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6.' This states the agreed rating as both 5 and 6, creating an internal inconsistency within the supporting documentation.

      - P4 states the client is 'aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating' when discussing the increase to 5, which creates ambiguity about whether 4 or 5 was the pre-existing agreed baseline.

    The report's justification for the override is present and is documented in the file. The questionnaire result (risk rating 4, Cautious Balanced) is evidenced in P14. The agreed rating of 5 (Balanced) is consistently stated across P2, P4, P6, P16, and P20. The override direction (4 to 5, increased) is correct. The justification 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' is quoted verbatim in P4 and P11. Client acknowledgement of the increase is documented in P4: the client confirmed wishing to increase to rating 5, confirmed not being reliant on pension funds, and confirmed the 10-year horizon and desire for growth. However, a material discrepancy exists in P11 (file notes), which states the agreed rating as 6 rather than 5 in one place ('You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6'), contradicting the consistent rating of 5 in the Suitability Report (P4) and other supporting documents (P2, P6, P16, P20). This inconsistency in the supporting file notes means the case file itself contains conflicting evidence about what the agreed rating actually was. Additionally, the wording 'aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating' in P4 is ambiguous—it could suggest 4 was previously agreed, or it could be imprecise language. The core justification (questionnaire did not fully reflect appetite) is present and client acknowledgement is documented, but the conflicting record of whether the agreed rating is 5 or 6 in the supporting notes creates uncertainty about the consistency of the override assertion across the case file.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P4)

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts agreed risk rating of 5 ('Balanced') on a 1-10 scale. Capacity for loss rated 'medium'. Investment term 10 years. Recommended portfolio: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). Both funds are stated to reflect the client's risk profile individually and combined, with rationale that 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined. This means that should there be any portfolio drift, the risk being taken remains the same.' Strategy is model portfolio type, 'Aviva Risk 5 Portfolio', mapped to IA Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares benchmark. Projections at low/mid/high growth show £97,600/£115,000/£135,000 at age 75 (10-year term to age 75, real terms). Rationale links to objectives and capacity for loss: medium capacity based on state pension £230/week, employment income £300/week net plus £20/week, spouse income £1,100/month, household expenditure £1,700/month comfortably met, savings £6,000, no liabilities, and client confirmation that underperformance would not detrimental impact day-to-day living.

    File:     Evidence on HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio: KIID states fund is 'rated as 3, where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk' and 'The Fund is rated 4 because historically it has shown a medium level of volatility' in its Risk and Reward Indicator on a 1-7 scale. Fund aims 'to provide growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of 5 years or more.' SIPP research report documents agreed risk level as 5 'Balanced' and shows both HSBC and CT Universal MAP at 50% allocation. Research report states 'The natural risk level, which is ascertained from the psychometric attitude to risk questionnaire score, is used as a starting point' and 'By using these projections and discussions, we came to an agreed risk level for your specific investment objective.' Risk profile report (dated 18 September 2025) confirms natural risk level was 4 'Cautious Balanced' per questionnaire, then agreed to 5. Risk profile document states 'The natural risk level is based on the answers given in the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire which was completed on 18/09/2025.' Capacity for loss detailed in report with state pension £230/week, HGV driver income £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse income £1,100/month, household expenditure £1,700/month, savings £6,000, no liabilities, and confirmation underperformance would not detrimental impact day-to-day living. Investment term: 10 years to age 75 stated. Fund cost disclosure shows HSBC at 0.18% ongoing, CT Universal MAP at 0.29% ongoing, total aggregated 0.24% ongoing (0.37% total with transaction costs). HSBC performance data shows 3-year volatility 7.12%, peer average 6.67%. CT Universal MAP performance 2024: 11.27%, HSBC 10.82%. Both funds in risk-controlled shortlist meeting Defaqto risk score mapping. No evidence that HSBC fund's individual SRRI or internal risk rating of 4 (on 1-7 KIID scale) conflicts with the 'risk rating 5' label used in report, as KIID explicitly states its scale differs from regulatory Risk and Reward profile and is for internal comparison within fund family.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts HSBC fund has 'risk rating 5' in table on page 12, but KIID evidence states fund is 'rated as 3' in internal risk classification and 'rated 4' in Risk and Reward Indicator on 1-7 scale. KIID explicitly notes 'The level of risk stated is to allow investors to compare the risk relative to other Global Strategy Portfolios. This differs from the Risk and reward profile below, which we are required to provide by regulation.' This is a scale mismatch: report uses 'risk rating 5' (likely referring to agreed portfolio risk level on 1-10 adviser scale), KIID uses ratings on fund family's internal 1-5 scale for HSBC Portfolios and regulatory 1-7 scale for Risk/Reward Indicator.

      - Report states investment term as 10 years (to age 75), HSBC KIID recommends minimum holding period of 5 years. Report's 10-year term exceeds fund's recommended minimum, so no mismatch by the guard (longer client term is acceptable).

      - Capacity for loss described as 'medium' in report with enumerated basis (income meets expenditure, savings, no liabilities, underperformance not detrimental). Evidence corroborates this reasoning across income, expenditure, liquidity, and client confirmation. No contradiction found.

      - Agreed risk level changed from questionnaire result of 4 to agreed 5. Risk profile report confirms natural level was 4, then 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and client 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5.' Suitability report states same: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' Both override documents align and client acknowledgement is recorded, satisfying override justification requirement.

    The core issue is a scale labelling mismatch, not a substantive portfolio-to-profile misalignment. The report labels each recommended fund with 'risk rating 5' in the fund recommendation table on page 12. The HSBC KIID shows the fund is 'rated as 3' on HSBC's internal 1-5 fund family scale and 'rated 4' on the regulatory 1-7 Risk and Reward Indicator scale. However, the KIID explicitly states these internal family ratings 'differ from the Risk and reward profile below, which we are required to provide by regulation and which allows investors to compare the risk for different types of funds.' This is a known difference between proprietary fund ratings and regulatory ratings. The 'risk rating 5' in the report appears to refer to the agreed portfolio risk level on the adviser's 1-10 Balanced/Cautious scale (confirmed in Risk Profile Report and Suitability Report narrative), not the fund's own SRRI or KIID rating. The guard states 'A fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's; use it as corroboration, never as a value match.' Applied here: the fund's KIID rating on its proprietary scale does not need to match the portfolio's agreed rating on the adviser's scale. The HSBC fund's KIID Risk/Reward Indicator of 4 (on 1-7 regulatory scale) and internal rating of 3 (on 1-5 family scale) are consistent with a 'Balanced' multi-asset approach suitable for risk rating 5 on a 1-10 adviser scale. Both funds are documented in the research report as part of the risk level 5 'Balanced' shortlist, and the research explicitly states the funds are mapped to achieve the agreed risk level via 'a collection of asset classes designed to expose you to an appropriate level of risk.' Capacity for loss is separately and substantively assessed and evidenced: medium rating justified by adequate income (state pension £230/week, employment £300/week, spouse £1,100/month), controllable expenditure (£1,700/month), liquid savings (£6,000), and client's own confirmation that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living. This satisfies the capacity for loss assessment requirement and is consistent with risk rating 5. Investment term of 10 years to age 75 is documented and does not conflict with either fund's recommended holding period (HSBC recommends 5 years minimum; 10-year term exceeds this, which is acceptable per the guard). The questionnaire produced a natural risk level of 4, which was overridden to 5 by adviser assessment and client agreement. Both the Suitability Report and Risk Profile Report document this override with identical language and client acknowledgement ('You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'). This satisfies the override justification requirement. No vulnerability overlay is noted in the evidence that would materially affect the suitability of this risk/allocation match. All statements relating to risk, capacity for loss, term, and strategy alignment are supported by corroborating evidence in categories D, G, and I.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report states the client's knowledge level is 'Moderate', but the questionnaire in [P1] records 'No understanding / knowledge' in response to three direct questions about understanding stocks and shares, EIS/VCT, and pension planning. The report describes suitability for a 'Balanced' risk investor who 'may have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' [P2, P12, P14], but the questionnaire responses record no understanding of core investment types and no investment experience in those products over the prior 5 years. The client's natural risk level assessed as 'Cautious Balanced' (4) conflicts with the stated 'Moderate' knowledge level, suggesting the report conflates risk tolerance with knowledge. 5 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client's stated knowledge level is 'Moderate'.

    File:     The Risk profile report questionnaire responses [P1] record the client's answers to direct knowledge assessment questions. The client responded 'No understanding / knowledge' to: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?'; 'Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work and do you know the risks associated with them?'; and 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' The client did respond 'Yes, by myself' to 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' and 'Yes, by myself' to 'Do you check the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis?', but responded 'No' to investment in Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning products within the last 5 years [P1]. The Risk profile report notes state 'No additional notes were recorded' for Investor Experience [P3], Capacity for Loss questionnaire [P3], and Agreed risk level sections [P4]. The natural risk level from the Attitude to Risk questionnaire indicated 'Cautious Balanced' (rating 4) [P5, P10], but was overridden to an agreed risk level of 'Balanced' (rating 5) [P2, P5, P12, P14].

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the client's knowledge level is 'Moderate', but the questionnaire in [P1] records 'No understanding / knowledge' in response to three direct questions about understanding stocks and shares, EIS/VCT, and pension planning.

      - The report describes suitability for a 'Balanced' risk investor who 'may have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' [P2, P12, P14], but the questionnaire responses record no understanding of core investment types and no investment experience in those products over the prior 5 years.

      - The client's natural risk level assessed as 'Cautious Balanced' (4) conflicts with the stated 'Moderate' knowledge level, suggesting the report conflates risk tolerance with knowledge.

    The report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge, but this is contradicted by the questionnaire evidence. The questionnaire [P1] contains direct, explicit assessment questions, and the client's responses were 'No understanding / knowledge' to three fundamental investment knowledge areas: stocks and shares, EIS/VCT, and pension planning. These are not self-deprecating asides but formal answers to direct assessment questions scored in the questionnaire. The client's experience corroborates limited knowledge: investment in current accounts and savings only, no prior investment in unit trusts, stocks, or pension products within 5 years. The Risk profile report notes [P3, P4] record 'No additional notes were recorded' for Investor Experience and Agreed Risk Level, indicating no discussion or nuance was documented to elevate the assessed knowledge level beyond what the questionnaire recorded. The agreed risk level of 'Balanced' (5) was justified by the descriptor 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' [P2, P12, P14], yet the questionnaire explicitly records the opposite. The report's assertion of 'Moderate' knowledge is not supported by and is materially contradicted by the assessed responses in the evidence file. This represents a high-severity mismatch: a report describing knowledge as 'Moderate' when the documented questionnaire assessment records 'No understanding' of core investment types.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report states the client has previously taken out cash savings products (confirmed by client), has NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by themselves, and HAS held group pensions for some time.

    File:     The risk profile questionnaire [P1] records: (1) 'No understanding / knowledge' of how stocks and shares work and risks; (2) 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement; (3) 'Yes, by myself' for invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA within last 5 years; (4) 'No' for invested in stocks and shares within last 5 years; (5) 'No' for invested in pension planning products within last 5 years. The fact find [P15] records client as HGV Driver employed by CDH Recruitment with gross annual earnings details. Existing arrangements show client holds multiple pension schemes (People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00) [P3, P4, P8, P9, P12], evidencing long-term pension holdings but as passive workplace/existing arrangements rather than self-directed selections. The questionnaire explicitly records 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' [P1].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' which is consistent with evidence [P1] showing 'No' for stocks and shares within last 5 years and 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares.

      - Report asserts client 'held your group pensions for some time' but evidence [P1] explicitly states 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' This is a material discrepancy: holding existing pensions passively (workplace pensions) is not the same as having invested in or chosen pension products, and the questionnaire distinguishes between the two. The report characterises passive holdings as active investment experience.

      - Report quotes client confirmation about cash savings products which aligns with evidence [P1] showing 'Yes, by myself' for current accounts and savings within last 5 years.

      - Report silent on client's recorded 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning despite recommending pension consolidation and new pension investments.

    The report's treatment of pension experience contains a critical inconsistency. The client holds multiple existing pension arrangements accumulated over time, but the risk profile questionnaire [P1] records that within the last 5 years the client has NOT invested in 'pension planning and income in retirement products' and has 'No understanding / knowledge' of 'how pension planning and income in retirement works and the risks associated with them.' The applicable guard states: 'Holding a workplace pension is passive experience; a report distinguishing it from self-directed investing is being accurate, not inconsistent.' However, the report does not distinguish between passive and active experience—it simply asserts the client 'held your group pensions for some time' without clarifying whether this represents active investment decisions or passive workplace enrollment. The questionnaire response 'No' to active pension investing within 5 years combined with 'No understanding' of pension planning indicates the client has not made active choices about pension products and explicitly records no understanding of the domain. The recommendation consolidates five existing pensions into a new Aviva Platform arrangement with two actively selected funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%, CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund 50%) [P23, P24]. The report does not evidence that the client understands pension planning or has any comprehension of the new product's features, despite the product's complexity requiring explanation. The fact find [P15] records employment as HGV Driver with no evidence of financial sophistication or investment knowledge beyond the questionnaire responses. The report quotes the client's confirmation of group pension holdings but does not address the 'No understanding' response or explain how lack of understanding is reconciled with recommending a complex self-invested pension platform with choice of two actively managed balanced funds. This is a Potential Concern because the report relies on passive pension holding as evidence of experience without addressing the explicit 'No understanding' of pension planning recorded in the same evidence pack, and without providing comprehension support where the product warrants it.
