  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the recommended product—Aviva Pension Portfolio with Flexi-Access Drawdown (FAD)—is Complex. The report identifies complexity drivers as: pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options, multiple fund choices within platform, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required, death benefit options and nominations, and Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. The report recommends a transfer of £110,185 into this product with two funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%, CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund 50%) and describes FAD as providing 'a flexible and tax-efficient means of drawing benefits from a pension fund' with no upper limit on withdrawals, each taxed at marginal rate.

    File:     The retrieved passages provide extensive product literature and terms describing the Aviva Pension Portfolio and FAD features. P1, P4, P20 explain FAD mechanics: clients can take up to 25% tax-free at outset, remainder invested, with regular or ad-hoc withdrawals taxed at marginal rate. P4 notes FAD provides 'a tax-efficient way of passing wealth down through the generations as any unused pension fund can usually be passed down to a wide range of beneficiaries free from Inheritance Tax.' P4 also details new allowances replacing lifetime allowance from 6 April 2024 (Lump Sum Allowance £268,275). Death benefits are explained in P1, P20, P4: before age 75 beneficiaries receive funds tax-free; after 75 taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate. P4 explicitly states IHT treatment change from 6 April 2027. Fund literature (P8, P9) confirms CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund seeks long-term growth with balanced volatility, equities 30-70%, has ongoing charge 0.29%, minimum investment £250,000. HSBC fund details shown in P19 with 5-year return 40.06%. However, the file contains no evidence documenting the client's stated knowledge level, experience, investment understanding, or comprehension of product complexity.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report asserts the product is 'Complex' based on identified drivers (FAD, multiple funds, tax strategies, death benefits, IHT changes), but the case file does not contain a fact-find, needs analysis, risk assessment summary, or any documented record of the client's knowledge level, investment experience, or demonstrated understanding before the recommendation.

      - The report identifies complexity drivers including 'tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required' and 'Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027,' but the file provides no evidence that the client was assessed as having, or being capable of acquiring, knowledge of these tax planning complexities.

      - The report recommends FAD with 'no upper limit to the amount the individual can withdraw as an income' (P4) and withdrawal decisions affecting tax position (P22: income 'may not match exactly what is shown on your illustration'), but there is no evidence in the file of any discussion with the client regarding tax planning capability, marginal rate awareness, or ongoing tax monitoring responsibility.

      - The report cites death benefit complexity and IHT planning from 2027, but there is no evidence in the file of client questions, acknowledgment, or comprehension regarding these features or their application to the client's circumstances.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Fact-find documenting client's prior investment experience, Documented client knowledge level or grade, Record of client's existing pension/investment holdings evidencing experience, Risk profile assessment or questionnaire response recording stated knowledge, Any notes or records of adviser discussion with client regarding product complexity, FAD mechanics, tax implications, or comprehension, Suitability assessment or needs analysis showing how client profile justifies complexity recommendation, Any evidence of client questions, explanations sought, or confirmation of understanding of FAD, withdrawal tax treatment, or IHT implications

    The canonical model extracted from the report identifies the recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio as 'Complex' and lists legitimate complexity drivers: FAD mechanics, tax efficiency, death benefit options, and IHT planning from 2027. The product literature retrieved (P1, P4, P20, P22) corroborates that these features exist and are material. However, the requirement under CHK-004 is not merely that the product is complex, but that the complexity is 'consistent with the client's demonstrated knowledge and experience' and that the 'comprehension is corroborated in category C where the product is complex.' The case file retrieved contains extensive product documentation but contains no evidence of the client's knowledge level, prior investment experience, existing holdings, risk assessment, or any record of comprehension discussion. The extraction metadata notes that the report itself contains an ambiguity regarding risk rating (4 vs 5 'Balanced') and capacity for loss ('medium'), suggesting the risk and knowledge documentation in the report itself may be unclear. Critically, no fact-find passage, client knowledge assessment, or comprehension notes appear in the retrieved evidence (Categories G, H, I returned 64 hits but none address client knowledge assessment or understanding confirmation). The comparison cannot be completed because the foundational data—documented client knowledge and experience—is absent from the file. The suitability report asserts complexity, and the product literature confirms complexity exists, but without evidence of the client's assessed knowledge or demonstrated understanding, no determination can be made whether the complexity is appropriate to the client's profile or whether comprehension was established.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P4)

        "Flexi-access drawdown provides a flexible and tax-efficient means of drawing benefits from a pension fund. An individual can take up to 25% of the pension fund being crystallised as a tax-free lump sum at outset (subject to the Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275 for people with no form of lifetime allowance protection). The remainder of the fund remains invested within a tax advantaged environment from which the individual can take regular or ad-hoc income withdrawals to suit their needs and circumstances. There is no upper limit to the amount the individual can withdraw as an income. However, each withdrawal is taxed at their marginal rate of Income Tax."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P4)

        "From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits (such as drawdown funds and uncrystallised pots) will be included in your taxable estate for IHT."

      · Key Features (1).md [H] (P1)

        "You can choose to draw down any level of income your pension pot can support. Under flexi-access drawdown, you can also take one-off withdrawals either alongside or instead of regular withdrawals. Payments are crystallised on a gross of tax basis."

      · Key Features (1).md [H] (P22)

        "If your tax position isn't exactly as described, the income you get through our Self-Select phased income drawdown option may not match exactly what is shown on your illustration. This is because when you take income from your drawdown fund, we assume your tax rate applies to all of your taxable income."

 

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report states the client's knowledge level is 'Moderate' and the recommended product complexity is 'Complex'. The report asserts: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing' and describes the client as understanding pension freedoms, flexi access drawdown, and investment pathways. The report states the client 'want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' and recommends a two-fund model portfolio (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%, CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%).

    File:     The fact find and risk profile evidence show contradictory findings about the client's actual knowledge. The risk questionnaire responses [P9] show: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'. The client has invested 'Yes, by myself' only in 'Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments' and answered 'No' to unit trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCT, and pension products in the last 5 years. The report notes [P7]: 'John, you are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.'

    Discrepancies:

      - Report characterizes knowledge as 'Moderate' but questionnaire [P9] records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning and income in retirement

      - Report states client has 'some experience in investments' but evidence shows investment limited to savings/cash products only, with no experience of unit trusts, stocks, shares, or pension products in the past 5 years

      - Report describes client understanding pension freedoms and flexi access drawdown, but evidence shows explicit 'No understanding / knowledge' of 'pension planning and income in retirement' in risk questionnaire

      - Report recommends keeping 'investment strategy fairly simple' but proposes actively managed multi-asset funds with exposure to equities, fixed income, REITs and derivatives, which is not simple for a client with no understanding of stocks and shares or pension planning

    The report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge and recommends a 'Complex' product. The canonical model extraction flagged an internal inconsistency: the questionnaire indicated risk rating 4 (Cautious Balanced) but the report documents the client agreeing to rating 5 (Balanced). The decision logic requires evidence that complexity is proportionate to knowledge. The evidence presents a material contradiction: the risk questionnaire [P9] explicitly records the client as having 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning and income in retirement. The client's investment experience is confined to cash savings products completed 'by myself'. Yet the report reproduces the precondition for a Balanced risk profile from [P1] and [P7]: 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' This precondition is not met. The guard states: 'A risk-profile description carries preconditions about the client's experience. Where the report reproduces one whose preconditions the file contradicts, the description is being used as evidence of knowledge it does not establish.' The report is using the Balanced investor profile description as proxy evidence that the client possesses the prerequisite knowledge, but the questionnaire directly contradicts this. The recommended funds [P5] and [P11] include actively managed exposure to equities (30-70%), fixed income, REITs, and derivatives. This is materially more complex than the client's demonstrated knowledge supports. The report notes at [P7] only that 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' but does not reconcile this with the fund complexity or the explicit 'no understanding' recorded in the knowledge questions.

        "The Fund seeks to provide long term growth (combining capital and income) consistent with a balanced volatility level over the long term. The Fund is actively managed. At any point the Fund may be invested in collective investment schemes, equities, fixed income securities including both government and non-government bonds, real estate investment trusts and derivatives.   [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report states that the client 'feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section). The report asserts the client has comfort with investments and reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards.

    File:     The Risk profile report (category D) contains two sections directly relevant to comprehension: 'Investor Experience' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire'. The Fact Find does not record any specific investment experience or questions asked by the client about the recommended products.       - Report implies active client evaluation; Risk profile shows no notes were recorded for investor experience or capacity for loss discussions

      - No evidence in file of client questions or discussion regarding the curated investment portfolio (CIP) structure with multiple funds across asset classes

    The suitability report asserts comfort and reasonable understanding of investments based on client self-assessment on page 4. However, the file evidence reveals material gaps. The file notes explicitly categorize the client's experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' (P2), which directly contradicts the report's characterization of 'reasonable understanding.' The Risk profile report (P14, P16) records 'No additional notes' for both Investor Experience and Capacity for loss questionnaire sections, indicating no exploration or documentation of how the client would evaluate the recommended CIP approach. The recommended solution is demonstrably complex: it comprises a 'carefully curated selection of funds across different asset classes, regions, and sectors' (P4) with 'active oversight and governance' by an investment committee (P7), and the report explicitly states 'You require a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions' and justifies rejection of simpler investment pathways (P7). This is a complex product as defined by the requirement. The file contains no record of client questions about the CIP structure, fund selection rationale, diversification mechanics, or how the multiple funds work together. The absence of recorded investor experience notes and the explicit statement that experience is limited, combined with the complexity of the recommended product, creates a material discrepancy. The guard permits that absence of recorded questions is not proof of lack of understanding, but here it is compounded by explicit file statements contradicting the report's assertion of reasonable understanding.

The evidence shows these are held, but the policy documents do not explain whether the client selected them, inherited them from previous arrangements, or accepted them passively through employer auto-enrolment or adviser recommendation. The guard states that 'Holding a product is not the same as understanding it. Holdings corroborate experience; they are not proof of knowledge.' Applied here, the client's holdings in equity funds suggest some exposure to stocks & shares but do not prove the client made the original investment decision or understands the products held. The report states the client has held 'group pensions for some time', which is consistent with holding five arrangements, but the evidence does not detail duration or engagement. The assertion of prior cash savings experience is entirely unsupported by the retrieved evidence. The evidence does confirm the client holds multiple arrangements across occupational and personal pensions with varying complexity (lifestyling, high-equity funds, managed funds), which suggests experience, but the supporting narrative for how the client came to hold these arrangements and what the client understands about them is absent. No fact-find dialogue, questionnaire responses, or adviser notes are retrieved that would evidence the client's own investment decision-making or comprehension. The report's narrative about knowledge and experience is sparse and does not reconcile the stated lack of personal stocks & shares investment experience with the current holdings of equity-heavy funds.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the recommended products are 'Complex' and identifies complexity drivers as: (1) Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options; (2) Multiple fund choices within platform; (3) Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required; (4) Death benefit options and nominations; (5) Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. The report provides detailed rationale across multiple topics including Employer Contributions, Small Pots Encashment, Lower Costs, Lifestyling Risk, Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments, and Multi-asset Fund Access.

    File:     The file evidence records limited investment knowledge and experience. P8 states the client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and that 'you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' P14 contains a section 'Investor Experience' with the note 'No additional notes were recorded,' indicating no substantive evidence of investment experience was documented. P8 records the client is a 'Balanced investor' with the description: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' P20 (Fact Find) shows Age 69, natural ATR from questionnaire as 4, selected ATR as 5. P12 records 'Natural ATR from Questions: 4' and 'Selected ATR (if different): 5.' The risk profile report (P22) indicates natural risk level 4 was assessed but then moved to 5. P8 notes on capacity for loss state the client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' and has income from employment, state pension, and spouse income, supporting capacity to bear loss but not demonstrating investment knowledge. No evidence of previous investment holdings, stock market experience, or understanding of pension flexibility features is recorded in the file.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report identifies complexity drivers including 'Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options,' 'Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required,' and 'Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027,' but the file records no evidence the client was questioned on or demonstrated understanding of these concepts. P14 shows 'Investor Experience' section has 'No additional notes were recorded.'

      - Report asserts product complexity is appropriate to client's knowledge and experience, but P8 explicitly states client experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements,' contradicting any assertion of sophisticated investment knowledge.

      - Report provides rationale addressing features like lifestyling, multi-asset fund access, and adviser charging facilitation, but no corroborating file notes document whether these complex features were discussed with or explained to the client in the context of their limited experience.

      - Report recommends moving from existing arrangements (which include lifestyling funds the client has been passively invested in) to an active choice platform requiring ongoing decision-making about fund selection; no evidence in file that client's limited experience was deemed sufficient for this transition or that comprehension of the new complexity was tested.

    The report asserts the recommended product is complex and identifies five substantive complexity drivers related to pension flexibility, tax treatment, and investment choice. The file establishes the client has limited investment experience, confined to workplace pension arrangements. The extract metadata notes (ambiguities section) records uncertainty about whether final agreed risk rating is 4 or 5, but both passages P8 and P12 confirm the client moved from a natural ATR of 4 (Cautious Balanced per P22) to agreed ATR of 5 (Balanced). P8's descriptor of Balanced investors states 'If you have some experience in investments...then you may be suited to a Balanced approach,' with the conditional 'if' suggesting experience may not be present. P8 explicitly records 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements,' which is a direct, material contradiction to any claim the client possesses knowledge matching a complex product. P14's risk profile report shows no documented investor experience beyond the questionnaire. The rationale provided in the report addresses platform features, cost, and fund choice, but none of the file notes (P8, P9, P10, P11, P14) record any discussion of these complex features, any evidence the client asked questions about pension freedoms or tax-efficient withdrawal strategies, or any assessment that the client understood the transition from passive lifestyling to active platform management. Under COBS 9.2.1R-9.2.2R, suitability requires knowledge and experience to be assessed and factored into recommendations; here the file explicitly records limited experience, yet the report recommends a complex product without corroborating evidence that comprehension was tested or that the complexity was proportionate to demonstrated knowledge. The file notes do not show discussion of the five complexity drivers identified by the report. This is not explained by any guard: a complex product still requires rationale even at small values, and a client's file contains contemporaneous records of discussions; absence of such records is material. Severity is High because the report's implicit assertion that the client's knowledge supports a complex recommendation directly contradicts the file's explicit statement that experience is limited to workplace pensions.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report states £1,700 per month expenditure; Fact Find shows £1,718.00 per month (1.1% higher). Report states 'combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure'; Fact Find shows monthly deficit of £288.00 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718). Report states pension funds totalling 'approximately £106,000'; itemised values sum to £116,998.47 (10.4% higher); transfer value stated as £110,185 (3.9% higher). 21 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts an initial advice fee of £3,305.55 (3% of amount invested) and ongoing costs of 1% per annum, stated as circa £1,101.85 for the first year. Affordability is supported by the statement that 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, you hold savings of around £6,000, and have no outstanding liabilities' with pension funds totalling approximately £106,000. The client confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living.

    File:     The Fact Find (P1) shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, producing a Total Monthly Disposable Income of £(288.00) — a deficit of £288 per month. Current savings are evidenced as £6,000 (P5). The intelliflo planning documents (P5, P8) show combined employment income of £15,600 p/a (John, £300 weekly) plus £1,040 p/a (Sea Cadets, £20 weekly) plus £13,200 p/a (Kim, £1,100 monthly) = £29,840 gross p/a. Pension fund values are itemised: People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00, totalling £116,998.47 (not the £106,000 stated in the report, and not the £110,185 transfer value on page 19).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states £1,700 per month expenditure; Fact Find shows £1,718.00 per month (1.1% higher)

      - Report states 'combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure'; Fact Find shows monthly deficit of £288.00 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718)

      - Report states pension funds totalling 'approximately £106,000'; itemised values sum to £116,998.47 (10.4% higher); transfer value stated as £110,185 (3.9% higher)

      - Report implies affordability of ongoing contributions from surplus income; Fact Find demonstrates negative monthly disposable income of £(288.00)

    The initial fee of £3,305.55 is a one-off transfer from existing pension assets and does not create an affordability concern from income — the guard permits one-off transfers of existing pension money not to be tested against monthly surplus. However, the ongoing commitment of 1% per annum presents a material inconsistency. The report states income 'comfortably meets' expenditure at approximately £1,700 per month, but the Fact Find establishes that actual expenditure is £1,718 monthly against net income of only £1,430, producing a deficit of £(288) per month. This £288 monthly shortfall directly contradicts the affordability assertion. The report acknowledges £6,000 in accessible savings, but these are finite liquid reserves and cannot sustainably fund a monthly deficit. The pension figure discrepancy (£106,000 stated vs £116,998.47 evidenced, a £10,998 variance, or £110,185 transfer value, a £4,185 variance) creates ambiguity about the asset base underpinning the resilience assessment. The cashflow modelling in the intelliflo scenarios (P2, P5) shows projected surpluses in retirement years, but those are forward projections, not current affordability. Current month-by-month affordability is not evidenced as comfortably positive; it is evidenced as deficit. The basis of affordability rests on an income-expenditure statement that is factually contradicted by the Fact Find. No evidence addresses how the monthly shortfall will be bridged in the near term, nor how the ongoing 1% adviser fee (approximately £1,102 in year 1, equating to circa £92 per month on the estimated £110k fund value) will be afforded within the current deficit position.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts total monthly net income of £4,486.67 (comprising HGV employment £1,300, Sea Cadets £86.67, State Pension £1,000, and C2 Employment £1,100), total monthly expenditure of £1,700, and a monthly surplus of £2,786.67. Total assets are £6,000 with zero liabilities and net worth of £6,000. The report presents three cashflow scenarios all projecting no shortfall to age 100: (S1) baseline cashflow to age 100 with all surplus income spent; (S2) high inflation stress test at 6% showing no shortfall; and (S3) combined market crash of -30% with 6% inflation showing no shortfall. All scenarios use disclosed assumptions including 2% general expenditure inflation, 2.5% state pension inflation, and 0% surplus income saved.

    File:     The Fact Find (P2) records Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, yielding Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00 (a deficit, not a surplus). The Fact Find shows only employment income of £1,200 net monthly plus state pension of £230 monthly (£1,430 total), with no mention of £86.67 Sea Cadets income or £1,100 C2 employment income. The intelliflo planning documents (P14) confirm opening savings of £6,000, pensions totalling £116,997 (not £116,998.47 as report figures sum), and zero liabilities. The Fact Find projects household expenditure in retirement of approximately £1,700 per annum, not per month. The intelliflo baseline scenario (P22) and proposed scenario (P9) both project positive surpluses throughout the plan to age 100, with liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining substantial (£122,991+ and £140,061+ respectively at age 100 in baseline and proposed scenarios). The planning documents show income statement first year combined regular income of £41,813 and combined expenditure of £24,001 in both scenarios (P12, P9).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states total monthly net income £4,486.67; Fact Find shows total net monthly income £1,430.00 - a difference of £3,056.67 (214% variance).

      - Report breaks down monthly income as HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + State Pension £1,000 + C2 Employment £1,100; Fact Find records only Employment £1,200 + State Pension £230 = £1,430, with no Sea Cadets or C2 Employment line items visible.

      - Report asserts monthly surplus of £2,786.67; Fact Find shows monthly disposable income of -£288.00 (a deficit of £3,074.67 difference).

      - Report states total monthly expenditure £1,700; Fact Find states 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' (P1) - this is annual, not monthly, creating a tenfold discrepancy in interpretation.

      - Report projects no shortfall in baseline scenario to age 100; intelliflo baseline scenario (P22, P5) shows declining assets after age 100 with deficits emerging in 2056-2059 (shortfalls of -£2,930, -£2,876, -£3,014, -£3,357) before exhaustion at age 100/96.

      - Report's cashflow assumptions include employment income (implicitly) as ongoing, but Fact Find 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future?' is marked 'No', and intelliflo timelines show 'Retire' events at 2026 (age 70/66) and 2027 (age 71/67) (P23), indicating employment income cessation.

      - Report states total assets £6,000; this matches Fact Find savings only, but excludes the £116,997 in accessible pensions that should be included in total liquid and accessible resources for affordability analysis.

    The comparison reveals material inconsistencies between the report's financial position narrative and the underlying Fact Find and planning documents. The most critical discrepancy is the income figures. The report derives £4,486.67 monthly income from four sources, but the Fact Find evidences only £1,430 monthly, a shortfall of £3,056.67. The Sea Cadets income (£86.67) and C2 Employment (£1,100) are not visible in the Fact Find table (P2, P21), and the HGV employment is recorded at £1,200 (not £1,300). The State Pension figure of £1,000 monthly in the report does not align with the Fact Find's £230 monthly state pension entry. The report appears to have modelled £11,973 annual state pension (corroborated in P1, P12) which is approximately £998 monthly, closer to the reported £1,000 than the Fact Find's £230 entry. The expenditure error is equally grave: the report states £1,700 monthly expenditure, but the Fact Find and planning documents indicate this is annual retirement expenditure. The Fact Find shows monthly expenditure of £1,718 (P2), which produces a deficit not a surplus. The monthly surplus of £2,786.67 reported is arithmetically inconsistent with both sets of underlying figures. The Fact Find shows a monthly deficit of -£288; the planning documents' first-year combined income (£41,813) minus first-year combined expenditure (£24,001) yields approximately £1,501 monthly surplus, not £2,786.67. The cashflow scenarios in the report project no shortfall to age 100, but the intelliflo baseline scenario (P5, P22) explicitly shows deficits of -£2,930 to -£3,357 in years 2056-2059 (ages 100-103), directly contradicting the report's assertion of 'no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used' throughout retirement to age 100. The proposed scenario (P9, P12) does show stronger asset growth and remains positive to age 100, but this is for the proposed consolidated arrangement, not the current situation the baseline tests. The report's statement that 'all surplus income throughout your lifetime is spent, rather than saved' is supported by the 0% surplus savings assumption (P4, P17), and the planning documents confirm surplus accounts remain at £0 (P14, P23). The employment income modelled in the report appears to assume continuation at current levels (£300 per week for HGV plus £20 for Sea Cadets per P19), but the Fact Find does not list Sea Cadets employment, the planning timeline shows retirement events in 2026-2027 (P23), and the statement 'typically earning around £300 per week net' (P19) uses the qualifier 'typically', suggesting variability not reflected in deterministic inputs. The recommendation rests on the assertion that financial position supports contributions and that affordability is robust, but the foundational income and expenditure figures are demonstrably inconsistent with the Fact Find. The total assets figure of £6,000 cited excludes accessible pensions of £116,997, which materially understates the resources available. The consistency requirement demands that the report's figures be supported by the Fact Find; they are not.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that the client has confirmed sufficient liquid capital (£6,000) to cover unexpected emergencies. The report states on page 3: 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning.' On page 8, it adds: 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' The report indicates emergency fund access is addressed and the client is comfortable with liquidity arrangements. The report also states that should the client require access to pension funds, it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete.

    File:     The Fact Find [P6] records a cash savings asset of £6,000.00 as at 22/09/2025. The intelliflo baseline scenario [P16] shows liquid assets declining from £5,912 in 2027 onwards, with continuing modest declines throughout the projection period. The proposed scenario [P7] shows opening liquid assets of £6,000 in 2025, declining to £5,956 in 2026 and continuing to decline. The cashflow statements [P5] and [P8] show positive in-year surpluses throughout the projection period (ranging from £20,620 to £21,195 per annum in baseline; and £21,064 to £21,329 in proposed scenario through 2055). The Fact Find [P14] shows current monthly income of £1,430 against expenditure of £1,718, yielding a monthly deficit of £288. However, the intelliflo income table [P3] and [P23] show total regular annual income of £41,813 (employment £29,840 plus Kim's income £13,200 minus tax & NI £3,385) against total regular expenditure of £24,001, generating surplus of approximately £17,812 annually. There is no explicit quantification of a required emergency fund amount in the evidence.

    Discrepancies:

      - The Fact Find [P14] shows current monthly cash flow as negative (£-288 monthly deficit), contrasting sharply with the proposed scenario's annual surplus projections of approximately £20,739-£21,329 per annum [P7], [P8]. The monthly deficit and annual surplus figures are mathematically inconsistent: a £288 monthly deficit would imply an annual deficit of approximately £3,456, not a surplus.

      - The report asserts client comfort with emergency fund sufficiency but does not quantify what constitutes sufficient emergency cover (no required amount stated in report or evidence).

      - The liquid assets are projected to decline modestly but continuously from £6,000 in 2025 to £5,912 by 2027 onwards in baseline [P16], and from £6,000 to £5,956 in 2026 in proposed scenario [P7], suggesting the emergency fund may be gradually depleted rather than maintained as a buffer.

      - No evidence in the file specifies whether the £6,000 cash reserve is explicitly earmarked and protected as an emergency fund, or whether it is available to be drawn as part of general expenditure or contribution payments.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Required emergency fund amount (quantified benchmark for what constitutes 'sufficient'), Confirmation whether the £6,000 cash asset is ringfenced as emergency reserve or available for general drawdown, Reconciliation of the monthly cash flow deficit (£-288 in Fact Find [P14]) with the annual surplus figures (£20,000+ in intelliflo scenarios [P7], [P8]), Foreseeable financial needs beyond the projection period (client is currently aged 69/65, projections extend to age 100+/96+)

    The comparison cannot be performed to a complete standard because no quantified requirement for emergency fund sufficiency appears in either the report or the evidence. The report asserts that the client 'confirmed' sufficiency but does not state what amount or duration of expenses that sufficiency represents. This is a common gap in affordability assessments—sufficiency is asserted without a measurable threshold. The £6,000 figure is corroborated in the Fact Find [P6] as a savings asset held, but there is no evidence stating this is dedicated to emergencies or that £6,000 meets the client's needs. A material inconsistency exists between the Fact Find's monthly cash flow analysis [P14], which shows the client currently in deficit (£-288 monthly), and the intelliflo planning scenarios which project consistent annual surpluses of £20,000+. This inconsistency is explained partly by the Fact Find capturing only employed income (£1,430 net monthly = £17,160 annually) while intelliflo captures total household income including state pension from age 66 and includes Kim's income (£13,200 p/a), as shown in [P3], [P23]. However, the reconciliation is not provided in the evidence. The fact that liquid assets decline modestly but persistently over time [P7], [P16] suggests that the £6,000 emergency buffer is not being actively replenished or maintained; it is being consumed. The case file includes evidence that ongoing charges will be levied from the cash account [P11], and disinvestment may be triggered if insufficient cash is available to pay income or charges [P11]. Against this backdrop, an undeclared, modest, and declining emergency fund may be at risk of depletion. The Key Features [P11] state that 'If there isn't enough in your cash account to pay your income, your adviser will be able to choose on your behalf how to sell assets,' implying a reliance on asset liquidation rather than cash reserves when shortfalls occur. The access to pension drawdown is confirmed as 10-15 working days [canonical model], which is consistent with standard pension withdrawal timescales documented in [P1] (Aviva cash account terms). No evidence addresses whether the client has foreseeable financial needs (e.g., planned expenditure, vehicle replacement, home maintenance) that should be funded from emergency reserves. The vulnerability overlay: no indicators of vulnerability, support needs, or communication requirements specific to liquidity or emergency fund management are noted in the evidence provided, so this does not materially affect the assessment.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts affordability based on: (1) Initial commitment of £3,305.55 (3% advice fee); (2) Ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 annually (1% per annum); (3) Combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (4) Client holds savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (5) Pension funds total approximately £106,000; (6) Monthly surplus of £2,786.67 derived from income and expenditure; (7) Client confirmed potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living.

    File:     Fact Find (Test 1) [P1] [P2] shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00; Total Monthly Disposable Income £-288.00 (a deficit). The same document states "Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure £1,718.00" with client confirming "No" to expected changes in expenditure. Intelliflo planning Baseline scenario [P11] shows Total Regular Income £41,813 annually (£3,485 monthly approximately) but also shows Total Regular Expenditure £24,001 annually. The Fact Find income details [P2] show Employment income £1,200 net monthly plus State Pension £230 net monthly (future, dated 22/09/2025) totalling £1,430 current net monthly income. The asset position [P21] confirms Liquid Assets £6,000 and Pensions £116,997, with total assets £122,997.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states monthly disposable income (surplus) of £2,786.67; Fact Find shows monthly disposable income of £-288.00 (deficit). These cannot both be correct.

      - Report states combined income comfortably meets expenditure of £1,700; Fact Find shows net income £1,430 against expenditure £1,718, creating a £288 monthly deficit, not a surplus.

      - Report asserts surplus income supports affordability; Fact Find demonstrates actual monthly deficit where expenditure exceeds income.

      - Report cites pension funds totalling approximately £106,000; computed sum of existing arrangements is £116,998.47, a difference of £10,998.47 (9.4% discrepancy).

      - Monthly surplus of £2,786.67 in report cannot be reconciled to any figure in the evidence. The fact find baseline shows deficit, not surplus. The intelliflo baseline annual income £41,813 does not generate monthly surplus of £2,786.67.

    The fundamental affordability assessment in the report is contradicted by the Fact Find document which is part of the same case file. The report derives a monthly surplus of £2,786.67 and asserts that combined income comfortably meets expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month. However, the Fact Find explicitly calculates Total Monthly Disposable Income as £-288.00, showing a monthly deficit of £288 where expenditure (£1,718) exceeds net income (£1,430). This is material. The report's basis of affordability rests on income covering expenditure with surplus remaining; the Fact Find contradicts this by demonstrating a deficit. The client's stated need is £1,700 net monthly, which is £270 short of even the stated expenditure of £1,718. The deficit position means the client cannot afford ongoing commitments of £1,101.85 annually from surplus income as the report claims. While the client does hold £6,000 in liquid savings which could temporarily offset this, and substantial pension assets, the affordability finding in the report (that income comfortably meets expenditure) is contradicted by the Fact Find's cash flow calculation. The ongoing advice fee of 1% (£1,101.85 in year one) cannot be supported by surplus income as no surplus exists on the evidence. The initial fee of £3,305.55 can be paid from the £6,000 savings, leaving only £2,694.45, but this depletes the emergency fund which the report cites (savings of around £6,000) as part of the affordability basis. The report offers no discussion of how ongoing commitments will be met in the face of documented monthly deficit, nor does it address the tension between deploying savings to pay initial fees and maintaining an emergency fund. The guard regarding employment income cessation is noted: the Fact Find shows State Pension beginning 22/09/2025, but employment income of £1,200 is stated with no end date. The client is aged 69/65 (per [P21]) and states intention to retire in 2 years ([P15]), which would eliminate the £1,200 employment income. If applied, this guard would further worsen the affordability position post-retirement. The report's monthly surplus calculation of £2,786.67 is unexplained and cannot be derived from any figures in the evidence. The Intelliflo planning document shows different income/expenditure figures at annual level but does not reconcile to the £2,786.67 monthly surplus claimed.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report's canonical model asserts that liquidity needs are funded from Pension, with no mention of emergency fund shortfall. The extracted model shows `/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/fundedFrom` as ["Pension"], and the absence of `/financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall` in the canonical model.

    File:     The evidence shows two scenarios: Baseline (existing arrangements) and Proposed (recommended solution). In Baseline scenario [P1], [P3], [P6]: opening liquid assets are £6,000 (Savings account [P6]), with Accessible Pensions of £116,997. The cashflow table [P1], [P6] shows "Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions" declining slightly over time (£122,991 in 2026 down to £122,953 by 2038), but maintains positive in-year surplus/deficit of £20,000+ annually with 0% surplus saved. In Proposed scenario [P7], [P9], [P10]: opening liquid assets remain £6,000, Accessible Pensions increase to £118,632 (2026) growing to £157,808+ (2044). The cashflow [P9] shows "Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions" growing from £122,997 (2025) to £157,808 (2044), with consistent positive in-year surplus of £20,000+ annually. Both scenarios show a separate "Surplus Account" line item initialized at £0 [P5], [P10]. No explicit emergency fund reserve is quantified or discussed in the narratives.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts liquidity needs are 'funded from Pension' but the evidence shows £6,000 cash savings exist separately from pension assets at plan outset [P6].

      - The canonical model contains no `emergencyFund` field at all, neither value nor shortfall, despite the requirement explicitly checking for emergency fund depletion or unavailability.

      - The planning documents show Surplus Account initialized at £0 with assumption "0% of any in-year surplus will be saved" [P5], meaning no emergency reserves are being accumulated despite consistent annual surpluses of £20,000+.

      - The report does not articulate what the £6,000 opening liquid assets represent or whether they are designated as an emergency fund.

    The guard states: 'A transfer between pension wrappers does not deplete an emergency fund — only new money from cash does.' The recommendation consolidates existing pensions into Aviva Platform (£110,185 transfer value) but this is a transfer between pension wrappers, not a draw from the £6,000 cash account. The cashflow projections [P1], [P9] show liquid assets remain stable (around £5,900-£6,000 range in early years, gradually declining) while Accessible Pensions grow, particularly in the Proposed scenario. The second guard states: 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it.' Examining the liquidation order: in both scenarios, the in-year surplus/deficit consistently remains positive (£20,000+ annually), and the tables show 'Where there is a deficit, a drawing from liquid assets and/or accessible pensions is made to meet the shortfall' [P6]. No such deficit occurs in either projection—all years show positive surplus. The £6,000 opening cash is never drawn down in the projections; it is maintained as a liquid buffer. However, the critical deficiency is that the report contains no express discussion of emergency fund adequacy. The canonical model lacks any `emergencyFund` field, meaning no analysis of sufficiency, quantification, or policy regarding this £6,000 was documented in the report. The assumption that 0% of surplus is saved means no additional emergency reserves are being built. The evidence supports that liquid capital remains available (the £6,000 plus growing accessible pensions), but the report does not articulate this as an emergency fund strategy or address adequacy for the clients' foreseeable needs. The Proposed scenario shows improvement in total liquid and accessible assets (£122,997 to £157,808 by 2044), but this growth is in pension wrappers (Accessible Pensions increase from £116,997 to £157,808+), not cash emergency reserves. The applicability statement 'capitalContributionsOrWithdrawals = true' confirms the check applies because pension transfers and growth involve capital movements, and the question of whether liquid emergency funds remain adequate is central to affordability.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs

  Potential Concern — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report recommends a Personal Pension wrapper with a selected retirement age of 71. The report asserts this recommendation without detailing specific liquidity or access needs that fall between now and age 71, or any emergency fund provisions that would bridge any gap.

    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Client is currently 69 years old (born 07/06/1956, per P12, P19). (2) Minimum pension age is currently 55 (P2, P3, P4). (3) Client can access pension at any time from age 55 unless they have a protected pension age (P2, P3, P4). (4) The Personal Pension wrapper permits flexible access from age 55 through flexi-access drawdown or lump sum withdrawal (P2, P9, P14). (5) Liquid assets are £6,000 in savings (P12, P19, P21). (6) The planning scenarios (Baseline and Proposed) show retirement events at age 71 for one spouse and age 66 for the other, with liquidity projections running to 2060 (P12, P19). (7) No specific access needs or emergency fund requirements are quantified in the file before age 71.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report does not assert or evidence any specific liquidity requirement between now (age 69) and the retirement age of 71. The Personal Pension wrapper does permit access from age 55, but the file does not establish whether the client has or lacks access needs in this 2-year window.

      - The report's selected retirement age of 71 differs from the spouse's retirement age of 66 shown in the planning scenarios (P12, P19), but this is not inherently a discrepancy with the report's own assertion since the recommendation may be tailored to one spouse.

      - Liquid assets of £6,000 are identified (P12, P19, P21), but the file does not specify whether this constitutes an adequate emergency fund or whether it is earmarked for other purposes.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Specific quantified liquidity or access needs (requiredFrom dates and amounts) are absent from the extracted canonical model and not clearly stated in the evidence. The canon entry `/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom` is absent, which means either the report is silent on this or extraction failed. Without this, a true liquidity mismatch assessment cannot be completed., Quantification of the emergency fund: whether the £6,000 liquid assets constitute an identified and ring-fenced emergency reserve, or whether an emergency fund adequacy assessment was performed., Affiant confirmation of whether access to the pension before age 71 is needed, or whether the 2-year gap from age 69 to 71 poses any identified cash-flow risk.

    The trigger is satisfied (capitalContributionsOrWithdrawals = true). The client is already 69 years old, which exceeds the current minimum pension age of 55. This invokes the false-positive guard: 'Pension access restrictions are irrelevant where the client is already over the minimum pension age — check age before flagging.' Since the client is already over 55, the minimum pension age restriction does not create a liquidity barrier. The Personal Pension wrapper offers flexible access from age 55 via flexi-access drawdown or lump sum (P2, P4, P9, P14), so the client can access funds if needed before age 71. However, the requirement asks whether affordability is supported by evidence of financial resilience and liquidity. The file shows: (1) £6,000 liquid savings; (2) pension funds totalling approximately £117,000; (3) planning scenarios to age 2060 with regular income and expenditure modelled. Yet the core input is missing: the report does not explicitly state what liquidity or access needs the client has before age 71, and the extraction did not capture a `/requiredFrom` field. Without quantified access needs stated in the report, a comparison cannot determine whether the recommended Personal Pension wrapper will restrict access when the client needs it. The planning projections (P5, P12, P19, P24) show cashflow modelling but do not articulate a specific emergency fund threshold or a dated access requirement. The guard allows the comparison to be skipped when the client is over minimum pension age and no specific access need is evidenced, but the absence of evidence of an access need is not the same as evidence of no access need. The requirement is to assess whether affordability is supported; the file does not provide the required input to make that assessment.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts State Pension income categories in the canonical model at /financialPosition/incomes[] showing 'StatePension' as a category appearing twice in the income list. [P5] Fact Find State Pension Entitlement section is present but appears blank (no amounts disclosed).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's State Pension start date of 2026-03-26 (March 26, 2026) contradicts the Fact Find [P6] which records State Pension income as commencing 22/09/2025 (September 22, 2025) — a discrepancy of approximately 6 months.

      - The month-day components '-03-26' in the report's startsOn date do not match any identifiable date in the passages. [P6] shows 22/09/2025 as the start date; [P4] shows retirement 'age 66 in 2026' but not a specific calendar date.

      - The report extracts show five income entries with categories, but the gross amounts for State Pension entries are marked as absent from the canonical model, whereas [P6] evidences £230.00 net monthly State Pension and [P8] evidences State Pension in the income composition.

    The comparison can be performed because State Pension is evidenced across multiple passages. However, material discrepancies exist in timing. [P6] Fact Find lists State Pension commencement as 22/09/2025, which is approximately 6 months earlier than the report's extracted date of 2026-03-26. Given that [P4] planning scenarios show retirement 'age 66 in 2026' and JS is age 65 (born 07/05/1960), State Pension age 66 would fall in May 2026, not March 2026. The specific date 2026-03-26 in the canonical model does not align with either the Fact Find date of 22/09/2025 or the projected retirement date coherent with age 66 reached May 2026. [P6] records State Pension income as part of the household income assessment at £230 net monthly. The report's absence of a gross State Pension figure in the extracted model is notable given that affordability analysis typically requires both gross and net amounts. [P8] Baseline scenario incorporates State Pension into 'Total Regular Income' projections but without explicit separation of the quantum. The planning scenarios [P2], [P14] show State Pension as a cashflow component but do not quote specific amounts. Employment income is consistently shown as £300 per week net (or £1,200 monthly in [P6]) across sources. The discrepancy in State Pension start dates is material to cashflow and affordability modelling, particularly given the client's stated need for £1,700 net monthly household income [P15] and current surplus deficit of -£288 [P6]. If State Pension income is delayed from September 2025 to March 2026, or further delayed to May 2026, the interim cashflow position differs materially.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts four planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs: (1) Client retirement in October 2027 with a financial impact of -£1,386.67 net monthly (loss of HGV employment income £1,300/month and Sea Cadets income £86.67/month), supported by the quote 'You expect to retire within the next two years'. (2) Kim's retirement in March 2026 with a financial impact of -£1,100 net monthly (loss of employment income), supported by 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully'. (3) Kim's State Pension commencement in March 2026 with a financial impact of +£1,000 net monthly, supported by 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age'. (4) Inheritance Tax regime change for pensions from 6 April 2027 with no quantified financial impact, supported by 'From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits (such as drawdown funds and uncrystallised pots) will be included in your taxable estate for IHT'. The report also asserts one liquidity and access need: access to funds in the future via pension drawdown with no quantified amount, noting it will take '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete'.

    File:     The Fact Find [P1] states 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future?' with answer 'No' and shows calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 against net monthly income of £1,430.00, creating a monthly disposable income deficit of -£288.00. The Fact Find [P14] records State Pension income of £230.00 monthly already in effect (showing '22/09/2025' start date). The Fact Find [P17] notes 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The intelliflo planning proposed scenario [P12] shows a Timeline with events: 'Retire (Kim)' at age 66 in 2026, 'Retire (John)' at age 71 in 2027, plus 'Illness (John)' and 'Illness (Kim)' at age 100, and 'Death (John)' and 'Death (Kim)' at age 100. The proposed scenario [P2] displays liquid assets declining from £5,411 (2039) downward through the projection period. The Baseline scenario [P6] shows Surplus Account opening value of £0 and Savings of £6,000. The proposed scenario [P12] shows current Assets Tree: Liquid Assets £6,000, Pensions £116,997, with a 'New Aviva pension' of £110,185. File notes [P15] mention consolidation and being 'ready for retirement with all the options'. The Summary Cashflow graph in the proposed scenario [P5] shows expenditure throughout the plan with Required Withdrawals from both Pensions and Investments where shortfalls occur, with red bars indicating insufficient liquid assets or accessible pensions to meet shortfalls.

    Discrepancies:

      -  The Fact Find [P1] records no expected changes in expenditure in the foreseeable future, yet the report asserts specific expenditure reductions upon client and Kim's retirements (£1,386.67 and £1,100 monthly respectively).

      -  The Fact Find [P14] shows State Pension income of £230.00 already commenced as of 22/09/2025, contradicting the report's assertion that this is a future need commencing in March 2026 when Kim reaches age 66.

      -  The report quantifies Kim's State Pension impact as +£1,000 net monthly, but the Fact Find [P14] records only £230.00 monthly State Pension. The quote provided states 'around £230 per week' which at face value would be approximately £1,000 monthly (230 × 52 / 12 ≈ £996), yet the Fact Find shows £230 monthly not weekly, creating ambiguity on whether this figure has been correctly converted or if a different income stream is being referenced.

      -  The current monthly cashflow in the Fact Find [P1] shows a deficit of -£288.00 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), but the report does not explicitly address how this existing shortfall will be managed or how the recommendation improves or sustains this position at retirement.

      -  The intelliflo planning scenarios [P2], [P5], [P6], [P7] show Summary Cashflow analysis with Required Withdrawals from pensions and investments throughout the projection period, including red bars indicating insufficient liquid assets or accessible pensions in certain years, but the suitability report does not quantify or detail these liquidity constraints or shortfalls during the stated retirement periods.

      -  The Fact Find [P1] records emergency fund/liquid assets of only £6,000 in cash savings, yet the report does not quantify or explicitly address whether this emergency fund is adequate or will be depleted by the recommendation or the retirement transition.

      -  The report asserts client retirement in October 2027 with -£1,386.67 monthly impact but the intelliflo timeline [P12] shows 'Retire (John)' at age 71 in 2027, and 'Illness (John)' and 'Death (John)' at age 100 in 2056 and 2060 respectively; the projection period in scenarios [P2], [P6] extends to 2060 (age 104/100), suggesting long-term withdrawal requirements are modelled but not explicitly reconciled with the near-term retirement date.

      -  The report references emergency fund provision and access to liquid assets as part of financial resilience assessment (per the check requirements) but provides no explicit commentary quantifying the emergency fund, its adequacy relative to expenditure, or its availability post-recommendation.

    The comparison can be performed as the Fact Find, planning scenarios, and suitability report all provide sufficient data on income, expenditure, retirement timing, and projected cashflows. However, material inconsistencies and gaps exist between what the report asserts and what the evidence shows. (1) Expenditure changes: The Fact Find explicitly states no expected changes in expenditure in the foreseeable future, contradicting the report's derivation of specific income losses upon retirement. This is a discrepancy in the source data, not a reporting error; the report has derived a different conclusion from the Fact Find. (2) State Pension timing and amount: The Fact Find [P14] shows State Pension of £230.00 already in the income schedule as of 22/09/2025 (the fact find date). The report asserts this as a future event in March 2026 when 'Kim reaches State Pension age', but the evidence suggests it may already be in receipt or the timing is misaligned. The amount mismatch (report says £1,000 net monthly; Fact Find shows £230 monthly) creates further uncertainty. The quote 'around £230 per week' could support £1,000 monthly if interpreted as weekly, but the Fact Find explicitly lists it as monthly, not weekly. (3) Existing deficit: The Fact Find shows current monthly disposable income of -£288.00 before any retirement or recommendation changes. The suitability report does not address how this existing shortfall is currently managed (whether from savings drawdown, pension access, or other means), nor how the recommendation affects this position during the transition to retirement. (4) Liquidity and emergency fund: The Fact Find records only £6,000 in liquid savings. The intelliflo scenarios [P2], [P5], [P6], [P7] show Summary Cashflow projections with Required Withdrawals from pensions and investments, and red bars indicating insufficient liquid assets in certain years. The suitability report does not quantify whether the £6,000 emergency fund is adequate, whether it is expected to be preserved, or whether it will be consumed by the recommendation or retirement transition. (5) Retirement income requirement vs. current deficit: The Fact Find [P17] notes the client 'needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' at retirement. Current expenditure is £1,718.00 monthly against current income of £1,430.00, a gap of £288.00. At retirement, with employment income ceasing (£1,300 + £86.67 = £1,386.67), the net income would fall to approximately £43.33 (£1,430 - £1,386.67), creating a required withdrawal of approximately £1,674.67 annually or £139.56 monthly to meet the £1,700 stated need. The planning scenarios [P2], [P5] suggest Required Withdrawals throughout the projection, but the specific affordability of the stated retirement income need is not explicitly quantified or reconciled with the recommendation in the suitability report. (6) Kim's income and retirement: The report asserts Kim's retirement in March 2026 with loss of £1,100 monthly income, offset partially by £1,000 monthly State Pension, for a net loss of £100 monthly. The Fact Find [P14] shows Kim's employment income of £1,200 monthly, not £1,100 as the report asserts. This creates a £100 discrepancy in the income loss calculation. (7) Capital accessibility and timeframes: The report notes pension drawdown will take '10-15 working days' for access, which is relevant to liquidity planning, but does not address whether this timeframe is acceptable given the existing monthly deficit and modest emergency reserves. The check requires commentary on 'Emergency fund provision and access to liquid assets' and 'Consistency of financial information and conclusions across the Fact Find, File Notes, Needs Analysis and Suitability Report'. The evidence shows inconsistencies in expenditure expectations, State Pension timing and amounts, and insufficient explicit quantification of emergency fund adequacy. The recommendation's interaction with the existing monthly deficit and the stated retirement income requirement is not clearly reconciled. The guard on SetCoverage allows for undated future plans and aspirational needs; however, the report's assertions on specific income reductions and access timing are contradicted or not fully supported by the evidence. The report's failure to quantify the emergency fund as 'sufficient' or address the existing cashflow deficit represents a gap in the affordability analysis required by COBS 9.2.1R-9.2.2R and the check's decision logic.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts multiple rationales for selecting Aviva as provider and the recommended investment strategy: (1) Provider selection rationales include financial strength (A rated by S&P, £407bn AUM), choice (6,900+ funds), expertise (Aviva Investors award-winning strategies), value (transparent competitive charges), convenience (online tools), governance, and innovation. Client-specific rationales include: lower costs than existing pension, greater investment choice aligned to risk profile, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring capability. (2) Investment strategy rationales include: funds reflect risk profile (rating 5 Balanced), diversification across asset classes, blend of active and passive investments keeping costs low, simplicity as desired by client, competitive charges, strong peer performance, CIP membership with quarterly investment committee review, and multi-asset fund providing professionally managed diversified solution aligned to financial goals and risk profile. (3) Recommended funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced (50%), both risk rating 5.

    File:     Evidence from research documents shows: (1) SIPP research report (P1) lists 4 products matching filters applied with no DNA analysis: Aviva Platform (Aviva), Elevate (Aberdeen), ARC (Aegon), Wealthtime Platform (Wealthtime). Later search (P9) shows 1 product from 118 total matched filters: Pension Portfolio - Choice (Aviva). No explanation of filter criteria or why Aviva was selected over other 117 products. (2) Fund performance data (P12) shows discrete performance: HSBC 2024: 10.82%, CT Universal MAP 2024: 11.27%; 5-year: HSBC 40.06%, CT Universal MAP 44.73%; 10-year: HSBC 122.95%, CT Universal MAP data not shown. (3) Cost disclosure (P10) shows HSBC total 0.19% (0.18% ongoing + 0.01% transaction), CT Universal MAP total 0.55% (0.29% ongoing + 0.26% transaction), combined 0.37%. Report cites 'competitive charges' but provides no benchmarking or comparison. (4) Key Features document (P8) describes Aviva Platform as consolidating investments in tax wrappers, potentially saving time/money and reducing fund charges in large range of funds. (5) Policy research table (P13) lists reasons for switching: Aviva (existing) 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; Scottish Widows similar. (6) Suitability report (P2) discusses investment pathways offering lower costs and reduced complexity but discounts them because client needs more tailored solution than four generic offerings. (7) No evidence in pack of whole-of-market research comparing Aviva to alternatives beyond the 4 platforms or 118 total products mentioned in research filters.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' (page 7), but existing Aviva plan charged 0.50%, Zurich 0.93%, and recommended Aviva Platform charges 0.44% total - only Zurich savings are demonstrated; Aviva-to-Aviva switch saves 0.06%. Report provides no cost comparison to Elevate, Aegon, or Wealthtime platforms shown in research.

      - Report claims Aviva offers 'Choice - Over 6,900 funds' but research document (P1, P9) shows no DNA analysis was carried out and platform selection filters show 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', suggesting fund range was not evaluated.

      - Report states funds 'have performed well in comparison with their peers' but research shows CT Universal MAP outperformed HSBC over 5 years (44.73% vs 40.06%) and 2024 (11.27% vs 10.82%), yet both are presented equally as 50/50 recommendation without explaining why the underperforming fund is included.

      - Report asserts provider selection included consideration of financial strength, expertise, governance, innovation as differentiators, but research document (P1) indicates 'No filters were applied' and 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', contradicting any detailed feature evaluation.

      - Policy research table (P13) notes existing Aviva as 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' with recommendation to switch, but report does not explicitly acknowledge that the existing provider being recommended against makes the rationale for selecting its successor platform require stronger comparative justification.

      - Report states investment strategy provides 'a blend of active and passive investments' but fund prospectus details not provided in pack; P10 shows HSBC classified as indexed/passive strategy (FTSE/S&P 500 holdings) while CT Universal MAP is active multi-asset, but this distinction is not explained in recommendation rationale.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Whole-of-market research documentation comparing Aviva Platform to Elevate (Aberdeen), Aegon Retirement Choices (ARC), and Wealthtime Platform beyond the fact that they matched filters, Explanation of filters applied to shortlist platforms, given research states 'No filters were applied', Detailed fund benchmarking data showing peer comparison for CT Universal MAP and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio against their respective peer groups, Comparative cost analysis across the four platforms identified in initial research, Documentation of adviser's assessment of alternatives to multi-asset fund strategy (e.g., why investment pathways were rejected is stated but supporting analysis not evidenced), Explicit rationale linking each recommended fund selection (HSBC 50% / CT MAP 50% allocation) to the client's objectives, rather than generic attributes

    The report provides multiple statements of rationale for both provider and investment selection, but the comparison between this case and available evidence reveals material gaps. On provider selection: the research document demonstrates that 118 products were searched but only 1 matched the final filters (Aviva). The document explicitly states 'No filters were applied' and 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', which contradicts the implicit suggestion in the report that features like financial strength, choice, expertise and governance were actively evaluated as differentiators. The initial search identified 4 platforms (P1) with no explanation of why Aviva was selected; the later search (P9) shows 118 products searched but only Aviva matched — yet the basis for this matching is not documented. The report's rationales are therefore stated in isolation rather than as conclusions of comparative analysis. On investment strategy: the report asserts competitive charges and strong peer performance but provides no benchmark data showing these funds outperformed alternatives, and in fact P12 shows CT Universal MAP outperformed HSBC over the past 5 years. The report does not explain why both funds are weighted equally despite different performance. The report's assertion of 'blend of active and passive' is not corroborated; fund classification data is absent from the pack. No cost comparison to the three alternative platforms shown in research is provided. The discrepancy between stated filter methodology ('No filters applied') and the report's implication of deliberate feature evaluation is not reconciled. The policy research table (P13) acknowledges existing Aviva was expensive and lacked adviser servicing, which supports switching; however, this does not alone justify selecting the successor platform over alternatives, yet the report presents provider rationales without acknowledging that the recommendation is to a platform offered by the same provider being switched away from. The overlay consideration regarding vulnerability: the evidence does not record any vulnerability characteristics in the case file, so this overlay does not affect the assessment of this requirement.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The canonical model asserts isClientSpecific values across 28 rationale entries. Of these, 23 are marked true and 5 are marked false. The report therefore claims that the majority of rationales are client-specific and personalised to the client's circumstances.

    File:     The suitability report [P13] states: 'Following a recent review of your financial affairs we identified certain needs and objectives as detailed below. This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives.' The policy research document [P15] shows that for individual arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows) the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank or contains no objective reference. The research notes state generically: 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' for Aviva and 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' for Zurich, without linking these to named client objectives. The fact find [P5] identifies the client's stated objective: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The document [P14] confirms: 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement.'

    Discrepancies:

      - The canonical model marks 23 of 28 rationales as isClientSpecific=true, but the policy research document [P15] shows that the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for multiple recommendations (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows), indicating rationales are not explicitly linked to named objectives.

      - The report asserts client-specific rationale exists for each recommendation, but the evidence in [P15] shows only generic reasons for rejection (e.g., 'expensive, no adviser servicing') without personalisation to the client's specific situation.

      - Five rationales are marked isClientSpecific=false in the canonical model, but no corresponding text is provided to explain what these non-client-specific rationales contain or why they were classified as such.

      - The fact find [P5] records a clear objective ('consolidate pensions to help him get his head around what he has'), but the policy research [P15] does not consistently map recommendations to this stated objective across all five existing arrangements.

    The suitability report asserts in its canonical model that 23 of 28 rationales are marked as client-specific (isClientSpecific=true). However, examination of the supporting evidence reveals material gaps. The policy research document [P15] explicitly contains a field 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' that is left blank for multiple recommendations (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows). This is direct evidence that rationales in the underlying research are not linked to named objectives. The reasons given are generic ('expensive', 'no adviser servicing', 'lifestyle') and repeat across different recommendations without personalisation. While the suitability report itself [P13] uses generic framing ('Following a recent review of your financial affairs we identified certain needs and objectives'), the underlying policy research shows that the mapping from recommendation to objective has not been completed for the majority of the five existing arrangements under review. The client's objectives are clearly stated in [P5] and [P14] (consolidation for simplicity, retirement in 2 years, household income need of £1,700 net per month), but these are not explicitly connected to each arrangement decision in the supporting documents. The false-positive guard does not apply: standard regulatory wording is distinct from the absence of objective linkage in the research layer. The discrepancy is material: the report claims personalised client-specific rationale, but the evidence shows blank objective fields in the underlying research and generic reasons for rejection.
