  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four alternatives were considered: (1) Leave pot to grow until retirement (rejection: simplification preference, not client-specific); (2) Internal fund switch within Zurich (rejection: pension consolidation preference linked to OBJ3, client-specific, citing unavailable multi-asset funds and poor performance vs Aviva); (3) Stakeholder pension with single multi-asset fund (rejection: higher cost at 1.5%/1% vs recommended solution, limited flexibility and fund ranges); (4) Investment Pathway solutions (rejection: lack of tailoring to OBJ1/OBJ2, inaccurate profile alignment, need for ongoing advice, poor performance). Additionally, five research options are documented: OPT1 (Leave untouched, no rejection reasons stated), OPT2 (In-situ fund switch, no rejection reasons stated), OPT3 (Switch to existing arrangement, no rejection reasons stated), OPT4 (Employer scheme, three rejection reasons: loss of employer contribution, higher costs 0.06% more expensive plus £4.50 admin, limited fund range/no multi-asset), OPT5 (New plan, recommended).

    File:     P6 lists five existing arrangements with a summary table showing retirement options and fund availability. P6 states the recommendation accounts for risk questionnaire and analysis of available options. P18 provides detailed rejection reasoning for four alternatives: (1) Scottish Widows: lacks single multi-asset aligned to risk rating 5, requires combination with cash holdings limiting market exposure, lacks adviser management, limits withdrawal flexibility, estimated £170 net after tax under small pots rules vs tax-free potential at retirement; (2) Aviva alternative: only mixed-asset fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet client's Attitude to Risk (ATR); (3) Zurich alternative: no risk level 5 funds, blend of 7 and 2 used to create risk 5, no multi-asset funds in risk range, blend has underperformed Aviva, client's main priority is consolidation and better control; (4) Standard Life alternative: all funds within 20-60% equities range more expensive than Aviva option. P2, P4, P5 provide comparative pension switching analysis showing effect of switching (reductions of 0.3-10% depending on scenario and arrangement). P16 states stakeholder pensions have maximum 1.5% charge for 10 years then 1%, are more cost-effective than typical stakeholder, lack flexible access and limited fund ranges.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states 'Leave pot to grow until retirement' has rejection reason 'simplification preference' marked as non-client-specific; P18 shows this option involves discussion of tax treatment (25% tax-free cash vs full personal allowance) which is client-specific tax analysis not reflected in report's characterization

      - Report lists 'Internal fund switch within Zurich' rejection reason as generic 'consolidation preference' in summary; P22 provides detailed client-specific reasoning that Zurich multi-asset funds unavailable, existing blend has underperformed, and client priority is consolidation and control — more specific than report suggests

      - Report characterizes Stakeholder pension rejection (cost comparison 1.5%/1%) as lacking client-specificity; evidence shows this is generic comparison, but report provides no reference to client's specific cost structure or comparison to recommended 0.44% solution which would be client-specific

      - Report states Investment Pathway rejection reasons link to OBJ1 and OBJ2; no evidence in file links these objectives to Investment Pathway comparison or validates that pathways were actually researched and rejected on these grounds

      - Report lists five research options (OPT1-OPT5) but OPT1, OPT2, OPT3 have no rejection reasons stated despite being listed as non-recommended; file evidence shows detailed rejection reasoning for Scottish Widows (OPT2 equivalent), Aviva alternative (OPT2 for Aviva), Zurich, and Standard Life, but research option framework lacks corresponding detailed rejection reasons

      - Report cites Aviva employer scheme (OPT4) rejection including 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution'; P18 states plan is 0.06% more expensive and has £4.50 admin charge, but canonical model shows Aviva existing at 0.50% and new solution at 0.44%, suggesting new solution is 0.06% CHEAPER not more expensive — this is contradictory phrasing in report

      - File evidence (P5, P1, P3, P10) shows multiple ranked comparisons of existing pensions vs new solution with switching effects ranging from 0.3% to 10% decrease depending on scenario; report does not present these ranked comparisons or quantify performance trade-offs for alternatives

    The report presents alternatives with mixed specificity. Four named alternatives appear in the recommendations section with stated rejection reasons; five research options appear in the research section with incomplete reasoning documentation. The evidence supports that alternatives were researched and rejection reasons documented, though the framing differs between research and recommendation sections. Key discrepancy: the report's characterization of rejection reasons as 'non-client-specific' for some options contradicts file evidence showing detailed client circumstance analysis (e.g., tax implications of small pots for Scottish Widows, client's stated consolidation priority for Zurich, risk profile misalignment for Aviva). The Aviva employer scheme cost comparison contains apparent internal contradiction: report text says plan is 0.06% 'more expensive' but numerical data suggests 0.50% existing vs 0.44% new equals 0.06% reduction, not increase. The research section lists options without detailed rejection reasons (OPT1, OPT2, OPT3), creating documentation gap between research performed and reasons stated in recommendations. Pension switching comparisons in evidence (P1, P3, P5, P10) show ranked outcomes (2-10% reduction in yield depending on option) but report does not reference these ranked comparisons or explain why performance trade-offs of 0.3-10% are acceptable given client objectives.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Five existing arrangements are identified with the following recommended outcomes and rationales: (1) People's Pension - Retain, with rationale that it is the current workplace scheme with ongoing contributions from client and employer until retirement; (2) Scottish Widows - Encash, with rationale that withdrawal under small pots rules would yield £170 net and would not detract from retirement plan, but also notes alternative that leaving it to grow could allow tax-free withdrawal within personal allowance at retirement; (3) Aviva pension my future focus consolidation - FullTransfer, with multiple rationales including lower costs (0.06% saving), lifestyling risk concerns, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring; (4) Zurich Managed Pn AP - FullTransfer, with rationales including lower costs, lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and explanation of multi-asset fund blending due to lack of risk level 5 funds at Zurich; (5) Standard Life at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund - FullTransfer, with rationales including lower costs (though text states 0.08% more expensive, contradicting the lower costs claim), lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring.

    File:     The case file contains policy information and valuations for all five existing arrangements: People's Pension (P21 shows 7 funds available, current workplace scheme); Scottish Widows (P21 lists as option); Aviva Designer Stakeholder (P7 shows plan value £3,744.36, invested in Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2 with auto-switching/Lifestage Investment Approach); Zurich Managed Pension AP (P9 shows plan holder Mr John Alan Sullivan, retirement date 7 June 2026 age 70); Standard Life Flexible Retirement Plan (P4, P6 show valuation statement issued 1 October 2025, value on 30 September 2025 £2,994.30). The overall file strategy (P12) notes the client 'has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together' and strategy involves consolidation and cashflow planning. Policy research (P3, P5) documents all five arrangements with dates and valuations. No document in the evidence explicitly states the recommended action for each arrangement or provides the specific rationales in the form presented in the report.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Standard Life transfer rationale includes 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' but then states on same page 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', directly contradicting the lower costs justification

      - Report presents Scottish Widows encash rationale as 'would not detract from your financial retirement plan' but simultaneously presents alternative rationale that leaving it to grow could achieve tax-free withdrawal, without clearly stating which action is recommended or why one is preferred over the other

      - Report rationale for Zurich transfer cites 'multi-asset fund selection' issue and lack of risk level 5 funds, explaining use of blend of risk level 7 and level 2 funds, but evidence (P9) shows Zurich illustration dated 24 September 2025 does not indicate what funds client is currently in or whether risk level issue exists in the evidence

      - Report states Aviva transfer rationale includes 'Lower Costs' but the charge comparison in the canonical model shows conflicting figures (0.50% existing vs 0.44% new equating to 0.06% saving, yet text claims 0.08% difference on Standard Life, and other passages reference different percentages), creating ambiguity about whether lower costs claim is properly supported

      - Scottish Widows arrangement shows conflicting advice: rationale states encash for £170 net, but alternative presented states 'If you were to leave this pot to grow until you retire, you could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due' - report does not clearly state which is the recommended action or explain why encash is chosen over the alternative

      - All transfer recommendations (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) present generic rationales verbatim across multiple arrangements (e.g., 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring') without arrangement-specific justification beyond general statements

    The report does state a recommended outcome for each of the five existing arrangements and provides rationales. However, there are material concerns about the quality and clarity of these rationales. First, the Scottish Widows arrangement presents two mutually exclusive outcomes (encash vs retain for growth) without clearly stating which is recommended or why. The report text supports encash but then immediately presents a superior alternative (tax-free withdrawal if left to grow) without explaining the rejection of that alternative. Second, the Standard Life rationale contains an internal contradiction: the 'Lower Costs' justification is immediately contradicted by the statement that 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. This is a factual contradiction within the same rationale. Third, multiple transfer rationales are duplicated verbatim across arrangements (Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments appear identically for Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life), suggesting boilerplate language rather than arrangement-specific analysis. The People's Pension rationale is appropriately specific (workplace scheme with ongoing contributions) and the Aviva rationale addresses lifestyling concerns specific to that arrangement, but the pattern across transfers indicates insufficient customisation. The evidence does corroborate that these arrangements exist, but does not contain the detailed advisory rationales presented in the report - these are extracted from the report itself and are not independently evidenced in the case file. The charge comparisons cited in rationales contain internal inconsistencies in the canonical model (different percentages for different arrangements) that undermine confidence in the cost-based justifications.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome

  Indeterminate

    Report:   The report makes 6 discrete recommendations: (1) Transfer People's Pension into Aviva Platform (Recommended, Adviser to implement, outcome: consolidate while retaining employer contributions); (2) Encash Scottish Widows pension (Recommended, Adviser to implement, outcome: withdraw under small pots and deposit to savings); (3) Switch Aviva pension into Aviva Platform (Recommended, Adviser to implement, outcome: consolidate with improved flexibility and lower costs); (4) Switch Zurich pension into Aviva Platform (Recommended, Adviser to implement, outcome: consolidate with flexibility and monitoring); (5) Switch Standard Life pension into Aviva Platform (Recommended, Adviser to implement, outcome: consolidate with lower costs and flexibility); (6) Rebalance consolidated pension into HSBC 50% and CT Universal MAP 50% (Recommended, Adviser to implement, outcome: diversified portfolio aligned to risk rating 5 over 10-year horizon). All six recommendations are explicitly stated as 'Recommended' with adviser implementation confirmed. Expected timescale for implementation is 10-15 working days. Implementation steps include beneficiary nomination completion post-transfer, cancellation notice review (30 days), and acknowledgment of out-of-market risk during pending transactions.

    File:     The retrieved passages consist of Aviva Platform Terms and Conditions (sections on charges, death benefits, income drawdown options, retirement choices, and service access), Key Features document describing retirement income options (lump sum, drawdown, annuity combinations), Risk Profile Report confirming agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) with no additional notes recorded on investment target, agreed risk level, investor experience, or capacity for loss, and a SIPP research report documenting the risk level agreement and fund allocations (CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%). The passages do not contain evidence supporting or contradicting whether the adviser is making these recommendations, whether the adviser will implement them, or what the intended outcomes are. The passages are administrative and contractual documents covering the destination platform's features and the risk profiling process, but contain no suitability narrative, recommendation statement, or implementation commitment from the adviser.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Suitability report narrative explaining the adviser's recommendation and implementation commitment, Adviser-to-client communication stating the recommendation status for each change, Documentation of adviser's commitment to arrange transfers and switches, Supporting evidence that adviser has stated intended outcomes for each action

    This requirement asks whether the report states that a change is recommended, who will implement it, and what the intended outcome is. The canonical model confirms that the report does contain all three elements for all six recommendations: each is marked 'Recommended', each specifies 'Adviser' as responsible party, and each includes a detailed intended outcome statement. The retrieved passages do not include the suitability report itself or adviser communications; they are ancillary documents (provider terms, key features, risk profiling records, fund research). These passages neither corroborate nor contradict the report's statements because they do not address whether the adviser recommends the changes or commits to implementing them. The comparison cannot be performed because the evidence pack lacks passages from the suitability report's own recommendation sections. Without retrieved passages containing the actual recommendation statements, alternative advisor communications, or contradictory evidence about implementation status, a consistency assessment cannot be completed. The report's own assertions are documented in the canonical model, but the file provides no independent evidence to test whether those assertions are accurate or whether they are supported by or contradicted by the case materials.

 

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts six recommendations with actions: Transfer, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch, and Rebalance. All six recommendations specify the responsible party as 'Adviser'. Three recommendations (Transfer, Encash, and the first Switch) specify an expected timescale of '10-15 working days'. The solution-level implementation specifies a timescale of '10-15 working days' for access to funds. The report includes next steps: (1) Complete nomination of beneficiaries form after transfer process begins (client-specific); (2) Review cancellation notice with 30-day period (generic); (3) Out-of-market risk disclosure noting client comfort with stock market movements pending completion. The report states 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' and notes cancellation rights of 30 days post-setup.

    File:     The evidence pack contains Terms and Conditions (P1-P7), Key Features (P8), Fund Factsheet (P6), policy information for Scottish Widows (P10) and Standard Life (P14), and financial planning scenarios (P9, P11-P12). P1 discusses cancellation or changing income drawdown payments, requiring adviser action 10 working days in advance of income payment dates. P7 describes withdrawal processes for Structured Products as non-Business Day transactions that wait until the next Business Day, with potential penalties for early exit. P1 notes fund closure procedures with 30-day advance notice 'unless external factors beyond Our control mean that only a shorter notice period is possible.' P8 (Key Features) states the scheme aims to 'give you and your adviser access to information about your investments online' and notes adviser requirements for certain changes. No specific passages detail the 10-15 working day timescale for transfer completion, adviser responsibilities for executing transfers, or step-by-step implementation sequencing. The evidence does not explicitly confirm who executes each action type or confirm the timescale applies uniformly across all five transfer recommendations.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report specifies six distinct recommendations with action types (Transfer, Encash, Switch x3, Rebalance) but evidence does not corroborate the existence or detail of each specific action recommendation or confirm responsibility assignment to adviser for each.

      - Report states three recommendations have '10-15 working days' timescale but evidence does not explicitly state this timescale applies to Transfer, Encash, or Switch actions; P1 references 10 working days for income payment changes (different context) and P7 references non-Business Day delays (different context).

      - Report asserts adviser responsibility ('Adviser') for all six recommendations but evidence does not explicitly confirm adviser executes transfers, encashment, or switches; P8 notes adviser involvement for 'certain changes' but does not enumerate which actions the adviser executes.

      - Report next steps include 'Complete the nomination of beneficiaries form that will be provided after the transfer process is begun' but evidence does not specify when this form is provided or whether completion timing is material to suitability.

      - Report states '30 days to review and consider your options' post-setup as a cancellation right but P1 and evidence do not confirm this 30-day period is a cancellation right distinct from statutory cancellation periods or how it applies to transfers.

      - Report claims client is 'comfortable with this concept' (out-of-market risk) 'as you have liquid cash should you require instance access to funds' but evidence does not confirm existence of liquid cash reserves or client comfort statement in any supporting document.

    The report provides implementation detail (timescale, responsible party, next steps) across multiple locations: /solution/implementation specifies '10-15 working days' and lists three next steps; /recommendations[]/implementation/expectedTimescale lists timescales for three of six recommendations; /recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty assigns all six to 'Adviser'. The evidence pack is dominated by Terms and Conditions (P1-P7, P8) which establish operational procedures but do not corroborate the specific timescale, responsibility, or sequencing the report asserts for this client's transfers. P1 section (iv) discusses cancellation or changing income drawdown payments with a 10 working day lead time, but this applies to income payment changes, not to the initial transfer process or timescale stated in the report. P7 discusses Structured Product withdrawal procedures and non-Business Day handling but does not address the Transfer or Encash actions here recommended. P8 (Key Features) emphasizes adviser involvement for 'certain changes' but does not enumerate action types or execution responsibility. The report's claim that the client 'have liquid cash should you require instance access to funds' (spelled 'instance' in the canonical quote) is not evidenced anywhere in the supporting documents; no asset schedule or cash position document is present. The 30-day cancellation notice period is mentioned in the report but the evidence (P1, P4) discusses death benefits, fund closure procedures, and general scheme rules, not a specific 30-day cancellation right post-transfer setup. The report asserts three distinct next steps—beneficiaries nomination, cancellation notice review, and out-of-market risk comfort—but none of the specific timings, sequencing, or client-specific comfort statements appear in the evidence. The guard 'Naming the adviser firm rather than an individual satisfies by whom' is satisfied (report says 'Adviser' not 'John Smith'). The guard 'A timescale quoted as a provider service standard is a stated timescale' is relevant: if '10-15 working days' is an Aviva standard for access/withdrawal, it may be treated as a stated timescale; but evidence does not confirm this is how the report intended it or that this standard applies to the initial transfer/setup process. The comparison can be performed because the report makes specific assertions (timescale, responsible party, next steps, client position on risk) and the evidence pack contains the scheme's Terms and Conditions and product information; however, the evidence does not address the specific implementation detail for this client's transfer scenario.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). The report's total fund charges do not arithmetically reconcile with component fund allocations: report discloses two funds at 50% each (HSBC 0.18% and CT Universal MAP 0.29%), which should yield a weighted average of 0.235%, but the report states Investment Management 0.22% separately and CT fund at 0.29%, creating ambiguity about whether the 0.22% figure represents the investment management charge layer or a weighted fund average. The fund value basis for the stated monetary charges is unclear: Platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value of £110,186, Investment Management £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195, and Total £484.81 at 0.44% implies £110,184, none of which match the actual total transfer value of £116,998.47 or the reported transfer total of £110,185 (which itself is unexplained, as individual plans sum to £116,998.47). The Pension Switch Charges table on page 11 (P3) shows Aviva existing charge 0.50% with -0.06% impact, implying new charge of 0.44%, which is consistent with the stated 0.44% total; however, the text on page 6 (per extraction narrative) states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution,' which directly contradicts the calculation that shows new charges as 0.06% lower, not higher. 11 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following charges for the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension: Platform 0.21% (£231.39), Investment Management 0.22% (£253.43), HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18%, CT Universal MAP Balanced C 0.29%, with a Total Charge of 0.44%. An initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) is disclosed. Transaction costs of underlying funds are excluded with explanation that these are variable and subject to change, included in broker commissions, taxes and trade execution costs, and are already accounted for in fund performance figures presented net of all charges. The report states an aggregated total ongoing charge of 0.44% and indicates cumulative effect is shown in the personal illustration.

    File:     The evidence contains multiple corroborating sources: P3 (Suitability Report) confirms Platform 0.21% (£231.39), Investment Management 0.22% (£253.43), Total Charge 0.44%, and repeats the transaction costs exclusion verbatim. P8 (Illustration) shows Aviva charge 0.21%, CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc 0.29%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18%, with weighted average fund charge 0.23%. P1 and P2 (KIIDs) for HSBC fund show Ongoing charge 0.18% with entry charge 0.00%, exit charge 0.00%, and note that 'All charges and fees, except any entry, exit and switching charge, have been included within the performance.' P6 (Key Features) confirms adviser charges are agreed with adviser and deducted on their behalf, visible in illustration. P8 notes initial fund charge will not be taken if funds are being reregistered from another provider. P17 (Suitability Report) confirms initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) 'with current transfer values, this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values' and states 'This will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer.'

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's total fund charges do not arithmetically reconcile with component fund allocations: report discloses two funds at 50% each (HSBC 0.18% and CT Universal MAP 0.29%), which should yield a weighted average of 0.235%, but the report states Investment Management 0.22% separately and CT fund at 0.29%, creating ambiguity about whether the 0.22% figure represents the investment management charge layer or a weighted fund average.

      - The fund value basis for the stated monetary charges is unclear: Platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value of £110,186, Investment Management £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195, and Total £484.81 at 0.44% implies £110,184, none of which match the actual total transfer value of £116,998.47 or the reported transfer total of £110,185 (which itself is unexplained, as individual plans sum to £116,998.47).

      - The Pension Switch Charges table on page 11 (P3) shows Aviva existing charge 0.50% with -0.06% impact, implying new charge of 0.44%, which is consistent with the stated 0.44% total; however, the text on page 6 (per extraction narrative) states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution,' which directly contradicts the calculation that shows new charges as 0.06% lower, not higher.

      - The report discloses transaction costs are 'already accounted for in fund performance figures, which are presented net of all charges,' but provides no evidence of the projected values or illustrations actually being presented net of transaction costs; the illustration documents retrieved do not separately itemize transaction cost impact or confirm that projections are net of these costs.

      - Initial advice fee statement includes qualifier 'this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values,' yet the recommendation table and suitability text treat £3,305.55 as a fixed figure without explaining the contingency or range, and it is not clear whether this fee is deducted from the transfer values before or after investment, or whether it reduces the initial fund value.

    The suitability report discloses the major cost layers: platform charge (0.21%), investment management charge (0.22%), fund charges (0.18% and 0.29%), initial advice fee (3%), and an aggregated ongoing total (0.44%). Transaction costs are stated as excluded with an explanation. This satisfies the basic trigger for disclosure. However, material inconsistencies and unexplained figures undermine confidence in the completeness and accuracy of the cost presentation. The monetary charges (£231.39, £253.43, £484.81) are based on a fund value of approximately £110,185, yet the actual combined transfer value is £116,998.47, a discrepancy of £6,813.47 that is not explained in the report. The extraction metadata notes this £6,813.47 shortfall as an internal inconsistency. The charge calculations therefore rest on a fund value that does not match the stated sum of individual plans. Furthermore, the statement that new charges are 0.06% lower (per the Pension Switch Charges table calculation) contradicts text claiming the plan is 0.06% more expensive; this is a direct arithmetic contradiction within the report itself. The allocation of the 0.22% investment management charge to a weighted average of two funds (50% at 0.18% + 50% at 0.29% = 0.235%) is also not reconciled in the report, leaving unclear whether 0.22% is an approximation, a separate adviser charge, or a different calculation basis. The initial advice fee is stated as contingent on transfer values but treated as fixed. On the positive side, the transaction costs exclusion is properly explained with reasoning (variability, already in net performance), which meets the guard condition. The report does reference that cumulative effect is shown in the illustration, though the specific illustration page is not cited in the extracted charges section. The evidence from KIIDs, Key Features, and the Illustration itself confirms the individual platform, fund, and ongoing charges, providing corroboration of disclosure. However, the unexplained fund value discrepancy, the internal arithmetic contradiction on charge comparison, and the unclear basis for the investment management charge layer create material gaps between what is stated and what is arithmetically verifiable. The report does not explain the reconciliation between the £116,998.47 total transfer value and the £110,185 basis used for charge calculations, nor does it explain the internal contradiction on whether charges increase or decrease by 0.06%.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P3)

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P3)

Despite their variability, these costs are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges."

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial adviser fee of 3% of the amount invested (£3,305.55 with current transfer values, paid by the provider on transfer) and an ongoing annual fee of 1% of fund value (circa £1,101.85 for the first year, paid via the Aviva Platform).

    File:     [P3] TFAS Wealth terms state: 'For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1% (with a minimum fee of £100 per annum).' [P13] Initial advice services document states: 'There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on' with a minimum of £1,350 for Pensions & Investment advice. [P5] Suitability report repeats: 'The costs for this service are 1% per annum' and 'this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year.' [P9] Suitability report states: 'we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested' and 'In monetary terms this is £3,305.55 with current transfer values.' [P12] Client Agreement confirms: 'For investments, we arrange and oversee on your behalf, there are two principal fees. A fee based on the work we do to arrange the initial investment, and a fee based upon any ongoing reviews which we will do on your behalf.'

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the initial fee of £3,305.55 is calculated at 3% of 'the amount invested' with current transfer values. However, the canonical model shows existing arrangements total £116,998.47, not the £110,185 that would produce £3,305.55 at 3%. The arithmetic implies a different base figure.

      - The ongoing fee of £1,101.85 stated as 1% implies a fund value of £110,185, which does not match any stated arrangement value or the total of existing arrangements (£116,998.47).

      - The report does not explicitly state which transfer value or arrangement value the £3,305.55 and £1,101.85 fees are calculated upon, creating ambiguity about whether the basis is the stated total, a subset, or a derived figure.

    The suitability report discloses both initial and ongoing adviser fees consistent in percentage terms with the TFAS Wealth client terms: 3% initial and 1% ongoing annual. Both fees are stated with their monetary equivalents based on 'current transfer values.' The Client Agreement [P12] confirms the fee structure. The percentage rates match the documented terms in [P3], [P13], and [P12] exactly. However, a discrepancy exists in the underlying calculation: the monetary amounts (£3,305.55 and £1,101.85) are consistent with a fund value of approximately £110,185, which differs from the canonical model's sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47). The extraction note identified this as an internal inconsistency: 'Total transfer value on page 9 shows £110,185. Sum of all five existing arrangements = £116,998.47, not £110,185. Discrepancy of £6,813.47 unexplained.' The report does not explicitly disclose which transfer value or net amount these fee calculations use as their basis, nor does it explain why the total differs from the sum of the individual plan values. The fees themselves conform to the percentage rates in the client agreement, and both are properly described as being paid via the provider from the pension schemes. However, without clarity on the basis for the monetary calculation, and given the £6,813.47 variance in stated transfer values within the report itself, the consistency cannot be fully verified. The report supplies the fee disclosure required but does not reconcile the apparent discrepancy in the amount on which the fees are calculated.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P9)

        "we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement. In monetary terms this is £3,305.55 with current transfer values, this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values. This will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P5)

        "The costs for this service are 1% per annum. Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year. This will vary in line with your fund value. This fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly."

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P3)

        "For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1% (with a minimum fee of £100 per annum)."

      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md [A] (P12)

        "For investments, we arrange and oversee on your behalf, there are two principal fees. A fee based on the work we do to arrange the initial investment, and a fee based upon any ongoing reviews which we will do on your behalf."

 

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts on page 18 a comprehensive Reduction in Yield (RIY) and Critical Yield comparison table. For the recommended solution at age 75: Low growth (0.00%) shows RIY 2.1%, Critical Yield 1.9%, Effect of switching -£10,400 (9.6% decrease); Mid growth (2.95%) shows RIY 2.2%, Critical Yield 4.8%, Effect of switching -£12,000 (9.4% decrease); High growth (5.88%) shows RIY 2.3%, Critical Yield 7.8%, Effect of switching -£15,000 (10.0% decrease). For existing arrangements, RIY is 0.3% across all growth scenarios. The report also states on page 11 that based on mid-growth rate of 2.94%, the recommended plan needs to grow by 4.8% per year to meet the same value as the current plan. On page 19, a charge breakdown shows Aviva Platform Personal Pension at 0.21% platform charge (£231.39) and 0.22% investment management charge (£253.43), totalling 0.44% (£484.81). The report quantifies the effect of switching as a monetary reduction and percentage of current fund value.

    File:     Passage [P1] provides the same comparison table from the suitability report showing identical RIY, Critical Yield, and Effect of switching figures for all three growth scenarios. The illustration at [P3] and [P10] shows charges reducing pension fund value from £129,000 (before all charges) to £115,000 (after all charges) at age 75 under mid-growth scenario, equating to a 2.1% reduction in growth rate from 2.9% to 0.8%. The illustration [P10] states 'Product and Investment charges could reduce this pension fund from £129,000.00 to £126,000.00 - this is the same as reducing the investment growth after inflation from 2.9% to 2.5%. This is a reduction in the growth rate of 0.4%.' The pension switch report [P21] and [P23] show alternative comparisons for individual existing pensions with different charge structures and switching effects. The charge breakdown at [P18] confirms platform charge of 0.21%, and investment charges for CT Universal MAP Balanced at 0.29% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio at 0.18%, with weighted average of 0.23%. Passage [P19] confirms the charge breakdown of 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's 0.22% investment management charge does not appear to be substantiated in the investment charges documentation. [P18] lists individual fund charges of 0.29% (CT Universal MAP) and 0.18% (HSBC) with weighted average 0.23%, which contradicts the 0.22% stated in the report on page 19. The weighted average of 0.23% differs from the 0.22% claimed.

      - The effect of switching amounts (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) are stated in the report as monetary terms but the arithmetic check notes these cannot be matched to the total transfer value of £110,185 or individual arrangement values. The canonical model notes the sum of individual plan values is £116,998.47 but recommended transfer value is £110,185, creating an unexplained discrepancy of £6,813.47.

      - The critical yield figures for mid-growth (4.8%) are stated consistently between the report (page 11 and 18) and [P1], but the basis differs: page 11 states this includes adviser fees at 2.94% growth rate, while the table on page 18 presents it without explicit qualifier on whether adviser fees are included in all scenarios.

      - The report claims on page 19 that charges exclude transaction costs as these are variable and already accounted for in fund performance net of all charges, but no evidence from fund literature ([P8], [P12], [P14]) explicitly confirms transaction costs are netted into the reported fund performance figures for the specific funds recommended (CT Universal MAP and HSBC Global Strategy).

    The core cumulative cost disclosure is present and largely consistent between the report and evidence. The RIY and Critical Yield table on report page 18 is directly corroborated by [P1]. The illustration projections showing the cumulative effect of all charges ([P10]) align with the report's narrative. However, material discrepancies exist: (1) The investment management charge of 0.22% stated in the report charge breakdown (page 19) does not reconcile with the fund-level charges shown in the illustration. The individual funds carry 0.29% and 0.18% OCF/TER charges; a weighted average of 50% each fund yields 0.235%, not 0.22%. This is a minor rounding difference but represents an understatement of charges. (2) The effect of switching monetary amounts cannot be verified against the transfer value basis. The canonical model reveals the planned transfer value (£110,185) differs from the sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47), yet the switching impact calculations reference current fund values without clarifying which fund value basis the -9.6%, -9.4% and -10.0% decreases are calculated against. The -£10,400 at 9.6% implies a fund value of £108,333, which matches neither the individual Zurich plan value (£103,439) nor the total transfer value (£110,185). (3) The transaction costs exclusion from charges is stated but not evidenced. The fund literature ([P8], [P12], [P14]) does not explicitly confirm transaction costs are netted into net-of-charges fund performance. This is a silent assumption rather than an evidenced exclusion. (4) The advisory charge of 1% and initial fee of £3,305 are disclosed and corroborated in [P23], but the cumulative impact table on page 18 does not show these fees applied to all three scenarios, making the Critical Yield figures potentially inconsistent if the adviser charges vary in application. The comparison can be performed because the RIY, critical yield and illustrations are substantiated, but the arithmetic precision of the switching effect and the investment charge breakdown contain material gaps.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report states (page 11) that 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'This means that your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year (in monetary terms).' The report provides a 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' table showing per-arrangement impacts: Aviva (EA3) existing charge 0.50% (£18.72) with -0.06% impact (£-2.25); Zurich (EA4) existing charge 0.93% (£961.98) with -0.49% impact (£-506.85); Zurich (EA5) existing charge 0.18% (£186.19) with +0.26% impact (£268.94). The report asserts these differences aggregate to a net saving of 0.24% or £264 annually.

    File:     The evidence base includes: (P1) existing arrangement charges table showing People's Pension 0.50% fund + £4.50 admin, Scottish Widows 0.19% fund + 0.10% platform, Aviva 0.50% fund, Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin, Standard Life 0.52% fund; (P3) proposed Aviva Platform charges: 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total (£231.39 + £253.43 = £484.81); (P24) CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund shows 0.24% ongoing costs (£258.93); (P6) Zurich fund charge 0.18%; (P2) Zurich annual management charge ¾% (0.75%); (P13) pension switch report shows projected value reduction of £3,000 (2.1%) at age 75 and critical yield of 6.3% for new solution. The canonical model calculation shows existing arrangements total £116,998.47 but the Aviva Platform charge of 0.44% (£484.81) implies a fund value of £110,184.09, not matching any individual or total plan value.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states overall comparison basis as -0.24% but the sum of per-arrangement impacts shown (-0.06% - 0.49% + 0.26% = -0.29%) does not equal -0.24%

      - Report states Aviva EA3 existing charge as 0.50% matching provider documentation, but then shows -0.06% impact implying new charge of 0.44%, which differs from proposed Aviva Platform charges (0.21% + 0.22% = 0.44%) only when applied to Aviva's current value of £3,744.36, yet the comparison basis is unclear—the report does not disclose which subset of plans transfer to Aviva

      - Report states Zurich EA5 existing charge 0.18% matching provider documentation (P6: 0.18% fund charge), but Zurich Policy Info (P2) shows annual management charge of ¾% (0.75%), creating ambiguity about total Zurich charge

      - Report states comparison on 'current fund value' basis but the calculation of £484.81 annual charge at 0.44% implies £110,184.09, which matches neither the Zurich existing value (£103,439.24) nor total transferring value (£110,185)

      - Report's monetary comparison of £264 first-year saving cannot be reconciled from the per-arrangement impacts: if calculated on Zurich EA4 value (£103,439.24) the -0.49% impact gives £-506.85; if on EA5 value (£103,439.24) the +0.26% impact gives £268.94; no arithmetic path produces exactly £264 across the stated three arrangements

      - Report does not disclose existing charges for People's Pension (0.50% + £4.50 admin) and Scottish Widows (0.19% + 0.10%) in the 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' table, suggesting these arrangements are being retained, yet this is not explicitly stated

      - Report compares only three arrangements (EA3, EA4, EA5) in the switch charges table but five arrangements exist; the 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' heading suggests only certain pensions are being switched, but the report's recommendation on page 6 states 'Retain your people's pension plan' and does not explicitly list which of the five will be consolidated into Aviva

      - The proposed Aviva Platform charge of 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management) is not reconciled to the existing Aviva charge of 0.50% shown on page 5, creating internal inconsistency within the report itself (0.50% vs 0.44%)

      - Report does not disclose the basis fund value for calculating the £264 monetary impact—it could be calculated on individual plan values, subset values, or total values, each producing different results

      - Critical yield of 6.3% (P13) and projected maturity value reduction of £3,000 (P13) indicate materially higher costs than the 0.24% saving claimed, suggesting the cost comparison in section [G7.4] omits relevant cost layers or fails to reflect switching costs and adviser fees

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Explicit confirmation of which five existing arrangements transfer to Aviva and which are retained, Reconciliation of proposed Aviva charges (0.44% stated as 0.21% + 0.22%) to the existing Aviva charge (0.50% per provider documentation on page 5), Total transfer value or fund value basis used to calculate the £264 monetary saving, Calculation workings showing how -0.06%, -0.49%, and +0.26% sum to -0.24%, Clarification of whether EA3 (Aviva £3,744.36) transfers to Aviva Platform or is retained, Disclosure of which two of the five existing arrangements are excluded from the 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' table and why, Total existing charges (percentage and monetary) across all five arrangements for comparison purposes, Advisor fee impact (3% initial fee disclosed separately on page 23 as £3,305.55) included or excluded from the cost comparison, Explanation of the critical yield (6.3%) and projected value reduction (£3,000 at age 75) in relation to the claimed 0.24% saving

    The comparison cannot reliably be performed because the report fails to disclose the foundational basis on which cost figures are calculated. The 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' table addresses only three arrangements (EA3, EA4, EA5) without stating that two arrangements are being retained and one existing Aviva holding (EA3) is being consolidated into the proposed Aviva Platform. The arithmetic is internally inconsistent: three per-arrangement impacts of -0.06%, -0.49%, and +0.26% do not aggregate to the stated -0.24% overall saving (they total -0.29%). The monetary calculation of £264 cannot be verified because no single plan value corresponds to the £484.81 annual charge at 0.44%; the charge must be applied to a specific subset or total, which is not disclosed. The report's own provider documentation (page 5) shows Aviva's existing charge as 0.50%, yet the proposed Aviva Platform is stated as 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management)—this 0.06 percentage-point saving for Aviva aligns with the EA3 impact but suggests EA3 transfers to Aviva Platform, which is not explicit. More critically, the supporting evidence (P13) shows a critical yield of 6.3% and a projected value loss of £3,000 (2.1%) at age 75 for the new solution versus existing, which materially contradicts the premise that the recommended plan is cheaper in a commercially meaningful sense. The report excludes initial adviser fees (3% or £3,305.55) from this 'ongoing charges' comparison, which is a legitimate basis choice, but the comparison does not disclose that choice. The report also does not aggregate the impact of the Zurich facility fee of £2.12/month (approx £25.44 pa) or the People's Pension admin fee of £4.50 pa into the comparison. No evidence corroborates the specific figures for EA4 (0.93%, £961.98) or EA5 (0.18%, £186.19) against provider illustrations; Zurich's documented charge is 0.18% fund only, yet Zurich Policy Info (P2) also references ¾% annual management charge, creating ambiguity. The comparison is presented as 'like-for-like' on 'annual recurring charges' but the scope (which arrangements, which charge layers, adviser fees in or out) is neither fully disclosed nor arithmetically verifiable from the evidence.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension, meaning more of your savings remain invested for growth rather than being lost to charges.' [P1, page 7]. It states benefits including lower costs, lifestyling risk management, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset diversification, and enhanced retirement flexibility. On page 13, it claims 'the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility.' The report recommends a 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (0.18% annual charge) and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (0.29% annual charge) [P6]. The solution ongoing charge is stated as 0.44% total [implied from extraction, though actual recommendation page 6 references charges but does not show explicit all-in figure on recommendation page]. The report asserts that based on past performance, the recommended funds will achieve outperformance at a critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 [canonical model reference, page 11 claim 'I do believe that this is achievable' based on past performance].

    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Existing Aviva Pension - My Future Focus Consolidation charge at 0.50% [P1, table]. (2) Recommended HSBC fund at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29% [P6, fund recommendation table]. (3) Performance comparison [P1, P2]: Zurich Managed Pn AP 5-year cumulative 44.32% with volatility 7.89%; Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 5-year 42.40% with volatility 7.56%; Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation 5-year 15.58% with volatility 4.15%. [P2] states: 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.' [P2 also notes] 'your Zurich investments are allocated to higher-risk funds, which are more susceptible to market volatility, resulting in greater fluctuations in value. In contrast, the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility.' (4) The arithmetic from the extraction shows platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% and investment management £253.43 at 0.22%, totalling 0.44%, but these percentages imply a fund value of £110,185.71, which does not match the existing arrangement total of £116,998.47 or any single plan value. (5) [P6] fund recommendation shows combined charges but does not show explicit comparison of total outgoing cost before versus after. (6) Evidence of lifestyling risk: [P8] table shows Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation 'Invested in lifestyle strategy: Yes'. (7) No explicit aggregated all-in cost table comparing existing total annual charges to recommended total annual charges is present in the passages. (8) The report states on page 11 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' but provides no forward-looking projections or critical yield calculation shown in the evidence passages provided.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'lower overall cost structure' but existing Aviva fund (0.50%) is being replaced by funds at 0.18% and 0.29% (averaging 0.235%), which is lower for Aviva but the comparison ignores that Zurich existing holding (0.93% per arithmetic derivation EA4) is significantly higher; however, the client is moving away from Zurich (higher cost) so this is a cost saving, not a contradiction.

      - Report asserts 'Aviva platform funds...have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility' [P2], but evidence also shows 'they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' [P2], creating ambiguity about whether 'comparable' means 'comparable to benchmarks' or 'comparable to existing Zurich holdings'; Zurich 5-year 44.32% vs Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 42.40% shows underperformance, not comparable performance.

      - Report grounds the recommendation in 'past performance' and states outperformance 'is achievable' to critical yield 4.8% per year, but no forward-looking projections, critical yield calculation, or cumulative cost illustrations are provided in evidence to substantiate this claim.

      - No aggregated total annual charges table is shown comparing existing arrangements (sum across all 5 plans) to the recommended solution; charges are stated separately by fund but not rolled up into a single comparable figure for the client's decision-making.

      - The fund values used to calculate charges (0.21% platform = £110,185.71; 0.22% investment mgmt = £115,195.45) do not reconcile with existing arrangement total (£116,998.47), creating arithmetic ambiguity about whether the cost percentages are applied to the correct base.

      - Report asserts benefits of 'adviser charging facilitation' and 'ongoing monitoring' but no evidence of a defined service proposition, fee agreement, or monitoring schedule is cited or quoted in the passages to substantiate what these benefits entail or their cost.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Forward-looking projected values or critical yield calculations with full cost impact shown over the client's accumulation horizon to age 75, as referenced but not evidenced., Aggregated all-in annual cost comparison table showing total existing charges (across all 5 arrangements) versus total recommended charges, summed and expressed as a cumulative impact., Client agreement or fee schedule documenting the adviser charging facilitation benefit and the ongoing monitoring service, with fees and frequency of service., Explicit statement of the fund value base to which platform (0.21%) and investment management (0.22%) charges are applied, to reconcile with the sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47) and verify arithmetic., Basis for the critical yield assumption of 4.8% per year, including any stress-testing or sensitivity analysis supporting its achievability.

    The report makes two classes of claims that require scrutiny: (1) Cost claims: 'Lower overall cost structure' is supported in part — the recommended Aviva funds (0.18% + 0.29% = 0.47% combined, or 0.44% platform + investment management) are cheaper than the existing Aviva fund (0.50%), but this is a comparison within Aviva only. The Zurich fund (implied 0.93% from arithmetic) is higher-cost and being switched out, so overall the recommendation does reduce costs for at least part of the portfolio. However, no single aggregated table showing the client what their total current costs are and what their total future costs will be is presented in the evidence. This is a 'High' severity issue under the decision logic (no aggregated all-in cost). (2) Performance and benefit claims: The report states the recommended funds 'have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility' [P2], but the evidence contradicts this for Zurich comparison ('have not outperformed your current Zurich funds'). The claim that outperformance 'is achievable' to 4.8% critical yield is stated on page 11 as 'I do believe that this is achievable' based on 'past performance', but no forward projections, cumulative cost illustrations, or critical yield workings are provided in the evidence pack to support this claim. Under the guard 'An assertion that required outperformance is achievable is a claim, not an analysis; require supporting performance data before accepting it', the assertion lacks supporting analytical evidence. (3) Benefit justifications (adviser charging, ongoing monitoring, investment choice, lifestyling risk management): These are described but not costed or evidenced against a service proposition or fee schedule. The lifestyling benefit is real (existing fund lifestyles; recommended funds do not, per [P8] and [P6] fund table showing static allocation), but the cost of managing this risk (ongoing adviser monitoring) is not quantified or evidenced. (4) Arithmetic concern: The platform and investment management charges imply a fund value of £110,185, but this does not match the total transfer value of £116,998.47, suggesting either rounding, a cash reserve, or a calculation mismatch that is not explained in the suitability report passages. This affects the ability to verify whether all material costs are disclosed. Conclusion: Material evidence is absent. The report discloses fund charges and benefit statements but does not show an aggregated cost comparison, forward-looking impact analysis, or service proposition for the benefits claimed. This is a 'Potential Concern' under the decision logic because 'a cost layer is silently omitted, no aggregated total is given' and 'a cost increase is unexplained' (even though the direction is downward, the lack of a total is the issue, not the direction).

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts two competing cost positions: (1) the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans when including adviser fees (initial and ongoing), and (2) the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans when considering annual recurring charges alone. The report states 'As we have agreed to be remunerated via the policy (initial and ongoing), our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' and separately 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans'.

    File:     No independent corroborating passages from supporting documents were provided for this comparison. The requirement is assessed as an internal consistency test within the suitability report itself, comparing the stated cost figures and the justification provided for any cost increase.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report presents contradictory cost comparisons: a 4.24% cost increase (including adviser fees) versus a 0.24% cost reduction (recurring charges only). These cannot both be true in the same comparison framework.

      - The 4.24% figure is stated to include 'initial and ongoing' adviser fees, but the basis for calculating this figure against 'current plans' is not explained — specifically, whether current plans included adviser fees and at what rate.

      - The report does not reconcile or explain why the same recommendation would be 4.24% more expensive when adviser fees are included but 0.24% less expensive when they are excluded, nor does it clarify which comparison is relevant to the suitability assessment.

      - Page 11 states the plan is 0.06% more expensive in one context (Pension Switch Charges table) but the narrative text claims it is 0.24% less expensive, creating an internal contradiction within the charges section itself.

    The report presents two cost metrics that are arithmetically and logically inconsistent. The 4.24% figure is stated to include adviser remuneration (initial and ongoing), while the 0.24% figure represents recurring charges only. However, the report does not explain: (1) what charges are included in the 'current plans' baseline for comparison (do they include adviser fees?), (2) whether the adviser fees for the recommended plan are additional to or replacing existing fees, or (3) how to reconcile these two figures in assessing whether costs are proportionate to benefits. The canonical model shows that the 4.24% figure implies a fund value of £110,188.68, which does not match any individual arrangement value (existing total is £116,998.47) or the solution total, suggesting a calculation that may not be arithmetically grounded in the plan values stated elsewhere. The report asserts benefits ('consolidation', 'lower platform', 'flexibility') but does not quantify them or demonstrate that they justify the 4.24% additional cost when adviser fees are included. The 0.24% saving claim is contradicted by the same page's Pension Switch Charges table showing different figures. The requirement to assess whether costs are excessive relative to benefits cannot be completed because: (a) the two cost baselines are not reconciled, (b) the benefits are stated qualitatively but not quantified, and (c) no comparison of value per pound spent is provided. This is a material clarity gap on a core suitability driver.

      · Suitability Report, Page 11

P18 shows alternatives were considered: Scottish Widows discounted 'as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing'; Aviva's only available mixed asset fund 'does not meet the clients ATR'; Zurich's blend of risk 7 and risk 2 funds 'have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally' and was rejected because 'Wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types'; Standard Life funds 'within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'. P8 and P10 state 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' for Aviva and Standard Life respectively. However, no explicit comparative cost table, cost per alternative, or quantified cost comparison (in percentage or monetary terms) for the alternatives appears in the passages.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts in P7, P10, P11 that alternatives exist and lower costs are cited as a reason, but no comparative cost figures are provided for any alternative to support this assertion.

      - P18 mentions Standard Life funds are 'more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' but provides no quantified comparison of those costs.

      - The report claims in P8 that the new solution (Aviva Platform 0.44%) offers lower costs, but does not provide a cost figure for the existing schemes to enable verification of the claim.

      - The decision logic explicitly requires 'a cost comparison' but none is present in either the canonical model or the passages for alternatives considered.

    The report states that alternatives were considered and rejected, and it asserts that the recommended solution offers 'lower costs' compared to existing pensions. However, the canonical model contains no extracted comparative cost data for alternatives, and the retrieved passages do not supply quantified cost comparisons between the recommended solution and the rejected alternatives. P18 provides qualitative reasons for rejection (fund range mismatch, lack of servicing, performance), but does not supply the cost figures that would allow verification of the cost claim. P8 shows the recommended Aviva Platform's total charge (0.44%), but no corresponding cost figures for the alternatives (Scottish Widows, Aviva internal switch, Zurich internal switch, or Standard Life) are presented. The guard states 'A cheaper option rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons (fund range, access, servicing) is a valid outcome.' The evidence does support rejection on non-cost grounds, but because no comparative cost evidence is present, the report's claim that the recommendation is cheaper cannot be verified. The comparison required by the requirement—whether alternatives provide better cost or benefit—cannot be performed from the available evidence. The missing input is the comparative cost data itself.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report states an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1% per annum. The services included in this charge are: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure investment portfolio remains suitable; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs; and (6) Continuous risk review in line with ongoing review meetings. The report states on page 17 'The costs for this service are 1% per annum' and describes the service as including annual review meetings with recommendations. Review frequency is stated as annually.

    File:     The client agreement [P1] states 'ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested. The amount you pay will fluctuate with the value of your investment.' The client agreement [P8] describes the on-going advice and review service to include: 'Review meeting at least once a year and recommendations which may include the following as part of this service; bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk.' Also listed are: 'Statements and Performance Reports twice per year', 'Cash flow analysis when relevant and beneficial', 'Family benefit where you can have an adviser fee free advice for direct family members for their ISA or pension set up on top of up to three hours advice for your children', 'Regular market updates', 'Quarterly e-magazine', 'On-line access for portfolio valuations', and 'Access to your adviser should you need us in the meantime.' The client agreement [P8] states the charge is 1% per annum 'with a minimum fee of £100 per annum'.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report lists six services for the ongoing charge but does not mention statements and performance reports (twice per year), cash flow analysis, family benefit for direct family members, quarterly e-magazine, or online access for portfolio valuations, all of which appear in the client agreement [P8].

      - The client agreement [P8] specifies 'Statements and Performance Reports twice per year' as part of the service, but the report does not mention reporting frequency or statement provision.

      - The client agreement [P8] includes 'Family benefit where you can have an adviser fee free advice for direct family members' as part of the ongoing service, but the report makes no mention of this benefit.

      - The client agreement [P8] states 'Quarterly e-magazine' is included, but the report does not reference this.

      - The client agreement [P8] includes 'On-line access for portfolio valuations' but the report does not list this.

      - The client agreement [P8] specifies a minimum fee of £100 per annum, but the report states only '1% per annum' without mentioning the minimum floor.

    The comparison can be performed because both the report and the client agreement address the ongoing service proposition and its cost. The suitability report on page 17 describes six service elements attached to the 1% annual charge. The client agreement [P1] and [P8] also describe the 1% charge (with a £100 minimum) and set out the services included. However, there is a material incompleteness in the report's disclosure. The client agreement lists additional services that form part of the same 1% ongoing fee but are not mentioned in the report: twice-yearly statements and performance reports, cash flow analysis when relevant, family benefit for fee-free advice for direct family members, quarterly e-magazine, and online access for portfolio valuations. These are not services the client declined; they are disclosed in the client agreement as part of what the 1% buys. The report's silence on these elements means it does not fully disclose what the client receives for the ongoing charge. Furthermore, the report does not mention the £100 minimum fee specified in [P1] and [P8]. This is a material omission because it affects what the client will pay in years where 1% of the fund value falls below £100. The services listed in the report do overlap substantially with those in the client agreement (risk review, portfolio suitability review, market updates, access to adviser, holistic planning), suggesting they are the same charging arrangement. However, the report's enumeration is incomplete and does not paint the full picture of what the client agreement commits to deliver. COBS 6.1ZA and COBS 9.2.2R require that the ongoing service be clearly described. A material charge layer of a service proposition left undisclosed in the report—even though it is present in the supporting agreement—represents a potential concern in consistency and completeness.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states seven benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension; (2) Lifestyling funds automatically reduce risk as approaching retirement, moving portfolio outside agreed risk range; (3) Greater investment choice allowing tailored strategy suited to risk profile; (4) Adviser charging facilitation allowing direct deduction of charges; (5) Ongoing monitoring and adjustment of investments; (6) Multi-asset fund selection providing professionally managed diversified solution; (7) Enhanced retirement flexibility providing flexible retirement options. It also states three cost justifications: (1) Based on past performance, the plan is believed achievable to outperform by critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75; (2) Recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings; (3) Aviva platform funds align with agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility.

    File:     The evidence supports most benefits stated. P4 and P8 confirm enhanced retirement flexibility with flexi-access drawdown, UFPLS, and annuity options available on Aviva. P5 confirms these retirement options are available. P12 confirms Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice product exists. P22 confirms the multi-asset fund recommendation with HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced (50%), both risk rated 5. P21 confirms greater investment choice is available through Aviva. P10 provides fund cost data showing HSBC at 0.19% total and CT Universal MAP at 0.55% total. However, P19 and P20 show the existing Zurich plan offers 60+ funds available, which demonstrates significant choice already exists. The 'Lower Costs' benefit is problematic: P22 shows recommended funds at 0.18% and 0.29% annual charges, but the report states on page 6 (per extraction) that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', contradicting the claimed cost saving. The extraction notes show Aviva existing charge at 0.50% versus new Aviva charge at 0.44%, implying a 0.06% saving, but this contradicts the 'lower costs' narrative when applied to all five consolidating pensions. No evidence supports the claim that lifestyling funds move the portfolio outside the agreed risk range or that this justifies switching. P22 states recommended funds are 'risk-profiled solutions, meaning they are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk'. P2 shows the client's agreed risk level is 5. Ongoing monitoring is stated but no service proposition or evidence of what this entails beyond standard advice is provided. Performance comparisons lack supporting data in the evidence provided.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension' but the extraction notes indicate the Aviva recommendation is 0.06% more expensive on the Zurich comparison, and no aggregated cost comparison across all five existing plans versus the recommendation is presented in the evidence.

      - Report states lifestyling funds 'automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement' and 'your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range', but P22 describes the recommended funds as 'specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk', indicating the new funds maintain rather than abandon the risk profile.

      - Report emphasizes 'Greater Investment Choice' as a benefit, but P19 shows Zurich already offers 60+ funds available, Scottish Widows offers 29, and Standard Life offers 300, suggesting the existing arrangements already provide substantial choice, not new choice gained through consolidation.

      - Report asserts 'Recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings' but no historical performance data, comparative performance tables, or evidence supporting this claim is present in the case file.

      - Report asserts the plan will 'outperform by critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75' based on past performance, but no critical yield analysis, past performance data, or projections supporting this figure appear in the evidence provided.

    The requirement tests whether higher-cost recommendations are justified by stated benefits supported by evidence. The report identifies seven benefits and three cost justifications. The principal issue is the 'Lower Costs' benefit claim. The extraction identifies a discrepancy in which the report text contradicts its own calculations: page 6 states the Aviva plan is 0.06% more expensive, yet the benefit summary claims 'lower overall cost structure'. P10 provides MiFID II cost disclosures showing fund costs of 0.19% (HSBC) and 0.55% (CT Universal MAP), aggregating to 0.37%, but the report's page 12 recommendation states 0.18% and 0.29%, creating arithmetic inconsistency. No evidence shows cumulative costs across all five existing plans versus the consolidated recommendation. The lifestyling claim is directly contradicted: the report states existing lifestyling funds move the portfolio outside the risk range, but the recommended funds are described in P22 as designed to stay within the agreed risk range, making this a false benefit attribution. The 'greater investment choice' benefit is weakened by evidence that existing plans already offer 29 to 300+ funds; consolidation is a convenience benefit, not a new-choice benefit, per the guard. Ongoing monitoring and adviser charging facilitation are stated but unsupported by evidence of the service proposition or evidence that the existing arrangements lacked these features. Performance comparisons lack evidentiary support; no historical returns, peer comparisons, or fund performance data corroborating the 'demonstrated stronger performance' or '4.8% critical yield' claims are provided. The advisor charging facilitation claim is generic platform functionality with no evidence it justifies incremental cost. The multi-asset and retirement flexibility benefits are genuine and supported. However, the guard 'a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify the extra cost' applies to retirement flexibility (existing plans offer FAD, UFPLS, and annuity options per P5, P19) and choice (P19 shows existing plans offer many funds). The cost justification rests on performance claims unsupported by the case file evidence.
