  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans (on an annual recurring charges basis), and that including adviser fees, it is 4.24% more expensive, requiring 4.8% annual growth to match the existing plan value. Projected values at age 75 under mid-growth (2.95%) are: existing pension £127,000; new solution £115,000 (a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease). RIY ranges from 2.1% to 2.3%, and critical yields from 1.9% to 7.8%.

    File:     The pension switch report [P10] corroborates the mid-growth projection figures: existing pension £127,000, new solution £115,000, effect of switching £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. It shows RIY of 2.2% and critical yield of 4.8% [P10]. The report also states on page 11 [P3]: 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' including adviser fees. However, arithmetic checks reveal the 0.24% saving figure does not reconcile: the fund value implied by the 0.44% total ongoing charge (£110,184.09) does not match any stated arrangement value. The existing arrangements total £116,998.47, not £110,184.09.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims a 0.24% annual cost saving (recurring charges only) but the charge arithmetic does not support this: the recommended 0.44% ongoing charge applied to the total existing value of £116,998.47 would be £514.40 annually, not the implied base case. The comparison basis (fund value of £110,184.09) differs from the actual transfer value.

      - The report states the new solution is 4.24% more expensive 'including adviser fees' but the arithmetic basis is unclear: the required outperformance of £4,672 at 4.24% implies a fund value of £110,188.68, again not matching the stated arrangement totals.

      - The projected values show the new solution underperforming at all three growth rates (low: £97,600 vs £108,000; mid: £115,000 vs £127,000; high: £135,000 vs £150,000), yet the report frames this as a cost-justified trade-off without explicitly stating the client will receive materially worse outcomes in absolute terms.

      - RIY figures in the canonical model (2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3%) match [P10], but the report presents these without clear explanation of why a 2.1% to 2.3% reduction in yield is justified when the existing pension shows only 0.3% RIY.

      - Critical yield of 4.8% stated on page 11 [P16] represents a significant hurdle — the fund must outperform by 4.24% annually just to match existing outcomes, far exceeding the 'about 0.5% a year' noise threshold.

    The report makes contradictory claims about cost comparison. First, it asserts the new solution is 0.24% cheaper on recurring charges; second, it states it is 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees. The evidence [P10] and [P16] support the latter assertion clearly: the critical yield of 4.8% and required outperformance of £4,672 in the first year demonstrate material cost loading. However, the 0.24% 'saving' claim cannot be verified against the actual fund values. The charge figures are based on a fund value of £110,184.09, which is £6,813.47 less than the actual combined transfer value of £116,998.47. This discrepancy explains the apparent cost advantage — it is computed on a lower base. When normalized to the actual transfer value, the new solution's charges would be materially higher, not lower. The projections consistently show the new solution underperforming: at mid-growth, £12,000 less at retirement (9.4% reduction). This is not compensated by the analysis. The RIY of 2.1%–2.3% versus existing 0.3% is a material cost increase not explicitly justified by better fund quality, lower platform costs, or consolidation benefits. The guard permitting cost increases 'buying materially better fund ranges or guarantees' is not evidenced: both the existing and new solutions use balanced multi-asset funds, and no guarantee differential is cited. The required outperformance of 4.24% per annum far exceeds the noise threshold and is inadequately explained to the client. No analysis demonstrates the client will receive better absolute value despite materially higher costs and lower projected outcomes.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report quantifies critical yield as 4.8% but does not explain what this means for the client's retirement outcomes if growth falls short—the supporting evidence shows projections at different growth rates (P2) but the report does not link these scenarios to the client's specific situation or what shortfall would mean in monetary terms at retirement. The report states the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive but does not clearly present this in the context of the pension switch report's 'Effect of switching' analysis which shows specific monetary decreases (£10 decrease in high growth scenario at P2); the report's £4,672 first-year figure is not reconciled to the underlying transfer values. The report discloses the lifestyling drift risk but does not explain whether the recommended fund allocation (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) avoids this risk or how the client will manage rebalancing as retirement approaches. 13 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states three material disadvantages: (1) Critical Yield Requirement - the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 to meet the same value as the current plan, based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94% including advice fees; (2) Adviser Charge Impact - the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring outperformance of £4,672 in the first year; (3) Lifestyling Drift - current funds are in lifestyling funds that automatically reduce risk as client approaches retirement, causing portfolio to move outside agreed risk range of 5. The report also discloses three limitations: withdrawal restrictions on the Aviva plan limiting flexible access; fund range constraint at Scottish Widows due to lack of single multi-asset fund matching risk rating 5; and a generic statement that all other risks are detailed in product literature. The report states there is no guarantee the new investment will outperform existing arrangements.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1, P2) provides detailed comparisons showing 'Effect of switching' metrics and 'Critical yield' calculations across different growth rate scenarios. The key features document (P4) discloses multiple risks: investment value may go down as well as up; you could get back less than invested; value depends on payments, investment performance, and charges; income drawdown may deplete pension fund if withdrawals are high; if you withdraw high income levels, less money remains for dependants or annuities. Terms and conditions (P6, P8) detail adviser charges, fund manager charges, DIM charges, and disinvestment procedures with additional costs (greater of £10 or 10% of disinvestment). The report itself (P15) states 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.' The performance analysis (P20) shows historical performance but notes 'Past performance is no guarantee of future returns.' The fact find (P3) records client as age 70 with monthly income shortfall of £288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report quantifies critical yield as 4.8% but does not explain what this means for the client's retirement outcomes if growth falls short—the supporting evidence shows projections at different growth rates (P2) but the report does not link these scenarios to the client's specific situation or what shortfall would mean in monetary terms at retirement.

      - The report states the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive but does not clearly present this in the context of the pension switch report's 'Effect of switching' analysis which shows specific monetary decreases (£10 decrease in high growth scenario at P2); the report's £4,672 first-year figure is not reconciled to the underlying transfer values.

      - The report discloses the lifestyling drift risk but does not explain whether the recommended fund allocation (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) avoids this risk or how the client will manage rebalancing as retirement approaches.

      - The report references product literature for 'all other risks' but does not specifically identify which material risks from the key features (e.g., risk of capital loss, income drawdown depletion risk, disinvestment costs) are material to this client's circumstances.

      - The client's financial position shows a monthly shortfall of £288 (P24) but the report does not explain how pension withdrawal will interact with this negative cash flow or whether the recommended solution adequately addresses this constraint.

      - The report states withdrawal restrictions as a limitation but does not explain what flexible access options the client is giving up from existing arrangements (the extraction notes expectedButAbsent for 'featuresOrGuaranteesLost' path), making it unclear what benefits are being surrendered.

    The report identifies material disadvantages and discloses risks, but several critical gaps and inconsistencies emerge when compared to the supporting evidence. The critical yield requirement is stated but not translated into a meaningful scenario for this client—the pension switch report shows effects of switching under different growth rates, but the report does not explain what happens if actual growth is below 4.8%, or what this means in pounds at retirement. The adviser charge impact (4.24% more expensive) is disclosed, but the calculation and its basis are not clearly explained in relation to transfer values or the effect of switching quantified in the supporting documents. The lifestyling drift risk is identified as a disadvantage of the current arrangement but not clearly resolved by the recommendation—no evidence is provided that the recommended static allocation avoids this risk or that the client understands the need for active management. The report relies heavily on product literature for risk disclosure, which is appropriate for generic investment risks, but given the client's specific circumstances (age 70, negative monthly cash flow of £288, multiple pension transfers), the report does not adequately translate product-level risks into client-specific harms. The key features document (P4) identifies material risks including capital loss, income drawdown depletion, and disinvestment costs—these are not specifically addressed in the suitability report. The financial constraint (negative cash flow) is recorded in the fact find but not discussed in relation to the pension recommendation, creating a potential inconsistency about affordability and suitability. The features or guarantees lost through consolidation are not detailed, contrary to the extraction's flag that this information is absent from the report.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report discloses the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) AccessDelay risk (10-15 working days for fund access); (2) MarketTimingRisk (stock market movements during switching period); (3) PerformanceVariability (recommended Aviva funds stronger than existing Aviva but not outperforming Zurich funds, which are higher-risk and more volatile); (4) NoPerformanceGuarantee and NoOutperformanceGuarantee (no guarantee new provider funds will outperform existing); (5) InvestmentRisk (unit prices can fall as well as rise, no guarantee of return of original capital, especially in early years or if taking income). These risks are stated on pages 8, 12, and 13 of the suitability report under sections 'Other Considerations of Switching' and 'Fund recommendation'.

    File:     Product literature on file includes KIIDs for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and Columbia Threadneedle Universal MAP funds (the two recommended funds at 50% each), Aviva Pension Portfolio Key Features, Zurich Policy Info, and Fund Factsheets. The HSBC KIID [P1] states the fund is rated 3 on a 1-5 risk scale and aims for growth over 5+ years with 45-70% equities, 20-55% bonds, 0-25% other assets. The HSBC KIID [P3] identifies material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, and liquidity risk. The HSBC Fund Factsheet [P5] states 'The value of investments and any income from them can go down as well as up and investors may not get back the amount originally invested' and identifies interest rate risk on bonds and currency exchange risk. The HSBC KIID [P9] recommends the fund may not be appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years. The Aviva Key Features [P7] explain pension options including income drawdown risks, stating 'Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund.' Zurich Policy Info [P16] and [P17] detail generic risks including fund volatility, derivative risk, OEIC structure risk, inflation risk on fixed-income, interest rate risk, and specific risks for property funds, high yield bonds, smaller company shares, and emerging markets. Risk profile research [P15] documents the client as a Balanced investor.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report does not mention currency risk or currency exchange risk, which is identified in both the HSBC KIID [P1] and Fund Factsheet [P5] as a material risk applicable to the recommended Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio ('Where overseas investments are held the rate of currency exchange may cause the value of such investments to go down as well as up' [P5]).

      - The report does not disclose interest rate risk on fixed-income holdings, which is identified in HSBC Fund Factsheet [P5] ('The Fund invests in bonds whose value generally falls when interest rates rise') and Zurich Policy Info [P17] as a generic fund risk.

      - The report does not mention counterparty risk, credit risk, or default risk on bonds, which are all listed as material risks in the HSBC KIID [P3] ('Counterparty Risk The possibility that the counterparty to a transaction may be unwilling or unable to meet its obligations. Credit Risk A bond or money market security could lose value if the issuer's financial health deteriorates. Default Risk The issuers of certain bonds could become unwilling or unable to make payments on their bonds.').

      - The report does not mention derivatives risk, which is identified in the HSBC KIID [P3] ('Derivatives Risk Derivatives can behave unexpectedly. The pricing and volatility of many derivatives may diverge from strictly reflecting the pricing or volatility of their underlying reference(s), instrument or asset.') and in Zurich Policy Info [P17].

      - The report does not disclose liquidity risk, which is identified in HSBC KIID [P3] as a material risk ('Liquidity Risk: the ability to buy and sell assets at a favourable price may be affected by a low level of counterparties willing to enter into a transaction with the Fund.').

      - The report does not mention the 5-year investment horizon recommendation. The HSBC KIID [P1] states 'The Fund aims to provide growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of 5 years or more' and the KIID [P9] explicitly recommends 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years'. This is material given the client's circumstances and withdrawal patterns are not addressed.

      - The report discloses generic income drawdown risks from the Aviva literature but does not integrate these with the specific client recommendation, which involves consolidating multiple pensions and potentially taking income.

    The comparison can be performed because the case file contains comprehensive product literature (KIIDs, Key Features, Factsheets, and Policy Info) for the recommended funds and existing providers. The report makes specific risk disclosures but the comparison against product literature reveals material gaps. The report states general investment risks (unit prices falling, no guarantee of capital return) which are consistent with product literature wording. However, the report omits several material product-specific and investment-specific risks that are explicitly identified in the fund literature on file. Currency risk, interest rate risk, counterparty and credit risk, derivatives risk, and liquidity risk are all identified in the recommended fund literature (HSBC KIID and Factsheet) but are not mentioned anywhere in the suitability report. The HSBC fund is structured as an OEIC investing globally with a range of asset classes including bonds and derivatives; these features generate the identified risks. The omission of currency risk is particularly material given the fund is Global Strategy Balanced with overseas holdings. The omission of interest rate risk is material given the fund holds 20-55% bonds. The 5-year recommendation horizon in the HSBC KIID is not addressed in the report's discussion of the client's suitability, despite the report not clearly establishing the client's investment horizon. The report's risk disclosure appears to rely on generic boilerplate (no guarantee of return, units can fall) and discussion of the switching process (market timing risk, access delay) rather than product-specific risks. Income drawdown risks are mentioned generically in Aviva literature but not integrated with the client's specific circumstances. These omissions are substantive and not explained by any guard. The false-positive guard about general wording does not apply here—the issue is not that general wording is insufficient, but that material product-specific risks identified in the fund literature are entirely absent from the report.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states five tax-related consequences: (1) a caveat that tax statements are based on current HMRC understanding and subject to change [P7]; (2) that the client understands pension freedoms and prefers Flexi Access Drawdown flexibility [P6]; (3) death benefit taxation rules—tax-free if death before age 75, taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate if death after 75 [P6]; (4) tax relief on personal contributions at 25% uplift (£80 net becomes £100 gross) [P6]; and (5) specific application to the Scottish Widows small pot encashment: 25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170 from £200 withdrawal [P10].

    File:     The supporting evidence confirms these points with near-identical language. The Key Features document [P2] and [P4] state: 'You can normally withdraw up to 25% of your pension fund as a tax-free lump sum' and 'income tax is payable on benefits taken above' lump sum allowances. Death benefits are described identically: tax-free before age 75, taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate after 75 [P4]. Tax relief mechanics are confirmed in the report itself [P6] discussing flexi-access drawdown. The small pots rule explanation in [P10] matches the Key Features caveat that 'the tax you pay will depend on your personal circumstances.' The research documentation [P8] shows the recommended solution but contains no specific tax consequence statements. The terms and conditions [P1], [P12] address charges and fund management but not tax consequences of withdrawal.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report does not disclose the client's marginal tax rate (£300 per week net income as HGV driver, plus £20 volunteer income, spouse £1,100 per month net). Without this, the statement that tax on small pot encashment and drawdown income is 'at your marginal tax rate' cannot be verified as material or accurately applied to this client's circumstances. This is a generic tax principle, not a client-specific consequence.

      - The report estimates 'net receipt of £170' from a £200 small pot withdrawal but provides no working or assumption about the client's tax band. If the client is a basic rate taxpayer (20%), 75% of £200 = £150, less 20% tax = £120 net, plus 25% tax-free = £50, totalling £170—this matches. However, the report does not state the client's assumed tax rate, making verification impossible from the pack alone.

      - The report does not address whether the encashment of Scottish Widows (£211.32) triggers any tax consequences beyond the small pots rule application. No mention of whether there are any transfer penalties or tax costs from the four pension switches (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, and retaining People's Pension).

      - The report emphasises Flexi Access Drawdown and its 'flexibility,' stating tax consequences of withdrawal, but does not quantify what tax the client will pay in retirement on regular drawdown income. No projection of marginal tax rate in retirement is provided, nor any illustration of tax payable on say £500, £1,000, or £1,700 monthly drawdown against the client's projected income (State Pension £230 per week = £998/month gross, spouse £998/month, HGV income ceases, leaving uncertain pension drawdown need).

    The report states generic tax principles (25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate, death benefits rules) and these are corroborated verbatim by Key Features and T&Cs. However, COBS 2.1.1R and 4.2.1R require material information to be fair, clear and accurate in context—and a 'tax consequence' stated without application to this client's personal tax position is a generic disclosure, not a consequence. The guard permits this: 'A tax consequence stated without the client's tax band established (see CHK-001 G1.2) is unverifiable, not necessarily wrong.' The client's tax band is not established in the pack. The £170 net estimate for the small pot encashment appears arithmetically sound if basic rate (20%) is assumed, but this assumption is not stated. The report does not explain the tax cost of the recommended action (pension switches) itself—no transfer penalty tax, no encashment tax on Scottish Widows (£211.32 treated as small pot), and no illustration of the tax impact of moving into drawdown at retirement. The Flexi Access Drawdown section [P6] explains the mechanism (tax at marginal rate on withdrawals above 25% tax-free lump sum) but does not calculate or project the client's specific tax liability in retirement. This omission is material because the client's retirement objective requires £1,700 net monthly household income [P5], and the interplay between pension drawdown tax and this target is unexplained. Severity is Moderate to High because: (1) tax consequences of the recommendation (the switch itself and the encashment of Scottish Widows) are stated in generic terms without client-specific working; (2) the small pot estimate is provided but not justified; (3) retirement drawdown tax is described mechanically but not illustrated for this client's circumstances.

    ** 1 QUOTE(S) NOT FOUND IN THE EVIDENCE SUPPLIED **

        "If you die before you're 75, we'll normally pay any benefits tax free to your nominated beneficiaries, at the trustees' discretion (unless you have an Aviva Pension Trust, in which case the rules of that Trust will apply). If you die after the age of 75 we can pay the full value of your remaining pension fund to your nominated beneficiaries, at the discretion of the trustees. Any benefits will be taxed at your beneficiaries' marginal rate."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P6)

        "Flexi-access drawdown provides a flexible and tax-efficient means of drawing benefits from a pension fund. An individual can take up to 25% of the pension fund being crystallised as a tax-free lump sum at outset (subject to the Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275 for people with no form of lifetime allowance protection). The remainder of the fund remains invested within a tax advantaged environment from which the individual can take regular or ad-hoc income withdrawals to suit their needs and circumstances. There is no upper limit to the amount the individual can withdraw as an income. However, each withdrawal is taxed at their marginal rate of Income Tax."

      · Key Features (1).md [H] (P4)

        "If you die before you're 75, we'll normally pay any benefits tax free to your nominated beneficiaries, at the trustees' discretion (unless you have an Aviva Pension Trust, in which case the rules of that Trust will apply).   [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts one loss scenario illustrated: 'if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall' (page 15, Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests section).

    File:     The case file contains evidence of loss scenarios in the supporting documents. P22 states: 'I have completed a stress test based on if an immediate market crash were to occur in combination with the increased inflation level. This showed that if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used.' This is the same scenario. P3 contains a table with three growth rate scenarios (Low 0.00%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88%) showing projected values at age 75 for both existing pension and new solution, with effects of switching quantified. The table illustrates loss scenarios implicitly through the 'Effect of switching' rows which show reductions of £10,400-£15,000 (9.4%-10.0% decreases). P2 and P7 describe general pension risks including 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up' and 'You could get back less than has been invested.' P10 and P15 present illustration scenarios at different growth rates (Lower 1.9%, Medium 5%, Higher 8%) with corresponding pension fund values after inflation adjustment.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states only one loss scenario (-30% market crash with 6% inflation), but the supporting evidence (P3) contains multiple scenario projections at different growth rates (0%, 2.95%, 5.88%) that illustrate potential value outcomes. The report does not reference or integrate these tabular loss scenarios from P3 into its loss scenario disclosure.

      - The report's stress test scenario (-30% immediate market crash) describes a downside scenario but does not quantify the specific monetary impact on the client's projected fund value in the main report text, only in the cashflow assertion of 'no shortfall'.

      - The illustration documents (P10, P15) show three growth rate scenarios with corresponding fund values, but these are not explicitly framed as 'loss scenarios' in the report's narrative - they appear only as illustrative projections in appendices.

    The comparison can be made because the report explicitly states a loss scenario and the supporting evidence contains corroborating stress test documentation and illustrative scenarios. The report asserts that a -30% immediate market crash combined with 6% inflation results in 'no shortfall', which is directly supported by P22. However, there is a presentation discrepancy: the report isolates and highlights one stress test scenario in its Alternative Scenarios section, but does not integrate or reference the three-scenario projection table from P3 (Low/Mid/High growth rates showing fund value ranges and effects of switching). Under the guard that 'a stress test in a cashflow model is a loss scenario even if not labelled as one', the tabular scenarios in P3 constitute loss scenarios that are present in the file but not explicitly discussed as loss scenarios in the report's risk section. P2, P7, P10, and P15 contain generic risk warnings about downside potential ('may go down as well as up', 'You could get back less than has been invested') and illustrative scenarios at different growth rates. The report does identify and stress-test a material downside scenario (-30% loss), which satisfies the basic requirement for loss scenario illustration. However, the discrepancy between the single named stress test and the multiple scenario projections in the evidence suggests the report's loss scenario disclosure is selective rather than comprehensive. The scenarios in P3 (showing 9.4%-10% reduction in yield on switching) represent quantified loss outcomes relative to the existing arrangement that could be characterised as loss scenarios material to the switching recommendation but are not explicitly highlighted as such in the report's disadvantages section.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8 addresses disadvantages and risks. The report also references a Critical Yield analysis in the Appendix and includes loss scenarios in projection tables.

    File:     The evidence shows disadvantages and risks presented across multiple locations: (1) Page 8 contains 'Other Considerations of Switching' section discussing stock market movements during pending transactions; (2) Loss scenarios appear in detailed projection tables showing reductions of £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%), and £15,000 (10.0%) at different growth rates; (3) Critical Yield requirements of 1.9%, 4.8%, and 7.8% are stated; (4) A generic statement that 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement'; (5) Charge comparisons showing 0.50% existing vs 0.44% recommended (6% reduction); (6) Risk to death benefit of £3,305 immediately post-switch; (7) Discussion of lifestyling fund constraints moving outside agreed risk range.

    Discrepancies:

      - Charge characterisation inconsistency: Page 6 text states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the Pension Switch Charges table on page 4 shows Aviva existing 0.50% versus impact of -0.06%, implying new plan is 0.06% cheaper, not more expensive. The arithmetic extract notes this as 0.44% new vs 0.50% existing, a 0.06% improvement, contradicting the text claim of 0.08% worse.

      - Material disadvantage visibility: While loss scenarios (£10,400-£15,000 reductions) are presented in projection tables, the report's main body (page 6) leads prominently with 'Lower Costs' as a key reason to switch, placing the benefit claim ahead of quantified loss scenarios that appear only in tables and the Appendix. The prominence of the benefit relative to the magnitude of loss is imbalanced.

      - Incomplete tax consequence disclosure: No tax consequences of the pension switch are discussed in the evidence provided. The report does not address potential tax implications of transferring between pension providers, nor does it explain the tax treatment of any withdrawal or access scenarios.

      - Death benefit impact prominence: The death benefit shortfall of £3,305 immediately post-switch is disclosed only in technical appendix material (P15), not in the main body's risk or disadvantages section on page 8.

    The report asserts a dedicated section on page 8 titled 'Other Considerations of Switching.' Evidence confirms this section exists (P6), so the structural assertion is correct. However, content within that section and its prominence present concerns. (1) Charge claim contradiction: The assertion that the new plan is less expensive (a benefit) contains an internal contradiction with stated percentage figures. Page 6 claims it is '0.08% more expensive' yet tables show 0.06% improvement. This is a material error in presenting a key disadvantage/benefit claim. (2) Prominence imbalance: COBS 9.4.7R requires disadvantages to be 'fair' and 'prominent.' The report leads page 6 with 'Lower Costs' as the primary reason, yet loss scenarios showing 9.4-10% reductions in fund value are presented in tables rather than integrated into the main narrative. The guard permits disadvantages distributed throughout, provided they are 'clearly stated.' Here they are stated but subordinated to benefit claims on the same page. (3) Tax consequences: COBS 4.2.1R requires fair and clear explanation of material tax consequences. No tax implications are addressed in either the main report or evidence provided. For a pension switch involving transfers between schemes, tax treatment (particularly around advanced member income, death benefit tax, and any uplift rules) represents material information. Its complete absence is a gap, though pension-to-pension transfers are typically tax-neutral; absence of any such reassurance is itself notable. (4) Death benefit impact: A £3,305 immediate shortfall to the death benefit is a material disadvantage clearly linked to the advice and quantified. However, it appears only in Appendix technical detail (P15), not in the dedicated disadvantages section on page 8 (P6). This is a material disadvantage present in the file but absent from the prominent risk disclosure location. (5) Risk content distribution: Critical Yield figures (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%), loss scenarios, and reduction-in-yield data are all present and quantified. However, their location in projection tables and appendix rather than consolidated in the page 8 'Other Considerations' section means the client's first encounter with disadvantages is via benefits narrative, with risks distributed. The structure complies with the guard permitting distributed disadvantages, but the imbalance in narrative prominence is material.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report identifies one feature being given up: 'Employer contribution will be lost' and states 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The report also confirms that all five existing arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits. The report does not identify loss of life cover, waiver benefits, or other features across the existing pension arrangements.

    File:     Product literature reveals material features in existing arrangements that are being given up: (1) Zurich policy [P3] states 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans. You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' (2) Scottish Widows [P5] confirms the existing policy contains no life cover or waiver. (3) Aviva [P14] shows the existing plan has no life insurance. (4) Standard Life [P17] shows the existing plan facilitates drawdown but contains no safeguarded benefits. (5) Zurich product research [P1] and [P2] confirm no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pensions, no guaranteed growth rates, and no life cover on new plans. The Zurich policy literature [P3] explicitly warns that life cover and waiver benefits 'are no longer available on new pension plans' and requires medical underwriting if similar cover is sought separately. The retirement options across existing arrangements [P8] show various options available (flexi access drawdown, UFPLS, partial UFPLS, annuity in some cases) that may not all be available on the new solution.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report identifies employer contribution loss but does not identify loss of life cover and waiver benefits that existed on the Zurich arrangement and will not be available on the new plan

      - Report does not disclose the requirement for medical questionnaire to obtain replacement life cover and waiver benefits on a new plan

      - Report states all arrangements have no safeguarded benefits but does not address other material features being lost such as life cover and waiver protection on the Zurich plan

      - Report does not discuss potential loss or change of retirement access options (flexi access drawdown, UFPLS, annuity availability) between existing plans and new solution

    The report asserts only that employer contributions will be lost. However, the evidence clearly shows that the Zurich arrangement includes life cover and waiver benefits that are explicitly noted in product literature [P3] as 'no longer available on new pension plans.' This is a material disadvantage that the report has failed to disclose. The Zurich policy information [P3] specifically warns: 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans. You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' This is a clear, material benefit being given up. The report's statement that all arrangements 'do not contain any safeguarded benefits' is accurate but insufficient—safeguarded benefits are a narrow category; the report conflates absence of safeguarded benefits with absence of any material features. The obligation under COBS 9.4.7R and the regulatory framework requires fair, clear and accurate disclosure of 'all material disadvantages, risks, limitations...and consumer harms' including 'benefits, features or protections the client is giving up.' The loss of life cover and waiver protection, particularly where the client may have deteriorating health and replacement cover requires medical underwriting, is material and prominently material given the explicit warning in the product literature. The report's silence on this point is a significant gap. The employer contribution loss is mentioned, which is positive, but the omission of life and waiver benefits constitutes material non-disclosure of a documented disadvantage.

You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits."

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four main justifications for the recommendation despite increased costs or reduced outcomes: (1) 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' regarding a 4.8% critical yield outperformance; (2) 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings'; (3) 'the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility'; and (4) the ongoing services including risk review, suitability review, market updates, and access to advisers. The report also states 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (repeated three times). The projections show mid-growth at 2.95% yields £115,000 at age 75.

    File:     The pension switch report [P1] shows the new solution has a critical yield of 0.6% reduction in yield compared to 0.3% for existing, with projected value of £125,000 against £127,000 for existing at mid-growth (2.95%), representing a £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. At high growth (5.88%), the new solution projects £147,000 versus £150,000 existing, a £3,000 or 2.0% decrease. The pension switch report [P11] ranks solutions by maturity value at medium growth: Existing Solution ranked 2nd at £127,000, New Solution ranked 5th at £125,000. The SIPP research report [P12] shows combined fund performance of 5.52% (3M), 9.71% (6M), 10.54% (1YR), 34.59% (3YR), 42.40% (5YR) with no 10-year data. Individual fund performance [P12] shows CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc with costs breakdown: ongoing 0.29%, transaction 0.26%, total 0.55%; HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio with ongoing 0.18%, transaction 0.01%, total 0.19%. Combined fund costs [P14] total 0.37% ongoing. The suitability report [P19] states Aviva Platform charge 0.21% + investment management 0.22% = 0.44% total, versus existing Aviva at 0.50%. However, the pension switch report [P1] shows the new solution has reduction in yield of 0.6% versus existing 0.3%, meaning higher total cost drag. The research report [P18] notes Aviva existing scheme is 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' and Zurich is 'Expensive, no adviser servicing', indicating expense concerns. No independent verification is provided that recommended funds have 'demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings' or that they deliver 'comparable performance with reduced volatility' beyond the research document itself.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 4.8% critical yield is 'achievable' based on past performance, but pension switch evidence [P1] shows critical yield required is 0.6% for new solution versus 0.3% for existing, and at mid-growth the new solution underperforms by £2,000 (1.6% decrease)

      - Report states recommended Aviva funds 'have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings' but no comparative performance data between old and new Aviva holdings is provided in the evidence; research report [P12] shows combined fund performance but not a direct comparison to the existing Aviva arrangement

      - Report asserts funds deliver 'comparable performance with reduced volatility' but pension switch report [P1] shows identical reduction in yield (0.6%) for both scenarios tested, and at high growth the new solution underperforms by £3,000 (2.0% decrease), contradicting 'comparable' claim

      - Report projects £115,000 at mid-growth (2.95%) to age 75, but pension switch report [P1] projects £125,000 for new solution and £127,000 for existing at mid-growth, indicating discrepancy in projected values

      - Suitability report [P19] shows Aviva Platform 0.44% total charge, but pension switch report [P1] shows reduction in yield of 0.6%, which is higher than the platform charge alone, indicating additional cost drag from fund selection not fully explained

      - Report provides no disclosure that new solution is ranked 5th out of comparable options (behind Zurich ranked 1st at £128,000) according to pension switch report [P11] maturity value ranking

      - Report does not quantify or evidence the claimed 'ongoing review service' benefits against the documented reduced projected outcomes

    The report justifies increased costs and reduced outcomes primarily through three claims: (1) historical performance of recommended funds enabling a 4.8% critical yield, (2) stronger performance of new Aviva funds versus existing Aviva holdings, and (3) comparable performance with reduced volatility plus ongoing services. However, the supporting evidence reveals material inconsistencies. First, the pension switch report [P1] clearly shows the new solution underperforms the existing arrangement at all three growth scenarios: at low growth (0.00%), projected values are £100,000 new versus £102,000 existing (£2,000 decrease); at mid-growth (2.95%), £125,000 new versus £127,000 existing (£2,000 or 1.6% decrease); at high-growth (5.88%), £147,000 new versus £150,000 existing (£3,000 or 2.0% decrease). The critical yield required to match the existing solution is 0.6% for the new solution, double the 0.3% for the existing solution [P1]. This contradicts the claim of 'comparable' performance. Second, the research document [P12] provides no comparative analysis of the new Aviva funds against the old Aviva holdings; it shows only combined fund performance metrics and individual fund costs. The claim that funds 'have demonstrated stronger performance' is asserted but not evidenced by a side-by-side comparison in the file. Third, while the report states platform charges drop from 0.50% to 0.44%, the reduction in yield of 0.6% indicates total cost drag exceeding the platform charge differential, with transaction costs accounting for 0.13% of total costs [P13] and fund-level ongoing costs of 0.24% [P13]. The report does not explain why this cost structure justifies accepting a £2,000-£3,000 reduction in projected outcomes. Fourth, the pension switch report [P11] ranks the new solution 5th among comparable products by maturity value at mid-growth (£125,000), behind Zurich at 1st (£128,000) and the existing solution at 2nd (£127,000). This relative underperformance is not disclosed in the suitability report. The 'ongoing services' justification (risk review, market updates, advice access) is stated generically [P20] and cannot be quantified against the documented reduction in projected outcomes. The report acknowledges 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' but does not adequately explain why the client should accept demonstrably lower projected outcomes, higher cost drag, and worse ranking among alternatives. The guards permit weak justifications that repeat benefits without connecting them to costs; however, here the justification is not merely weak but contradicted by the quantified evidence of underperformance.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report discloses three material disadvantages: (1) Critical Yield Requirement—the recommended plan must grow by 4.8% per year to age 75 to match current plan value; (2) Adviser Charge Impact—the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one; (3) Lifestyling Drift—current funds automatically reduce risk approaching retirement, moving portfolio outside agreed risk range. The report also states two limitations: (1) Withdrawal Restrictions—transferring all pensions limits future flexible access; (2) Fund Range Constraint—Scottish Widows lacks a single multi-asset fund matching risk rating 5, requiring 20-30% in cash. Three product-specific risks are disclosed: access delays of 10-15 working days, market timing risk during transfer, and generic risks in product literature.

    File:     The pension switch report [P1] [P2] [P3] [P10] contains multiple alternative comparison tables showing critical yields and effects of switching across three growth scenarios. For the overall consolidation, the critical yield is stated as 6.3% [P1], not 4.8%. The 4.8% figure appears only in the Zurich pension only comparison [P10]. The report [P2] states the growth rate used for projection is 2.95% (mid growth). The report [P3] shows an 'Overall position' section addressing alternative comparisons. The Zurich policy [P8] discloses that life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans and medical questionnaires would be required for separate cover. Standard Life policy [P15] notes low charges may not be available from other providers upon transfer, and mentions a Differential Fund Discount. Aviva policy [P16] confirms no adviser charging is allowed on this plan type. Key Features [P20] explain flexible retirement options including drawdown. The research report [P12] discusses risk level mapping to asset classes. KIIDs [P18] detail material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, and derivatives risk. The pension switch report [P6] states the analysis excludes the effect of current advice charges, then separately addresses advice charges as initial (£3,305) and ongoing (1.00% annually). The comparison summary [P6] notes 'Values shown are in real terms which mean that they have been adjusted to take into account the effect of inflation.' No evidence found disclosing that benefits, features or protections are being given up by switching (other than potential guarantees and cover mentioned in [P8]). No evidence found stating that withdrawal flexibility is materially restricted; rather [P20] describes multiple flexible retirement options available on the new plan.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the critical yield requirement is 4.8% per year, but the pension switch report shows 6.3% as the critical yield for the overall recommended solution at mid growth rate [P1], while 4.8% appears only for the Zurich pension alone comparison [P10]

      - The report does not disclose that life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans and that medical questionnaires would be required [P8]

      - The report does not disclose that low charges currently available on Standard Life may not be available from other providers upon transfer [P15]

      - The report states the withdrawal restriction limits flexible access, but the Key Features [P20] describe multiple flexible options including drawdown, lump sum, annuity purchase, and phased withdrawal; no actual restriction on flexibility is evident in the new solution

      - The report states the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive but the comparison shows this relates to the combined effect of switching charges; the underlying Aviva platform charge structure (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total) is lower than the existing 0.50% on some arrangements, creating ambiguity about whether the statement accurately represents the nature of the charge difference

      - The report does not disclose risks specific to the funds recommended (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP) such as counterparty risk, credit risk, derivatives risk, and currency risk detailed in the KIIDs [P18]

      - The report does not state that projections are in real terms (inflation-adjusted) as opposed to nominal terms [P6]

    The report's assertion about critical yield of 4.8% appears to conflate the overall consolidation recommendation with the Zurich-only alternative comparison. The critical yield for the main recommendation is 6.3% at mid growth [P1], not 4.8%. This is a material misstatement affecting client understanding of the outperformance requirement. The report discloses the adviser charge impact (4.24% more expensive, £4,672 outperformance needed) but this figure relates to a specific comparison scenario and the underlying fund charges are actually lower on the new platform for some existing arrangements, creating potential confusion. The withdrawal restriction claim is contradicted by the Key Features document [P20], which explicitly describes flexible retirement options including drawdown, lump sum, and annuity choices—no restriction is evident on the recommended solution. The report omits disclosure of lost benefits: life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans and would require separate medical questionnaires [P8], a material feature given the client's age (69, per [P15]). The report does not disclose material product-specific risks from the KIIDs [P18] concerning counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, and currency risk applicable to the recommended funds. The report does not clarify that projections are in real (inflation-adjusted) terms [P6], which materially affects client understanding of expected outcomes. Standard Life's note about low charges potentially unavailable elsewhere upon transfer [P15] is not disclosed. The lifestyling drift concern is stated but not addressed in terms of the new solution's fund allocation or how risk is managed going forward. The generic statement delegating all other risks to product literature [P20] appears in the report but without specific cross-reference or emphasis that the client should read these documents. While the report meets the minimum threshold of disclosing some disadvantages and risks, material omissions concerning lost benefits, specific fund risks, critical yield misstatement, and false withdrawal restriction claim represent significant gaps in fair, clear and accurate disclosure.

    ** 2 QUOTE(S) NOT FOUND IN THE EVIDENCE SUPPLIED **

        "Critical yield – (2.95%): 4.8%"

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Report states Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin = 0.93% total charge; existing arrangements table [P16] shows '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin' but does not explicitly state 0.93% total—the arithmetic (£2.12 × 12 = £25.44 annual ÷ £103,439.24 = 0.025% ≈ 0.03%, plus 0.18% = 0.21%, not 0.93%). Report states Aviva existing risk level 2 lifestyling fund moving outside agreed risk range; policy documentation [P18] confirms lifestyling approach but does not label it as risk level 2; report's own extraction notes ambiguity about risk level classifications [P11] shows 'The client's agreed risk level is 5' for Zurich in switch report context. Report pension switch impact table quote '-0.49% -£506.85' attributed to Zurich comparison; Pension Switch Report [P17] for Zurich-only comparison shows under mid-growth 'Effect of switching: -£2,000 or 1.6% decrease' which at £103,439 fund value implies -1.9% effect, not -0.49%. 30 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations unverified

    Report:   The report asserts 14 separate product/investment comparisons across 5 existing arrangements being switched to a new Aviva Platform solution. Key comparisons include: (1) People's Pension: 0.50% fund + £4.50 annual admin vs 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management), assessed as Better; 7 funds vs 80+ funds, assessed as Better; Risk level 9 vs Risk level 5, assessed as Better; FAD/UFPLS equivalent to FAD/UFPLS/Flexi Access Drawdown. (2) Aviva existing: 0.50% fund charge vs 0.44%, assessed as Better with -0.06% -£2.25 impact; 80+ funds (lifestyling) vs 80+ funds assessed as Better; Risk level 2 (lifestyling auto-reducing outside agreed range) vs Risk level 5, assessed as Better; adviser servicing not allowed vs allowed, assessed as Better. (3) Zurich: 0.18% fund + £2.12/month (0.93% total) vs 0.44%, assessed as Better with -0.49% -£506.85 impact; 60+ funds vs 80+, assessed as Better; Risk level 7 vs Risk level 5, assessed as Better; adviser servicing not allowed vs allowed, assessed as Better.

    File:     Evidence shows: (1) People's Pension plan value £6,601.55 with 0.50% fund and £4.50 annual admin charge [P16]; retirement options FAD, Full and partial UFPLS [P16, P23]; 7 funds available [P16, P23]; no adviser servicing allowed [P8]. (2) Aviva existing £3,744.36 valued plan [P16, P18]; invested in Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2 fund with lifestyling/auto-switching investment approach [P18]; described as risk level 2 in report but policy document shows no explicit risk level; retirement options FAD, Full and partial UFPLS [P16, P23]; 80+ funds available [P23]. (3) Zurich existing £103,439.24 plan [P16]; Pension Switch Report [P3] identifies it as Zurich pension with charges shown in context of switching analysis; retirement options Full and partial UFPLS [P1, P16]; 60+ funds available [P16, P23]. (4) Recommended Aviva Platform solution: 0.44% total charge (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management) [P16]; 80+ funds available; funds allocated 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio + 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced [P12]; retirement options FAD, Full and partial UFPLS, Flexi Access Drawdown [P16, P7]. Pension Switch Report [P3, P9, P17, P20] provides alternative comparisons and consolidation analysis but shows projected maturity values for new solution at £125,000 (mid-growth) versus existing £127,000, indicating a 1.6% decrease or -£2,000 effect of switching under mid-growth scenario [P17].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin = 0.93% total charge; existing arrangements table [P16] shows '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin' but does not explicitly state 0.93% total—the arithmetic (£2.12 × 12 = £25.44 annual ÷ £103,439.24 = 0.025% ≈ 0.03%, plus 0.18% = 0.21%, not 0.93%)

      - Report states Aviva existing risk level 2 lifestyling fund moving outside agreed risk range; policy documentation [P18] confirms lifestyling approach but does not label it as risk level 2; report's own extraction notes ambiguity about risk level classifications [P11] shows 'The client's agreed risk level is 5' for Zurich in switch report context

      - Report pension switch impact table quote '-0.49% -£506.85' attributed to Zurich comparison; Pension Switch Report [P17] for Zurich-only comparison shows under mid-growth 'Effect of switching: -£2,000 or 1.6% decrease' which at £103,439 fund value implies -1.9% effect, not -0.49%

      - Report asserts retirement options for new solution include 'Flexi Access Drawdown' in addition to 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS'; FAD and Flexi Access Drawdown are synonymous terms, making this double-counting of the same benefit

      - Report lists comparison of existing Aviva (80+ funds available; lifestyling fund) vs recommended (80+ funds available); states 'Better' assessment but both have 80+ funds—fund range equivalence is not substantiated as 'Better'

      - Pension Switch Report [P3, P9] shows comparison table ranking alternatives; at mid-growth, Existing Solution projects £127,000 vs New Solution £125,000 (-1.6% or -£2,000), contradicting multiple 'Better' assessments for the recommended solution on charge basis alone

      - Report's charge impact for Zurich states -£506.85 annual saving; calculated at 0.44% on £103,439 = £455.33, not £506.85; discrepancy of £51.52 annually unresolved

      - Extraction metadata notes internal inconsistency in report: 'People's Pension fund value stated as £6,601.55 on page 5 table, but recommendation on page 6 states Retain... while advice is to consolidate' [transfer value in recommendations £110,185 vs sum of individual values £116,998.47, shortfall of £6,813.47 unexplained]

      - Report compares Aviva existing charge 0.50% vs recommended 0.44%, assessed Better with -0.06% -£2.25 impact; at Aviva's existing value £3,744.36, -0.06% = £2.25 annually—but this is savings only on that one plan, not representative of consolidation into £110,185 fund where savings would be materially different

    The report performs comparisons across multiple dimensions (Charges, Fund Range, Risk Alignment, Adviser Servicing, Retirement Options) for each existing arrangement against the recommended Aviva Platform solution. Corroborating evidence for most dimensions is present in provider documentation and switch analysis. However, several material discrepancies emerge: (1) Charge arithmetic: The report's calculation of 0.93% total for Zurich (0.18% + £2.12/month) cannot be verified; standard calculation yields ~0.21% fund charge. The quoted impact '-0.49% -£506.85' does not align with Pension Switch Report's own mid-growth scenario showing -1.6% effect. The £2.25 saving on Aviva is calculated only on the existing £3,744.36 fund, not the consolidated £110,185 into which it will be transferred, creating a misleading 'Better' assessment. (2) Fund Range: comparison of 80+ vs 80+ for Aviva lifestyling fund is stated as 'Better' with no substantive basis provided. (3) Risk Alignment: Aviva lifestyling fund described as 'Risk level 2' in report but policy document [P18] shows it as 'Lifestyling Investment Approach' with no explicit risk level stated; this may be inferred but is not corroborated by provider documentation. (4) Retirement Options: FAD and Flexi Access Drawdown are identical benefit, not additive; stating both for recommended solution inflates apparent benefit. (5) Critical Yield Contradiction: The Pension Switch Report [P17, P3] shows switching from existing (£127,000 mid-growth projection) to new solution (£125,000) produces a -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease in projected value, directly contradicting the uniformly 'Better' assessments in most comparison dimensions. The switch analysis indicates the recommended solution underperforms the existing portfolio on projected maturity value under the same growth assumptions—a material fact that contradicts the charge and feature 'Better' assessments unless substantial performance outperformance is expected. (6) Consolidation Opacity: The extraction notes explain that total existing values (£116,998.47) do not match the recommended transfer value (£110,185), with unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47. The recommendation to 'Retain' People's Pension while switching others adds further inconsistency to the consolidation narrative. The comparison lacks clarity on which arrangements are retained, which are consolidated, and what switching costs or entry penalties apply. (7) Safeguarded Benefits and Guarantees: The report's extraction metadata flags 'featuresOrGuaranteesLost' as absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument), yet all arrangements are marked as containing 'no safeguarded benefits' [P16]. This appears correctly handled rather than omitted, but the explicit analysis of any valuable features (e.g., lifestyling auto-reduction, existing adviser relationship, employer contribution continuation on People's Pension) is not substantively addressed. (8) Provider Documentation Currency: Pension Switch Report dated and uses illustration methodology; fund range and charges may reflect point-in-time data. Guard on data currency applies but does not resolve the contradictions in projected maturity values.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits of switching: lower costs (receiving scheme 0.06% cheaper annually, saving £2.25); lifestyling funds moving portfolio outside agreed risk range; greater investment choice suited to risk profile; adviser charging facilitation; ongoing monitoring; multi-asset funds providing properly risk-profiled solutions; enhanced retirement flexibility; consolidation for simplification; small pots rule withdrawal at £170 net from £200 fund with tax-efficient treatment; no switching charges. For most plans, annual charge difference is stated as -0.06% (cheaper). For Zurich plan specifically, the report states -0.49% annual charge difference. No initial advice fee is disclosed. Critical yield requirements are stated for comparisons.

    File:     The pension switch report [P15] shows detailed comparisons across three scenarios. For Aviva plan only [P3]: reduction in yield 0.59% existing vs 0.6% new, critical yield 6.0%, effect of switching £30 or 0.6% decrease (marginal worsening). For Standard Life only [P2]: reduction in yield 0.59% existing vs 0.6% new. For Zurich only [P3]: reduction in yield 0.29% existing vs 0.6% new, critical yield 0.3%, effect of switching £2,000 or 2.0% decrease. For all three combined [P6]: reduction in yield 0.3% existing vs 0.6% new, critical yield 3.2%, effect of switching £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. Death benefit shortfall identified: £3,305 immediately after switch [P7]. Zurich policy info [P9] confirms no fund switch charge (£0.00). No cost savings are demonstrated in the comparisons—all show new solution with higher reduction in yield (0.6%) versus existing (0.29-0.59%). Critical yields required range from 0.3% to 6.0% depending on plan.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims annual charge difference of -0.06% (cheaper) for most plans, but pension switch report shows new solution with 0.6% reduction in yield versus existing 0.3-0.59%, meaning new solution is MORE expensive not less expensive

      - Report states charge difference for most plans as -£2.25 saving, but ValueMatch found no corroboration of -0.06% or £2.25 in passages; nearest figure for -0.06% is 0 in [P7], 100% away; nearest for £2.25 is £2.32 in [P10], 3.1% away

      - Report claims 0.49% charge savings for Zurich specifically (-0.49%), but pension switch report [P3] shows new solution 0.6% RIY versus existing 0.29% RIY, indicating new solution is MORE expensive by 0.31 percentage points, not cheaper by 0.49%

      - Report asserts 'lower costs—receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure' but effect of switching tables show negative effects: £2,000 or 1.6-2.0% decrease in value at mid-growth rate, £10,400 or 9.6% decrease for one plan at low growth

      - Report claims small pots withdrawal would yield £170 net from £200 fund with 25% tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate, but ValueMatch found no corroboration of 25%, 75%, £200, or £170 figures in passages

      - Report omits disclosure of death benefit reduction: immediate shortfall of £3,305 on new solution versus existing £110,185.60 [P7]

      - Report does not disclose that at low growth rate (0.00%), one plan shows projected value £97,600 new versus £108,000 existing (£10,400 or 9.6% decrease) [P12]

      - Report does not disclose at high growth rate (5.88%), projected value £135,000 new versus £150,000 existing (£15,000 or 10.0% decrease) [P12]

      - Critical yield requirements not matched to specific plans in report; evidence shows critical yields ranging 0.3% to 6.0% but report does not tie these to individual pensions being switched

    The requirement G9.2 mandates that where benefits of switching are claimed, they must be supported by evidence and must outweigh any higher costs. The analysis reveals a material contradiction: the report asserts lower costs and cost savings as a key benefit of switching, but the evidence in the pension switch report contradicts this fundamentally. Across all three switching scenarios examined [P2, P3, P6], the new Aviva Platform solution consistently shows a higher reduction in yield (0.6%) compared to existing arrangements (0.29-0.59%), meaning the new solution bears higher ongoing charges net of investment growth. The effect of switching is uniformly negative: decreases of £2,000 to £15,000 depending on growth rate and plan. ValueMatch found no supporting figures for the -0.06% charge difference or £2.25 annual saving claimed in the report. The alleged 0.49% saving for Zurich contradicts evidence showing 0.6% new RIY versus 0.29% existing RIY. The small pots benefit (£170 net receipt) is unsubstantiated in the evidence pack; the 25%/75% tax split and £200 fund value find no corroboration. Critically, the report omits the £3,305 death benefit reduction identified in the switch report [P7]. While the report cites benefits including lifestyling correction, greater investment choice, and consolidation simplification, the cost analysis upon which the switch recommendation rests appears to be contradicted by the very evidence used to support it. The comparison has been performed and shows clear discrepancies. The claimed cost benefit is the inverse of what the evidence demonstrates. A switch justified on cost grounds is contradicted by the report's own cost comparison tables. No alternative to switching Zurich (the largest plan by value at £103,439) is evidenced; the switch report shows alternatives compared only at aggregate level. The vulnerability overlay is not engaged: no actual or potential vulnerability drivers affecting this assessment are evident in the passages.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts that employer contributions will be lost if the client transfers away from an existing arrangement. The report states: 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The report also confirms that all five existing arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits, citing: 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' (stated five times, once for each arrangement). The replacement analysis records that only the first of the five arrangements (People's Pension) has employer contributions being lost; the other four are marked false for employerContributionsLost.

    File:     The evidence documents from providers (Zurich, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Standard Life, and People's Pension) all confirm the absence of safeguarded benefits. P2 states via table that Guaranteed Minimum Pension, Guaranteed Minimum Fund, Reference Scheme Test, Waiver of premium, Life cover, Reinvestment of Charges, Fund Rebates, and Former Protected Rights are all marked 'No'. P4 (Standard Life) explicitly states: 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's), No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP), No Guaranteed Fund at retirement, No Reference Scheme Test' and 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' P3 shows across multiple plans (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) that Guaranteed annuity rates (GAR), Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP), and Guaranteed growth rates are all 'No'. However, the evidence contains no specific documentation confirming the presence or absence of employer contributions on the People's Pension arrangement, nor any provider confirmation regarding ongoing employer contributions on any of the five plans. P1 (Zurich Policy Info) discusses life cover and waiver benefits being lost: 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans.' This indicates loss of features, but is generic guidance rather than plan-specific analysis. P8 (People's Pension Policy Info) contains no mention of employer contributions, contribution structures, or workplace scheme status.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts employer contributions will be lost on transfer but provides no evidence from the People's Pension provider documentation confirming that employer contributions are currently being made to that plan.

      - The report identifies employer contributions as a lost feature only for the People's Pension (employerContributionsLost = true for arrangement 0, false for arrangements 1-4) but does not explain why the other four arrangements do not have employer contributions or whether they might be workplace schemes.

      - P1 identifies life cover and waiver benefits as features that are lost on transfer ('Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans') but the report does not mention these as features being given up, despite them being valuable features the existing plan may hold.

      - The report does not address what features might be lost on the Zurich arrangement, which shows on P1 that it may have life cover and waiver benefits that would be lost in transfer.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Provider documentation from People's Pension confirming whether employer contributions are currently being made to this arrangement., Current contribution status and employer contribution amounts for each of the five existing arrangements., Provider documentation for Zurich plan specifically confirming whether life cover and waiver benefits are attached to this member's plan (P1 indicates these may exist but the Zurich Policy Info provided does not contain plan-specific confirmation for this member)., Plan-specific analysis addressing what valuable features are attached to each existing arrangement and what would be lost on transfer., Evidence supporting the assertion that only the People's Pension has employer contributions while the other four do not.

    The comparison cannot be properly performed because critical information about employer contributions is absent from the case file. While the report correctly identifies and corroborates that safeguarded benefits (GARs, GMPs, guaranteed growth rates) are absent from all five arrangements—supported by P2, P3, P4 showing explicit 'No' entries and P4's statement 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan'—it fails to address a material lost feature. P1 explicitly states that 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans,' indicating these are features that exist on the existing Zurich plan that would be lost. The report does not mention these features as being given up in its analysis. The employer contributions assertion is unsupported: the report asserts employer contributions will be lost if the client transfers away, but no People's Pension provider documentation in the case file confirms that employer contributions are currently being made. P8 (People's Pension Policy Info) contains no information about contribution structure, employer participation, or ongoing contributions. The report marks only arrangement 0 (People's Pension) as having employerContributionsLost = true, but provides no evidence distinguishing it from the other four arrangements. This is a material omission because losing employer contributions is cited as a reason to retain the People's Pension (recommendation on page 6 states 'Retain your people's pension plan'), but the factual basis for this recommendation is not corroborated by the evidence provided.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts that three alternatives were considered: (1) Switch investment funds within People's Pension — rejected because there are no multi-asset funds available and funds do not meet risk profile of 5; (2) Leave pension invested and allow to grow — considered but rejected (inferred); (3) Switch investment funds within Zurich — rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, blended approach reduces diversification, performance marginally worse than Aviva, and main priority is consolidation with better control and drawdown options.

    File:     The evidence confirms that alternatives were considered across the case: People's Pension — the report states 'There are no Multi-Asset funds and only a limited range of other options' (P1) and 'the funds do not meet your risk profile' (P1). For Zurich, the report states 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so to achieve this profile, a blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund has been used' (P4) and 'the blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' (P4). The report also notes 'your main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options' (P4). For Scottish Widows, the report discusses retention versus encashment as options (P1). The pension switch report (P9) confirms consolidation recommendation across Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life plans.

    Discrepancies:

      - The canonical model lists three alternatives considered, but the evidence shows that for Scottish Widows (which is being encashed rather than switched to Aviva), the alternative consideration is only implicitly referenced as 'Retain and Encash' (P3 table) without explicit rejection reasoning documented in the way the other alternatives are analysed

      - Alternative analysis for Scottish Widows lacks plan-specific comparison detail — the report discusses switching to Aviva Platform but does not formally document why retention within Scottish Widows was rejected as an alternative to encashment

      - No alternative consideration is documented for Standard Life (which is switched to Aviva Platform) — the canonical model does not list a 'switch funds within Standard Life' alternative, yet the provider offers 300 funds (P3) which might have supported an in-situ switch before consolidation

      - The rejection reasoning for People's Pension alternative focuses on fund range and risk alignment but does not explicitly address whether funds within People's Pension could have been rebalanced to achieve risk 5, only that no multi-asset funds exist

    The comparison can be made. The report considers alternatives to switching at both the case level (consolidation objective) and plan-level (in-situ fund switches). For People's Pension and Zurich, the alternatives are well-documented with specific rejection reasons: People's Pension lacks multi-asset fund range and funds do not align with agreed risk rating 5 (P1); Zurich requires blended funds that perform marginally worse and consolidation is the priority (P4). The 'do nothing' alternative (leave pension invested) is inferred from the People's Pension analysis in P1 where retention is presented as an option but encashment is recommended. For Scottish Widows, the report explicitly presents both retention and encashment as advice options (P3 table shows 'Retain and Encash'), and P1 explains why encashment is preferable (small pot rules tax efficiency). However, the canonical model does not capture explicit rejection reasoning for Scottish Widows alternatives in the same structured way. For Standard Life, no in-situ alternative is documented in the canonical model, though the provider offers 300 funds. The pension switch report (P9) confirms that alternatives were analysed through 'Appendix' sections for individual plan comparisons ('Aviva plan only', 'Standard Life pension only', 'Zurich pension only' noted at P5). The rejection reasoning provided is consistent with the plan features: People's Pension offers 7 funds (P3), Scottish Widows offers 29 funds (P3), Zurich offers 60+ funds (P3), but all lack the specific risk-rated multi-asset fund range available through Aviva Platform at 80+ funds (P3). The consolidation priority (OBJ3) is cited as justification for not recommending in-situ switches within larger pots. The guard permits options assessed once case-wide to cover several plans, and here the consolidation objective legitimately explains why in-situ switches are rejected: the client's stated priority is 'consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options' (P4). No material plan-specific differences are overlooked in rejection reasoning across People's Pension and Zurich. The Scottish Widows alternative (retain vs. encash) is addressed but not with the same structural documentation as the switching alternatives.
