  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts no exit penalties (£0), no initial advice fees, and annual charge differences ranging from -0.06% to -0.49% depending on the plan. For the Zurich arrangement (the largest by value at £103,439.24), the report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' with an annual charge difference of -0.06% (£268.94 saving). For the Scottish Widows small pot (£211.32), the report recommends encashment under small pots rules, estimating net receipt of £170 after tax (25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate on a £200 withdrawal). The report identifies multiple benefits including lower costs, lifestyling misalignment, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring. Critical yields are stated as 1.9% (low growth), 4.8% (mid-growth), and 7.8% (high growth) for the main Zurich switch.

    File:     The pension switch report [P7] shows for Zurich pension at mid-growth (2.95%): existing pension reduction in yield 0.3%, new solution reduction in yield 2.2%, with projected effect of switching of £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in maturity value. Critical yield is 4.8%. At high growth (5.88%), the existing pension has 0.3% reduction in yield, new solution 2.3%, with effect of switching £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. [P1] shows total projected maturity value retaining all existing pensions versus switching some: with new solution (0.6%), the total is £127,000 versus retention strategy. [P7] shows at low growth (0.00%), switching would result in £10,400 or 9.6% decrease. The Scottish Widows small pot comparison is absent from the passages; no independent analysis of the small pots encashment vs. transfer alternative appears in the evidence.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims annual charge difference of -0.06% (£268.94 saving) for Zurich, but the passages do not contain this specific figure or the -0.06% reduction in yield claim. Passages show 0.3% existing vs. 2.2-2.3% new solution reduction in yield, not -0.06%.

      - The report states a critical yield of 4.8% for the main Zurich switch at mid-growth, which matches [P7], but the context in the passages shows this is the rate needed to match the existing pension's projected maturity value, not a justification threshold.

      - The report asserts projected maturity value effect of switching as savings, but [P7] shows switching results in £12,000 (9.4%) or £15,000 (10.0%) DECREASE in projected maturity value at mid and high growth rates, which contradicts the claim of net cost savings.

      - The report cites £170 net receipt from small pots encashment (25% tax-free, 75% taxed), but no passage contains this specific figure or validates the tax treatment calculation. The passages do not evidence this analysis.

      - The report's charge arithmetic shows £268.94 annual saving at -0.26% for Zurich (per canonical model computation), but no passage corroborates the -0.26% or -0.06% differential. Passages show existing 0.3% RIY and new solution 2.1-2.3% RIY, indicating higher costs in the new solution, not lower.

    A material inconsistency exists between the report's assertion that switching costs are justified by benefits and the evidence in the passages. The report claims cost savings from switching (lower annual charges, -0.06% differential for Zurich), yet the pension switch report [P7] shows that at realistic growth rates (2.95% mid-growth and 5.88% high-growth), the effect of switching is a DECREASE in projected maturity value of £12,000-£15,000 (9.4%-10.0%). This means the new solution's higher reduction in yield (2.2-2.3% vs. 0.3% existing) more than offsets any claimed charge benefits. The report cites 'Lower Costs' as a primary benefit, stating 'more of your savings remain invested for growth rather than being lost to charges,' but the evidence directly contradicts this: the new solution's RIY is 7-8 times higher than the existing arrangement's RIY, resulting in significantly lower projected values. The critical yield of 4.8% required to match the existing pension's maturity value represents an unusually high hurdle. The small pots recommendation lacks any supporting evidence in the passages; no calculation, tax analysis, or comparison of alternatives (transfer to another arrangement vs. encashment) is evidenced. The report's own figures (canonical model totals of existing arrangements £116,998.47 vs. transfer value £110,185 stated on page 9, a shortfall of £6,813.47) are not explained, raising concerns about undisclosed costs. The vulnerability overlay notes are not explicitly addressed in the passages reviewed, though the client's status as an HGV driver with variable income and 'medium' capacity for loss may materially affect whether a switch with worse projected outcomes and higher required yields is suitable.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that all five existing arrangements have no exit penalties. The canonical model states for each arrangement: 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'. The report is silent on safeguarded benefits—the extracted model shows /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent, which the extraction's own metadata confirms as 'NotPresentInDocument'.

    File:     P13 (SW Further Info 4.md) from Scottish Widows confirms of the SWMT Money4Life Plan: 'I can also confirm that there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' P12 (Test Case 1 - Policy Research 2025.md) documents five existing arrangements with fields for safeguarded benefits checked. P3 (SW MT Plan Schedule.md) outlines the Scottish Widows Master Trust with no mention of guarantees or special features. P14 (Fact Find) includes sections for 'Guaranteed Annuity Rate / Pension (or similar)', 'EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus', and 'Guaranteed Growth Rates'—all left blank. The pension switch report (P1, P2, P4, P5, P7, P9, P10) provides detailed analysis of charges and projected values but does not explicitly state whether safeguarded benefits exist or are being foregone. P11 (Standard Life Policy Value statement) and P8 (Standard Life Policy illustration) do not mention guaranteed benefits or bonuses.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for all five arrangements, but does not state whether safeguarded benefits, guarantees, bonuses, or other valuable features exist on any of those arrangements or would be lost upon transfer.

      - The extraction failed to capture any data on /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp despite this being material to CHK-009.

      - P13 explicitly states that the Scottish Widows Money4Life Plan 'do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission', which may be a material constraint on the transfer, not addressed in the report's analysis.

      - P12 includes a field 'Market Value Reduction (MVR)?' with all values marked 'No', but the report does not cite or discuss whether any arrangement has an MVR that could affect transfer value.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The suitability report does not state whether safeguarded benefits, guarantees, bonuses, enhanced tax-free cash, guaranteed annuity rates, or other valuable features exist on any of the five existing arrangements., The suitability report does not state whether any such benefits, if they exist, are being given up upon transfer., The report does not explicitly address whether the Scottish Widows Money4Life Plan's restriction on ongoing advisor charging constitutes a material feature comparison between existing and recommended arrangements., Provider documentation explicitly confirming the absence of safeguarded benefits on all arrangements is only partially available (P13 covers one arrangement; others lack such confirmation).

    The trigger requires evidence that lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client. The comparison cannot be performed because the report is silent on whether safeguarded benefits exist on the existing arrangements. P13 states that the Scottish Widows Master Trust arrangement has 'no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan', which supports the absence of safeguarded benefits for that one arrangement. However, no equivalent confirmation exists for the other four arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life arrangements shown in P2, P8, P11). The extraction metadata confirms /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent from the document—not extracted but genuinely not stated. The pension switch report (P1, P2, P4–P10) does not discuss safeguarded benefits, only charges and maturity values. P12's 'Market Value Reduction (MVR)?' field marked 'No' for all arrangements suggests no MVR penalty, but this is not cited or discussed in the report. The fact-find (P14) includes blank fields for guaranteed features, which is consistent with their absence but does not constitute the report's explicit assertion of that absence. Without the report stating whether safeguarded benefits exist or are being foregone, and without evidence covering all five arrangements, the comparison cannot be made.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report considers multiple alternatives for each pension arrangement: (1) For People's Pension: in-situ fund switch rejected due to no multi-asset funds available and funds not meeting risk rating 5; do-nothing alternative considered. (2) For Scottish Widows: encashment recommended with retention as an alternative considered. (3) For Aviva: in-situ fund switch rejected; consolidation into employer scheme rejected due to loss of employer contributions, higher costs (0.06% more expensive), and limited fund range. (4) For Zurich: in-situ fund switch rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, blended approach reduces diversification, funds underperformed Aviva options marginally, and consolidation objective is priority. (5) For Standard Life: in-situ fund switch rejected; lifestyling funds move outside agreed risk range. (6) Overall: Option 5 (new plan) recommended to consolidate existing pensions, gain better control, access drawdown options, and achieve lower costs and greater investment choice.

    File:     The Pension Switch Report provides detailed comparisons of existing arrangements against the new Aviva Platform solution. For overall consolidation: existing pensions project to £141,000 at age 75 with 0.6% reduction in yield; new solution projects £138,000 with 0.6% reduction in yield, representing £3,000 or 2.1% decrease in maturity value [P1]. Individual plan comparisons show: Aviva plan alone - existing £3,630 vs new £3,620 (£10 or 0.3% decrease) [P5]; Zurich alone - existing £102,000 vs new £100,000 at 0% growth (£2,000 or 2.0% decrease), and at mid-growth £127,000 existing vs £115,000 new (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease) [P3]; Standard Life alone - existing £2,910 vs new £2,900 at 0% growth (£10 or 0.3% decrease), and at mid-growth £3,130 existing vs £3,110 new (£20 or 0.6% decrease) [P8]. Performance analysis shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (recommended funds) cumulative performance at 37.25% over 3 years compared to Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation (existing) at 22.89% [P15]. The report states initial adviser charge of £3,305 and ongoing 1.00% annual charge [P14]. For Scottish Widows: £211.32 small pension available for encashment [P21]. Safeguarded benefits analysis states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P14]. Features and guarantees lost analysis absent from extraction per expectedButAbsent list. Alternative comparisons section notes Zurich reduction in yield 0.29% existing vs 0.6% new [P3].

    Discrepancies:

      - Projected maturity value contradicts cost savings claim: report states switch justified by lower costs and better outcomes, but P1 shows existing pensions £141,000 vs new solution £138,000, a £3,000 decrease (2.1% reduction), not an improvement

      - Charge comparison inconsistency: report states Aviva plan 'is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (in rejection rationale for Option 4 employer scheme), but P5 shows both existing Aviva and new solution at 0.6% reduction in yield, suggesting similar not higher charges

      - Zurich fund comparison: report claims blended funds 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally', but evidence of comparative performance between Zurich blended approach and Aviva funds is not provided in the passages; only overall portfolio performance shown in P15

      - Missing features/guarantees analysis: extraction metadata indicates '/replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost' absent from report; P14 states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' but this does not constitute evidence that no safeguarded benefits exist across all five plans

      - Scottish Widows option: report recommends encashment of £211.32 but does not address whether retention and continued growth would provide better outcomes given the very small amount

      - Consolidation rationale vs. outcomes: report prioritizes consolidation objective and control, but financial projections show consolidation results in lower maturity values at all growth rates examined, creating tension between non-financial objectives and financial suitability

      - Defaqto Diamond Ratings: P4 and associated fund details reference Diamond Ratings for recommended funds (CT Universal MAP and HSBC) but no comparative Diamond Rating analysis against existing funds is provided to support 'better outcomes' claim

    The requirement is to evidence that alternatives have been considered and that other alternatives do not provide better outcomes for the client. The report demonstrates consideration of multiple alternatives for each arrangement and for consolidation overall. However, the evidence creates material tension on financial outcomes. The report recommends switching five pension arrangements into a new consolidated Aviva Platform solution, justified by 'lower costs' and 'better outcomes'. The Pension Switch Report (category G, supporting research) directly contradicts the financial benefit claim: at mid-growth rates (2.95%), the existing pensions project £141,000 while the new solution projects £138,000 — a £3,000 (2.1%) decrease [P1]. Individual plan analysis shows losses across all scenarios: Zurich (the largest plan at £103,439) shows £12,000 or 9.4% projected loss at mid-growth [P3]. The report acknowledges this ('Effect of switching £3,000 or 2.1% decrease') on page 6 but frames it as acceptable given consolidation and control benefits. The qualitative justifications (consolidation, control, flexibility, drawdown options) are stated but the quantitative financial analysis undermines the 'better outcomes' premise for financial objectives. The report does not explain why projected maturity value reduction is consistent with 'better outcomes'. The alternative of consolidating into the existing Aviva workplace scheme (Option 4) is rejected partly on grounds it is '0.06% more expensive', but charge data in the switching report shows both the existing Aviva and new Aviva arrangements at 0.6% reduction in yield [P5], suggesting the cost comparison in the rejection rationale may be inaccurate. The Scottish Widows encashment recommendation does not compare retention with growth against encashment of the small £211.32 balance. The report addresses alternative product types (in-situ fund switch, do-nothing, consolidate-to-existing, new plan) but the evidence does not support that the recommended option provides better financial outcomes — only that consolidation achieves client objectives for control and flexibility. This is appropriateness on non-financial grounds, not evidence that alternative arrangements would produce worse outcomes. Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability characteristics identified in the evidence that materially affect this assessment of alternatives.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits of switching to justify the recommendation, including: (1) Lower costs - receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure; (2) Lifestyling - current funds automatically reduce risk, moving portfolio outside agreed risk range; (3) Greater investment choice - wider range of options for tailored strategy; (4) Enhanced retirement flexibility - more flexible retirement options; (5) Adviser charging facilitation - charges deducted directly for ongoing planning; (6) Ongoing monitoring and adjustments - active monitoring of investments; (7) Multi-asset fund selection - properly risk-profiled solutions rather than blended single-asset funds. For the Zurich plan specifically, the report states 'Despite higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year), the benefits of proper risk alignment, consolidation, and active management justify the switch.'

    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Charges comparison - Aviva existing 0.50% vs new 0.44%, a saving of 0.06% (£2.25 annually on one arrangement); Zurich existing 0.93% vs new 0.44%, a saving of 0.49% (£268.94 annually). The pension switch report [P3] shows rankings by maturity value at mid-growth rate: Zurich pension ranked 1st at £128,000 (0.2% RIY), Existing Solution 2nd at £127,000 (0.3% RIY), New Solution 5th at £125,000 (0.6% RIY). For Zurich-only switch [P4], projected value is £127,000 existing vs £115,000 new (9.4% decrease). (2) Fund choice - New solution offers CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio [P2]; Zurich offers a single 'fund' with N/A details [P12]; Scottish Widows fund offerings not detailed in evidence. (3) Lifestyling - Report mentions Zurich uses lifestyling; switch report shows Zurich fund as N/A details. (4) Retirement flexibility - Comparison table [P10] shows both existing and new solutions offer Flexi Access Drawdown, UFPLS, and annuity options. (5) Adviser charging - New solution enables adviser charges at 1% pa [P17] deducted from Aviva Platform. (6) Risk alignment - Client agreed risk rating is 5 [P12]; new solution provides CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced (both described as 'Balanced' funds without explicit risk level stated).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'lower costs' as a general benefit, but for the Zurich arrangement specifically, the switch results in higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year), contradicting the 'lower costs' justification in the main switching section [P15]; the report acknowledges this in one netBenefitConclusion but presents 'lower costs' as a switching benefit without qualification in others [P15,P19].

      - Report states new solution offers 'greater investment choice' but evidence shows new solution has 2 named funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) versus specific fund counts for existing arrangements not clearly stated; no comparative fund count is provided to evidence this claim.

      - Report asserts funds are 'lifestyling funds' in current Zurich arrangement that will drift outside risk range, but the switch report shows Zurich pension fund details as N/A [P12], providing no evidence that lifestyling is actually in place or will cause drift.

      - For retirement flexibility, the comparison table [P10] shows both existing arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) and new solution offer identical flexibility options (Flexi Access Drawdown, UFPLS available on existing arrangements; annuity availability varies). The new solution does not appear to provide enhanced flexibility compared to at least some existing arrangements.

      - Charges analysis shows a contradiction: report text states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' [P15], but the pension switch charges table shows Aviva -0.06% impact, meaning the new plan is 0.06% cheaper, not more expensive. For Zurich, the report acknowledges higher charges (£268.94 more per year, or 0.49%) but justifies this with benefits; however, the pension switch report [P3,P4] shows switching from Zurich results in a 9.4% decrease in maturity value at mid-growth rate (£127,000 to £115,000), directly contradicting the claimed net benefit.

      - Report identifies multi-asset fund selection as a benefit for the Zurich switch, stating Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds and a blend was used instead [P19]; however, the recommended new solution (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) are described only as 'Balanced' funds without explicit confirmation they meet risk level 5 or outperform the blended approach.

    The requirement tests whether higher costs are justified by additional benefits. The report does assert cost savings for most arrangements, but for the Zurich pension specifically, it acknowledges higher costs (£268.94 pa, 0.49% additional charge) and claims these are justified by benefits including consolidation, risk alignment, and active management. However, the evidence presents material contradictions: (1) The pension switch report ranked by maturity value at mid-growth shows the Zurich-only switch produces a 9.4% decrease in projected value (£127,000 to £115,000), which is not outweighed by the asserted benefits and contradicts the net benefit conclusion. (2) The 'lower costs' benefit is stated universally in the switching section but does not apply to Zurich (higher by 0.49%), and the report's own text contains an arithmetic error ('0.06% more expensive' when the table shows -0.06%, i.e., cheaper). (3) The 'enhanced retirement flexibility' claim is not supported: the comparison table shows existing arrangements already offer the same flexibility features as the new solution. (4) 'Greater investment choice' is claimed but no fund count comparison is provided; evidence shows 2 named funds in new solution vs. undefined counts in existing. (5) 'Lifestyling' is cited as a reason to switch from Zurich but the switch report shows Zurich fund details as N/A, providing no corroboration that lifestyling is actually present. (6) Multi-asset fund benefit is asserted for Zurich but the new funds are not confirmed to be multi-asset or to meet risk level 5 explicitly. The strongest evidence of mismatch is the 9.4% maturity reduction for the Zurich switch despite claimed benefits. This is a material inconsistency between the stated justification and the quantified outcome in the supporting switch analysis.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts five replacement rationales: (1) People's Pension retention because it is the current workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions that would be forfeited if transferred, with a recommendation to switch investments to Global Investments fund to better align with risk rating 5. (2) Scottish Widows encashment because it does not offer a single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5; combining two funds (one entirely cash) would leave 20-30% out of market, and the plan does not allow adviser management or limit withdrawal flexibility. (3) Aviva switch to better align portfolio with risk rating 5, move away from lifestyling fund that will drift outside risk range at retirement, lower costs, and enable ongoing adviser servicing. (4) Zurich switch for greater investment choice, multi-asset fund selection aligned with risk rating 5, enhanced retirement flexibility, lower annual charges (0.49% saving), and adviser servicing. (5) Zurich switch justified by moving away from lifestyling fund, providing fixed risk level 5 portfolio, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, ongoing adviser oversight, and states that despite higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year), the benefits justify the switch.

    File:     The file evidence shows: (1) People's Pension: Report states it is current workplace scheme with ongoing employer and employee contributions continuing to retirement. Report recommends retention but also recommends switching investments to Global Investments fund — this is presented as retaining the plan but altering its investments. (2) Scottish Widows: P10 and P14 confirm 'Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging.' P14 confirms no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached. Report claims plan does not offer single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5 and would require combining two funds with one entirely cash. P16 notes plan has '30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three' as alternative option, suggesting some fund combination exists. (3) Aviva existing: P8 shows 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2' with Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching). Charge shown as 0.50% in report. (4) Zurich existing: P12 shows Zurich illustration to age 70 (retirement date 7 June 2026), not age 75 as used in switching report. Charges shown as 0.93% fund charge. Report recommends switch citing lower charges (0.49% saving implies new charge 0.44%). P5 switching report shows Zurich existing at 0.2% yield ranking, not lower charges than recommended solution. (5) Charges contradiction: Report page 7 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but P22 shows Zurich existing annual charge 0.93% with Impact -0.06%, implying new charge 0.87%, not 0.44%. The switching report P20 recommends consolidation into 'Aviva Platform Personal Pension' as SIPP, yet report repeatedly refers to switching to Aviva. P5 and P6 show switching report comparing existing solution at 0.3% yield to new solution at 0.6% yield to age 75, a loss of £2,000 or 1.6% decrease.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts lower annual charges as benefit of Aviva switch but P22 Pension Switch Charges table shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs Zurich existing 0.93%, implying Zurich (not Aviva) is more expensive; the report states Zurich plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended, contradicting the claimed 0.49% saving

      - Report states 'lower costs' as benefit of switching but P5 switching report comparison shows new solution projected value £125,000 vs existing solution £127,000, a loss of £2,000 at medium growth to age 75

      - Report recommends Zurich switch to move away from lifestyling fund that will drift outside risk range, but does not reference that Aviva existing arrangement (P8) is also in Lifestage Investment Approach auto-switching fund

      - Report recommends Scottish Widows encashment citing plan does not offer single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5, but P16 file notes reference Scottish Widows alternative with '30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three' suggesting fund combination options exist

      - Report states People's Pension should be retained because employer contributions would be forfeited if transferred, but also recommends switching investments within the plan to Global Investments fund — rationale does not address whether this internal switch is subject to plan rules or costs

      - Report recommends consolidation and switch to Aviva Platform as single solution to all five plans, but P20 switching report only compares three existing pensions (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) to new solution; People's Pension and Scottish Widows comparison absent from switching report

      - Report page 6 states for Aviva 'I do not recommend that you consolidate into this plan as the funds do not meet your risk profile' yet page 6 also states recommendation is to retain People's Pension workplace scheme with ongoing contributions — two different consolidation strategies not reconciled

      - Report claims Zurich switch justified by charges benefit but switching report P5 ranks new solution 5th by maturity value (£125,000) below existing Zurich solution ranked 1st (£128,000) at medium growth

    The comparison can be performed. All five replacement actions have stated rationales. The critical discrepancies are: (1) Charges contradiction: The report claims lower costs justify switches to Aviva and away from Zurich, yet the switching report P5 shows Zurich ranked highest for maturity value (£128,000) and new solution ranked 5th (£125,000), indicating a loss not a gain. The report page 7 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' referring to Zurich, but this contradicts the claimed 0.49% saving and the switching report's own 1.6% maturity value decrease. (2) Scottish Widows encashment rationale: Report cites unavailability of multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5, but P16 file notes reference a fund combination (30% liquid, 70% Portfolio three), suggesting the plan does accommodate this need, albeit through combination. P14 confirms no ongoing adviser charging available, which is a genuine constraint not addressed by claiming funds unavailable. (3) Lifestyling contradiction: Report recommends Zurich switch to move away from lifestyling fund, but does not note that Aviva existing (being retained in recommendation for consolidation) is also in a Lifestage auto-switching approach per P8, creating inconsistency in rationale application. (4) People's Pension retention with investment switch: Report recommends retaining People's Pension specifically to preserve employer contributions, then recommends internal investment switch to Global Investments fund. No evidence in file that this internal switch is cost-free, rule-compliant, or addresses the risk alignment concern stated for other plans. (5) Consolidation strategy ambiguity: Report recommends consolidating five plans but switching report only compares three to new solution. People's Pension and Scottish Widows switching analysis absent from switching report, creating a gap between recommendation scope and evidence scope. The switching report itself (P5, P6) shows existing solution outperforming new solution by £2,000 (1.6%), contradicting the claimed benefit. The charge analysis contains internal contradictions: report states 0.06% more expensive but also 0.49% saving; P22 shows -0.06% impact on Zurich meaning new charge 0.87%, not the 0.44% shown for Aviva Platform. The narrative guard applies: the rationale rests on charges benefit but the charges schedule contradicts this. No evidence supports the specific claim that Scottish Widows cannot be structured to meet risk profile needs — only that combining funds is required and ongoing adviser charging is unavailable (a genuine constraint but distinct from unavailability of suitable funds).

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that the client would 'miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions' if transferring away from the People's Pension plan. The report identifies employer contributions as a feature that would be lost. The report states all five arrangements have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'.

    File:     Evidence confirms the People's Pension plan receives ongoing employer contributions. Policy documentation [P1-P8] shows regular monthly employer contributions alongside employee contributions over an extended period from 2021-2023. The Zurich Managed Pension arrangement documentation [P11-P12] confirms 'Currently, no payments are being made to this plan' and shows exit charges apply: the charge reduces by 0.3%-0.4% per month until Selected Retirement Age where no charge applies, capped at 1% from age 55. Standard Life documentation [P16] states 'No bid offer spread' and '100% allocation'. Scottish Widows documentation [P15] provides charge illustrations. The policy research document [P20] notes the advice strategy is to 'consolidate where necessary' and that 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand.' Pension switch analysis [P22] shows projected value reductions of 9.4%-10.0% at age 75 depending on growth rate, with critical yields of 1.9%-7.8% required to match existing pension performance.

    Discrepancies:

      -  The report states all five arrangements have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching', but evidence shows Zurich plan [P11] has exit charges that reduce by 0.3%-0.4% per month until SRA (capped at 1% from age 55), contradicting the 'no charges' assertion for that arrangement.

      - The report identifies employer contributions as lost only from the People's Pension plan, but does not analyse or compare whether any of the other four arrangements being transferred (Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) also receive or have previously received employer contributions that might be lost.

      - The pension switch analysis [P22] shows the new solution would produce a 9.4%-10.0% decrease in projected value at age 75 across growth rate scenarios, contradicting any implicit claim that the recommendation produces equivalent or superior outcomes to the existing arrangements.

      - The report does not disclose the exit charge structure for the Zurich arrangement despite its materiality (potentially up to 1% of fund value).

      -  The recommendation to consolidate multiple pensions is stated as the advice strategy [P20] but the specific plan-by-plan comparison of employer contributions, exit penalties, and switching costs is absent from the report's replacement analysis.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Plan-specific analysis of whether Scottish Widows, Aviva, Standard Life, or other ceding plans receive or have received employer contributions that would be lost on transfer., Quantification of the exit charge amount for the Zurich arrangement at the date of recommendation., Explicit acknowledgment and analysis of the critical yield requirement (1.9%-7.8% depending on growth scenario) shown in the switch analysis, indicating funds must grow substantially faster in the new arrangement to recover losses., Details of the client's active member status with any scheme offering ongoing employer contributions, to establish whether contributions would genuinely be lost or would cease naturally on departure., Alternative options considered for each arrangement, particularly whether retaining any existing arrangement or accepting lower consolidation was analysed.

    The report asserts employer contributions will be lost from the People's Pension plan but corroborating evidence confirms this is factually accurate—the policy information [P1-P8] demonstrates ongoing employer contributions. However, the requirement is whether material replacement-related information is omitted. The report fails to perform a complete plan-by-plan replacement analysis. Critically: (1) Four of the five arrangements being transferred are never analysed for whether they also carry employer contributions or other safeguarded benefits that would be lost; (2) The Zurich exit charge structure [P11] applies specifically to capital units and reduces by 0.3%-0.4% per month until SRA—this is material and contradicts the report's blanket statement of 'no charges for transfer and/or switching' for all five arrangements; (3) The pension switch analysis [P22] contemporaneously prepared shows projected value decreases of 9.4%-10.0% and critical yields of 1.9%-7.8%, yet the report does not disclose or reconcile these findings or explain why such substantial underperformance in the new solution is justified; (4) No analysis addresses whether the client remains an active member with ongoing employer contributions, which would determine whether the benefit is genuinely lost or merely ceases at departure; (5) The policy research strategy [P20] identifies consolidation as the objective but no evidence shows plan-specific comparison of costs, benefits, guarantees, or alternatives for each of the five ceding arrangements. The omissions are material because they prevent the client from understanding what safeguarded benefits (employer contributions, exit charge holidays, protected rights) attach to each existing arrangement and what the actual switching costs and performance impact are. A guard applies: an employer contribution that stops on leaving service is not necessarily lost—but the file provides no evidence of the client's active member status to determine this. The absence of this evidence means the comparison cannot be properly performed.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  No trigger was recorded; this check applies to every case and was assessed anyway. Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The report records no third-party support and no support needs, while the case file contains questionnaire evidence of no understanding of stocks, shares, pensions and investment schemes, and no prior investment experience with complex products. This represents either a failure to record identified vulnerability drivers or a failure to assess them. The Fact Find shows 'No Preference' for delivery method and no other accessibility or communication adjustments are documented anywhere in the file. No vulnerability assessment notes, support needs documentation, or communication requirements are recorded in P5 (Risk Profile) or P21 (Fact Find) sections, both of which have fields for such notes but record 'No additional notes were recorded' or leave them blank.

 

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that third-party support is not present (isPresent: false). No support needs, communication adjustments, or third-party involvement are documented in the extracted canonical model.

    File:     The case file contains evidence of significant capability and knowledge gaps relevant to FG21/1. P1 (Risk profile questionnaire) records the client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to questions about stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning. The same passage shows 'No' to experience with Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, and pension products in the last 5 years. P5 records 'No additional notes were recorded' under both Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss sections. P7 shows the client selected 'No Preference' for preferred delivery method. P21 (Fact Find) records a Video Call meeting on 18/09/2025 with 'Anybody else present at the Meeting? No'. P2 and P17 reference Power of Attorney as a mechanism the firm accepts for third-party authority but do not indicate one is in place for this client. P1 also shows the client checks their portfolio 'Yes, by myself' and invests in savings 'Yes, by myself', indicating solo decision-making. No evidence of vulnerability assessment, support needs, communication adjustments, or third-party support is documented in any passage.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report records no third-party support and no support needs, while the case file contains questionnaire evidence of no understanding of stocks, shares, pensions and investment schemes, and no prior investment experience with complex products. This represents either a failure to record identified vulnerability drivers or a failure to assess them.

      - The Fact Find shows 'No Preference' for delivery method and no other accessibility or communication adjustments are documented anywhere in the file.

      - No vulnerability assessment notes, support needs documentation, or communication requirements are recorded in P5 (Risk Profile) or P21 (Fact Find) sections, both of which have fields for such notes but record 'No additional notes were recorded' or leave them blank.

    The canonical model correctly reflects what the suitability report asserts: third-party support is marked absent with no other vulnerability, support needs, or communication details captured. However, the comparison reveals a material gap. FG21/1 drivers exist in the case file: the client has no understanding of stocks, shares, pensions and investment schemes (per P1 questionnaire responses), and no prior experience investing in these product types. These are low capability indicators under FCA guidance. The report's recommendation places £110,185 into a balanced portfolio including investment funds with allocations to HSBC Global Strategy and CT Universal MAP (per P11 and P4). A client with 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks, shares and pension planning risks, combined with no prior investment experience in these areas, would typically trigger support needs assessment, communication planning, and verification of comprehension. The file records the client attended a video call meeting alone (P21), selected 'No Preference' for delivery method (P7), and the Risk Profile document records no additional discussion notes on investor experience or capacity for loss (P5). The guards specify that a spouse present is not automatically third-party support (not applicable here, as the client was alone) and that a recommendation to establish power of attorney is future planning, not current support (no such recommendation is evident in the file). The absence of any support needs, communication adjustments, or vulnerability assessment documentation in a case presenting clear low-capability drivers under FG21/1 is the substantive finding: the report is silent on how the client's demonstrated knowledge gaps and inexperience were addressed to ensure informed decision-making on this complex recommendation.

  [G10.2] Communication preferences and formats

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report contains no assertion regarding the client's communication preferences, support needs, or accessible formats. The canonical model path `/vulnerability/perClient[]/communicationPreferences` is absent.

    File:     The case file contains multiple references to communication and contact preferences. [P9] records 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference' in the Fact Find. [P1] and [P3] establish the Client Agreement framework allowing instructions via post, phone, or email, and describe third-party authorization via Power of Attorney. [P24] addresses preferred communication methods, stating 'Where We are unable to contact You through Your preferred method of communication, We may try to communicate with You by another means of contact, depending on the information You or Your Adviser have given Us.' [P3] explicitly mentions that clients 'may also allow a third party to provide instructions on your behalf by providing a valid Power of Attorney or providing other confirmation as requested by us.' [P9] also notes that 'any member of The Financial Advice Service Ltd may contact you by post, phone or e-mail, or in any other way a member feels is appropriate.'

    Discrepancies:

      - The Fact Find (P9) records communication preference as 'No Preference,' but the suitability report does not reference, confirm, or account for this recorded preference.

      - The Client Agreement framework (P1, P3, P24) establishes multiple communication channels and the ability to designate third-party representatives via Power of Attorney, but the suitability report does not mention these contact arrangements or confirm how the client will be contacted or whether a Power of Attorney or trusted contact exists.

      - [P18] mentions prudence of setting up and registering Power of Attorney for financial affairs/health and welfare, but the report does not indicate whether the client already holds such authority or has designated representatives.

    A guard applies: 'A standard contact preference is not a vulnerability adjustment; record it, but do not treat its presence as evidence that vulnerability was assessed.' The Fact Find entry 'No Preference' is a standard administrative recording, not a vulnerability assessment outcome. The absence of communication preferences from the suitability report itself is not itself a finding, provided the case involved standard advisory interaction. However, the report does not even acknowledge or reference the recorded preference. More significantly, the file establishes frameworks for third-party involvement and alternative communication methods (P1, P3, P24), and P18 identifies Power of Attorney as prudent, yet the report does not confirm whether such arrangements exist, are needed, or have been put in place. This creates a gap: the report recommends a substantial pension consolidation and switching strategy for a client described as an HGV driver (P3 notes '£300 per week as an HGV driver'), yet does not reference how communications will be managed, whether the client has capacity-of-attorney arrangements, or whether any trusted contact or third party should be involved in the advice process or ongoing oversight. The report's silence on these documented or framework elements is material because they relate to how the client will be serviced going forward and whether vulnerability or support needs have been considered.

  [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report contains no explicit statement of adjustments made to support client comprehension. The extracted model holds no values at /vulnerability/perClient[]/adjustmentsMade[] or /vulnerability/perClient[]/characteristics[]. The report does reference communication methods and ongoing review, but does not assert specific comprehension adjustments.

    File:     The file contains multiple references to communication, support and information provision: (1) Fact Find records 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference' [P2]; (2) Client Agreement acknowledges consent to processing sensitive personal data and confirms client received Client Agreement and Business Card [P2]; (3) Report states 'If there are any points on which you are unsure, or require further clarification, please contact me and I will be pleased to explain these in greater detail' [P1]; (4) Report invites client to contact adviser 'at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required' and offers 'Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs' [P4]; (5) Report emphasizes importance of reviewing product literature and understanding risk: 'It is important to continually review your risk profile moving forward to ensure that this level of risk remains in line with your personal attitudes' [P5]; (6) Report confirms client understanding: 'You are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' and 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' [P5]; (7) Risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' under sections for Investor Experience and Capacity for loss questionnaire discussion [P23]; (8) File notes show no recorded vulnerability assessment, support needs documentation, or accessibility requirements.

    Discrepancies:

      - No formal vulnerability assessment or vulnerability indicator checklist appears in the file despite FCA FG21/1 requirements to assess vulnerability drivers (health, life event, capacity, resilience).

      - No explicit accessibility adjustments are documented: no mention of large print, plain language, communication aids, extended time, third-party support, or other accommodations despite the client's status as a self-employed HGV driver with household cashflow showing deficit (£1,718 expenditure vs £1,430 income) [P8].

      - Risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss discussion sections [P23], indicating no depth of exploration of comprehension barriers recorded.

      - Communication preference shows 'No Preference' [P2] yet report offers multiple contact channels without documenting whether client's actual communication needs or literacy considerations were explored.

      - No record of whether the complex pension consolidation advice (5 schemes, multiple fund switches, critical yield calculations) was delivered in format suitable to client's demonstrated capacity, particularly given limited investment experience noted in report.

    The comparison can be performed because the extracted model correctly reflects that the report is silent on adjustments made to support comprehension. The file does show general communication provisions (contact availability, marketing consent, Client Agreement provision) but these are standard regulatory requirements, not client-specific adjustments. The three material gaps are: (1) No vulnerability assessment framework applied, despite clear FG21/1 drivers present (self-employed with volatile income, household cashflow deficit, limited investment experience, no record of health or life-event assessment); (2) No accessibility or communication adjustments documented for this specific client beyond generic 'contact us if unclear'; (3) Risk profile report explicitly records 'No additional notes' for Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss discussion, suggesting discussions were minimal or standardized template was used without depth. The report does confirm client understood basic concepts (values rise/fall, could have detrimental impact on day-to-day living - though client then denied impact [P5]) but does not assert that comprehension of the specific recommendation was actively supported by tailored measures. The guard applies: standard cancellation rights and general information provision are regulatory features, not adjustments for this client. A finding of Potential Concern stands because FG21/1 drivers are present (limited experience, household deficit, self-employment income variability, age 69), a vulnerability assessment should have been performed and recorded, and any adjustments arising from it should be visible in the report's narrative. Their absence means the report's account is incomplete, even though the client may have understood adequately in practice.

You are in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net, and receive an additional £20 per week from your role with the Sea Cadets."

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report identifies six foreseeable harms: (1) Market volatility with mitigation through multi-asset funds and ongoing monitoring; (2) Underperformance of new funds with mitigation through performance analysis and critical yield assessment showing 2.94% outperformance; (3) Pension switching costs with mitigation through no exit penalties and charge comparison; (4) Illiquidity and administration delays with mitigation through £6,000 liquid reserves and 10-15 day access timeframe; (5) Loss of employer contributions if People's Pension is left, mitigated by retaining that scheme; (6) Inheritance Tax impact from April 2027 with mitigation through IHT analysis and monitoring. The report does not extract a formal `/vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[]` field, meaning the specific link between identified vulnerabilities and recommendation suitability is not formally documented in the canonical model.

    File:     The file contains multiple vulnerability indicators: (1) Risk profile shows client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, pension planning, and investment risks [P1]; (2) Client responded 'No' to understanding EIS/VCT [P1]; (3) Client has no prior investment experience in Unit Trusts, stocks/shares, or EIS/VCT in last 5 years [P1]; (4) Risk questionnaire showed natural risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) but was upgraded to 5 (Balanced) by adviser assessment [P10, P14]; (5) Capacity for loss justification relies on income comfortably meeting expenditure with savings and pension [P14]; (6) Client's actual monthly cash flow shows deficit of £288 per month (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718) [P12]; (7) Client is 69 years old with 2-year retirement horizon [P16]; (8) Client expressed need to 'consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' [P6], indicating comprehension concerns; (9) Fact Find notes 'limited or moderate experience of investments' [P14]; (10) No Power of Attorney or enhanced support arrangements are documented as in place, though report recommends establishing them [P20].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts capacity for loss based on comfortable income meeting expenditure, but Fact Find shows actual monthly deficit of £288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718) [P12], contradicting the sufficiency claim.

      - Report recommends risk rating 5 (Balanced) but client's natural risk level per questionnaire was 4 (Cautious Balanced) [P10, P14], and client has zero understanding of stocks/shares and pension risks [P1], creating a mismatch between capability and assigned risk profile.

      - Report identifies client needs help getting head around pensions (consolidation goal) [P6], indicating comprehension challenges, but treats client as capable of Balanced risk-5 portfolio without addressing this cognitive gap.

      - Report states client 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living' [P14], but Fact Find shows household already spending £288 more per month than earning, so any underperformance directly impacts livelihood.

      - Report asserts £6,000 liquid cash reserve exists for emergency access [P14], but this is also the total household liquid assets in the financial plan [P21], leaving zero buffer after one emergency withdrawal.

      - Report recommends Power of Attorney be 'set up and registered' [P20] but provides no evidence this has been done or that it is incorporated into the suitability assessment or ongoing support arrangements.

    This case presents a material mismatch between assessed vulnerability indicators and the suitability of the recommendation. The client is a 69-year-old with no investment experience, explicitly stating 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks, shares and pension planning [P1]. The natural risk assessment questionnaire placed him at level 4 (Cautious Balanced) [P10], yet the adviser upgraded this to level 5 (Balanced) based on discussion [P14]. The stated rationale was that the client wished to increase the rating and is 'not reliant upon pension funds', seeking 'best growth potential'. However, this sits uncomfortably against multiple vulnerability drivers: (1) Low financial capability—client explicitly lacks understanding of core investment concepts [P1]; (2) Low resilience—the household is already in monthly cash deficit of £288 [P12], meaning any market volatility directly threatens living expenses, contradicting the report's assertion that underperformance 'would not have a detrimental impact'; (3) Cognitive support need—client identified need to consolidate to 'get his head around' holdings [P6], indicating comprehension challenge; (4) Age and near-retirement—69 with 2-year retirement horizon means limited recovery time for market downturns. The report acknowledges six foreseeable harms and proposes mitigations, which is appropriate. However, the fundamental issue is that the risk-5 recommendation itself may not be suitable for a client with zero investment knowledge and negative cash flow. The report treats vulnerability indicators as matters to mitigate around rather than as material constraints on the appropriateness of the recommendation itself. The gap between natural risk level (4) and agreed level (5) is never justified by reference to the client's actual capability or resilience. The Power of Attorney recommendation [P20] is mentioned as prudent but not executed or integrated into the suitability logic. The £6,000 liquid buffer, while identified as a mitigation for access delays, comprises the entire household liquid assets, making it inadequate as a genuine emergency reserve. Under FG21/1, the adviser must assess whether the client has the knowledge, experience and financial situation to properly understand the risks of the investment. The evidence shows: knowledge is absent, experience is absent, and financial situation (monthly deficit) is weak. The upgrade from risk 4 to risk 5 appears to have been driven by the client's expressed goal (growth over 10 years) rather than by demonstrated capability to sustain that risk. This is a classic FG21/1 vulnerability overlay issue: material characteristics exist that are not fully reflected in the appropriateness of the core recommendation.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts `/vulnerability/perClient[]/hasVulnerability` = "No". No vulnerability categories, characteristics, or assessment dates are extracted from the report. The report contains no section explicitly documenting vulnerability assessment.

    File:     The file contains substantial evidence across multiple categories relevant to vulnerability assessment: (C) File notes record the client's risk profile assessment, capacity for loss, and communication of risk understanding. (B) Fact Find shows client age 70, employment as HGV driver, current net income £300/week typical, total monthly disposable income of -£288 (expenditure exceeds income), and £6,000 cash savings. (D) Risk profile report shows investor experience section and capacity for loss questionnaire with "No additional notes recorded" in both. The file does not contain any explicit vulnerability assessment or recorded vulnerabilities.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts no vulnerability is present, but does not document any vulnerability assessment process. The absence of a vulnerability assessment record is distinct from a conclusion that no vulnerability exists.

      - The client is age 70, which is within FG21/1 considerations for older clients, yet no age-related vulnerability assessment is evident in either the report or file notes.

      - The client shows negative monthly disposable income (£-288), which is a material financial stress indicator that could indicate low financial resilience, yet this is not addressed as a vulnerability consideration in the report.

      - The client's employment income is described as 'typically earning around £300 per week net' (P1), indicating variability and income uncertainty, which could affect capacity for loss, but no vulnerability assessment addresses this.

    The comparison can be made: the report explicitly states hasVulnerability = "No", and the file evidence is available. However, the finding is not straightforward. The report does not merely conclude that vulnerabilities were assessed and none found; rather, the report contains no explicit vulnerability assessment narrative. The extracted model shows absence of vulnerability categories and characteristics. Per the extraction metadata, the path `/vulnerability/perClient[]/categories[]` is 'NotPresentInDocument' - meaning the report is silent on this point, not that extraction failed. The file notes (P1) document the client's risk profile, capacity for loss, and understanding of risk, but do not contain a formal vulnerability assessment. The Fact Find (P3, P4) captures demographic, financial, and lifestyle information but no vulnerability screening. The Risk Profile Report (P13) documents that 'No additional notes were recorded' in both Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss sections. Material financial stress exists objectively: monthly expenditure of £1,718 against net income of £1,430 creates a £288 monthly deficit. The client is in active employment at age 70 as an HGV driver, suggesting potential physical and occupational stress factors. However, the file notes (P1) show the adviser explicitly discussed capacity for loss with the client and recorded that 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' This statement, combined with the client's state pension (£230/week), employment income (£300/week), and spouse's employment income (£1,100/month via Kim), suggests the adviser's reasoning for concluding no material vulnerability. The client is classified as a retail client (P17) with no recorded accessibility needs (P10: 'No Requirement'). Neither the report nor the file contains any documented vulnerability indicators, assessments, or outcomes. The absence of both does not itself constitute a discrepancy; it becomes a concern only if the case presented clear FG21/1 drivers that warranted assessment. The primary driver present is age (70), but age alone is not a vulnerability per the comparison guard. Secondary drivers (negative monthly cash flow, variable income, occupational stress) exist in latent form but were not explicitly surfaced as vulnerabilities in either document.
